Deep-water and shallow-water limits of statistical equilibria for the intermediate long wave equation
Andreia Chapouto, Guopeng Li, and Tadahiro Oh

Abstract
. We study the construction of invariant measures associated with higher order conservation laws of the intermediate long wave equation (ILW) and their convergence properties in the deep-water and shallow-water limits. By exploiting its complete integrability, we first carry out detailed analysis on the construction of appropriate conservation laws of ILW at the H k 2 H^{\frac{k}{2}} -level for each k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N} , and establish their convergence to those of the Benjamin-Ono equation (BO) in the deep-water limit and to those of the Korteweg-de Vries equation (KdV) in the shallow-water limit. In particular, in the shallow-water limit, we prove a rather striking 2-to-1 collapse of the conservation laws of ILW to those of KdV. Such a 2-to-1 collapse is novel in the literature and, to our knowledge, this is the first construction of a complete family of shallow-water conservation laws with non-trivial shallow-water limits. We then construct an infinite sequence of generalized Gibbs measures for ILW associated with these conservation laws and prove their convergence to the corresponding (invariant) generalized Gibbs measures for BO and KdV in the respective limits. Finally, for k ≥ 3 k\geq 3 , we establish invariance of these measures under ILW dynamics, and also convergence in the respective limits of the ILW dynamics at each equilibrium state to the corresponding invariant dynamics for BO and KdV constructed by Deng, Tzvetkov, and Visciglia (2010-2015) and Zhidkov (1996), respectively. In particular, in the shallow-water limit, we establish a 2-to-1 collapse at the level of the generalized Gibbs measures as well as the invariant ILW dynamics. As a byproduct of our analysis, we also prove invariance of the generalized Gibbs measure associated with the H 2 H^{2} -conservation law of KdV, which seems to be missing in the literature.

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Intermediate long wave equation

We consider the intermediate long wave equation (ILW) on the one-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/(2\pi\mathbb{Z})
:

(1.1)

\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}u=\partial_{x}(u^{2}),\qquad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}.

This equation was introduced in
[
48
,
57
]

as a model describing the propagation of an internal wave at the interface of a stratified fluid of
finite depth
\delta>0
,
and the unknown

u
denotes the amplitude of the internal wave at the interface.
See also Remark 1.1 in
[
64
]
.
Note that the derivation in
[
48
,
57
]
is formal;
see
[
9
,
25
]
for the rigorous derivation of (
1.1
).
The operator
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
characterizes the phase speed and it is defined
as the following Fourier multiplier operator:

(1.2)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{\delta}f}(n)=\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{\delta}}(n)\widehat{f}(n):=-i\bigg(\coth(\delta n)-\frac{1}{\delta n}\bigg)\widehat{f}(n),\quad n\in\mathbb{Z},

with the convention that
\coth(\delta n)-\frac{1}{\delta n}=0
for
n=0
.

The ILW equation (
1.1
)
is an important physical model with further applications
in the study of atmospheric sciences, oceanography, and quantum physics

[
22
,
96
,
55
,
104
,
7
]
.
Furthermore,
it serves as an “intermediate” equation of finite depth
0<\delta<\infty
,
providing a natural connection
between the Benjamin-Ono equation (BO), modeling fluid of infinite depth (
\delta=\infty
),
and the Korteweg-de Vries equation (KdV), modeling shallow water (
\delta=0
).
From the analytical point of view,
(
1.1
) is also of great interest due to its rich structure;
it is a dispersive equation, admitting soliton solutions.
Moreover, it is completely integrable with an infinite number of conservation laws
[
53
,
54
,
101
]
.
See
[
103
,
52
]
for an overview of the subject and the references therein.

Our main
goal in this paper is to advance our understanding
of convergence issues for ILW
from both the deterministic and statistical viewpoints.
In the deep-water limit (
\delta\to\infty
),
an elementary computation shows that
the operator
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
in (
1.2
) converges to the Hilbert transform
\mathcal{H}

with symbol
1
1

1

In this paper, we work with mean-zero functions.
In order to avoid ambiguity when
\mathcal{H}
acts on a product
(which can be checked to have mean zero in our application),
we define
\mathcal{H}
to be the Fourier multiplier operator
with multiplier

-i\textup{sgn}(n)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n\neq 0}
.
Namely, we have
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}
,
where
\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}
denotes the projection onto non-zero frequencies.

-i\textup{sgn}(n)
(see Lemma
2.2
)
and thus
ILW (
1.1
) formally converges to BO:

(1.3)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}^{2}u=\partial_{x}(u^{2}).

Next, we consider
the shallow-water limit (
\delta\to 0
).
Recall that ILW (
1.1
) describes the motion
of the fluid interface in a stratified fluid of finite depth
\delta>0
,
where
u
denotes the amplitude of the internal wave at the interface.
As
\delta\to 0
, the entire fluid depth tends to
0

and, in particular,
the amplitude
u
of the internal wave at the interface is
O(\delta)
, which also tends to
0
.
Hence, in order to observe any meaningful limiting behavior,
we need to magnify the fluid amplitude by a factor
\sim\frac{1}{\delta}
.
This motivates us to consider the following scaling
[
1
]
:

(1.4)

v(t,x)=\tfrac{1}{\delta}u(\tfrac{1}{\delta}t,x),

which leads to the following scaled ILW:

(1.5)

\partial_{t}v-\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}v=\partial_{x}(v^{2}),\quad\text{where }\ \widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}:=\frac{1}{\delta}\mathcal{G}_{\delta}.

By studying the limiting behavior
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\partial_{x}
as
\delta\to 0
(see Lemma
2.3
), we see that
the scaled ILW (
1.5
) formally converges to
the following KdV equation:

(1.6)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v+\frac{1}{3}\partial_{x}^{3}v=\partial_{x}(v^{2}).

In the deep-water limit,
the Fourier multiplier of
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}
converges
to that of
\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}
, uniformly in the frequency
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
On the other hand,
such uniform convergence does not hold
in the shallow-water limit;
see Lemmas
2.2
and

2.3
.
In this sense,
the shallow-water limit
is
singular
(especially when compared to the deep-water limit).
As we see below, this singular nature of the shallow-water limit
also appears in
convergence of (suitably constructed) conservation laws of the scaled ILW
to those of KdV
(exhibiting regularity jumps)
and also in the statistical study of ILW.
See Remark
1.2
.

In recent years, there have been intensive research activities
on mathematical analysis of ILW;
see
[
1
,
73
,
74
,
72
,
47
,
19
,
15
,
31
,
65
]
for the known well-posedness and ill-posedness results
for ILW.
See also
[
1
,
76
,
63
,
64
,
19
]

for results on convergence
of ILW to BO in the deep-water limit (
\delta\to\infty
)
and to KdV in the shallow-water limit (
\delta\to 0
).
2
2

2

Strictly speaking,
in order to discuss convergence to KdV in the shallow-water limit,
we first need to apply the scaling (
1.4
)
and consider the scaled ILW (
1.5
).
In this informal discussion, however, we suppress this point.

In particular, in
[
64
]
,
the second and third authors with G. Zheng
initiated the study of convergence issues from the statistical viewpoint,
where
they constructed the Gibbs measure for ILW
and proved its convergence in the deep-water and shallow-water limits
to those for BO and KdV, respectively,
along with convergence results of invariant Gibbs dynamics
(without uniqueness).
Our main goal in this paper is to
further the statistical study on convergence of ILW to BO and KdV
by exploiting the complete integrability of ILW.

The construction of invariant Gibbs measures
for Hamiltonian PDEs
was initiated in the seminal works
[
62
]

by Lebowitz, Rose, and Speer
and
[
12
,
13
]
by
Bourgain.
This study lying at the intersection of dispersive PDEs
and probability theory has been particularly active
over the last two decades.
See surveys papers
[
82
,
5
]

for an overview of the subject
and the references therein.
Here, a Gibbs measure
is a probability measure
on
periodic functions (of low regularity)
associated with the Hamiltonian of a given equation.
See

[
62
,
12
,
79
,
110
,
27
,
99
,
88
,
90
,
18
,
89
]

for the construction of Gibbs measures
and their invariance for KdV- and BO-type equations.
For completely integrable PDEs
with infinitely many conservation laws
such as BO and KdV,
one may use higher order conservation laws to construct invariant measures
supported on Sobolev spaces of increasing smoothness.
By importing the terminology from statistical mechanics
[
100
]
,
we refer to such measures
as

generalized Gibbs measures
.
In
[
118
,
119
]
,
Zhidkov constructed an infinite sequence of invariant generalized Gibbs measures
associated with higher order conservation laws of
KdV.
In a series of works

[
113
,
114
,
115
,
28
]
,
Tzvetkov and Visciglia with Deng
carried out an analogous program
for BO.
See also

[
120
,
77
,
33
,
34
]

for related results on the one-dimensional (derivative) cubic nonlinear Schrödinger equation.
In this work, we investigate
the construction
of invariant generalized Gibbs measures
associated with higher order conservation laws of ILW
and their convergence properties
in the deep-water and shallow-water limits
from both the static and dynamical viewpoints.
More precisely,
we establish the following results:

(i)

For each
0<\delta<\infty
,
we construct generalized Gibbs measures,
supported on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
,
associated
with the conservation laws of ILW
at the
H^{\frac{k}{2}}
-level
for each integer
k\geq 2
.

(ii)

We
prove convergence of these generalized Gibbs measures to
the corresponding (invariant)
generalized Gibbs measures
for BO and KdV
in the deep-water and shallow-water limits, respectively.
We also study equivalence
3
3

3

Namely, mutual absolute continuity.
/ singularity properties
of these generalized Gibbs measures.

(iii)

For
k\geq 3
,
we construct invariant ILW dynamics (with uniqueness) associated with these
generalized Gibbs measures
supported on
H^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})
,
\varepsilon>0
.
4
4

4

In the remaining part
of the paper, we use
\varepsilon>0
to denote an arbitrarily small constant.
Various constants depend on
\varepsilon
but,
for simplicity of notation, we suppress such
\varepsilon
-dependence.

Moreover, we prove convergence in the respective limits
of the ILW dynamics at each equilibrium state
to the corresponding invariant dynamics for BO and KdV
constructed by Deng, Tzvetkov, and Visciglia
[
113
,
114
,
115
,
28
]

and Zhidkov
[
118
,
119
]
, respectively.
This provides the first example of invariant ILW dynamics
(with uniqueness)
and their convergence to invariant BO / KdV dynamics.

See Theorems
1.6
and
1.7

for (i) and (ii), and
Theorems
1.9
and
1.10

for (iii).
We point out that, when
k
is large,
the invariant dynamics is supported on smooth functions
and thus the statistical study of ILW carried out in this work
is physically relevant.
For example, the (scaled) ILW invariant dynamics constructed in
Theorems
1.9
and
1.10

enjoys a recurrence property on a phase space of smooth functions,
described by the Poincaré recurrence theorem

[
119
, p. 106]
; see also

[
114
, Corollary 1.3]
.

In order to achieve these objectives,
we first need to carry out a detailed study
on the construction of appropriate (polynomial) conservation laws of
the (scaled) ILW
via the

Bäcklund transform

(see (
3.1
) in the deep-water regime
5
5

5

Hereafter, we use
the terms “deep-water regime” and
“shallow-water regime”
to refer to analysis on the ILW equation (
1.1
)
and the scaled ILW equation (
1.5
), respectively.

and (
4.1
) in the shallow-water regime).
We then
derive
a suitable formulation for conservation laws
of the (scaled) ILW
in each of the deep-water and shallow-water regimes
such that
the (scaled) ILW conservation laws converge
to those of BO and KdV in the respective limits.
We remark the following two points.

•

In order to guarantee the desired convergence
of the (scaled) ILW conservation laws,
we need to use different
Bäcklund transforms
in the deep-water and shallow-water regimes,
generating two sets of the (scaled)
ILW conservation laws.

•

The conservation laws of the (scaled) ILW
thus constructed
are at the

H^{\frac{k}{2}}
-level,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, just like the BO conservation laws.
On the other hand,
the KdV conservation laws are at the
H^{\kappa}
-level,
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
.
Indeed,
we establish a

2-to-1 collapse of the conservation laws
of the scaled ILW to those of KdV
in the shallow-water limit

in the sense that,
for each
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
,

both

the

H^{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}
-level
and

H^{\kappa}
-level
conservation laws of the scaled ILW
converge
to the
same

H^{\kappa}
-level conservation law of KdV;
see Figure
1
below.
Such a 2-to-1 collapse is novel in the literature,
explaining
the reason that
KdV has half as many conservation laws as ILW and BO.
To our knowledge, this is the first construction of a complete family of
shallow-water conservation laws
6
6

6

In the following,
we often refer to conservation laws for ILW (and for the scaled ILW, respectively)
as deep-water conservation laws
(and shallow-water conservation laws, respectively).

with non-trivial shallow-water limits.

The derivation of conservation laws
for ILW (
1.1
) and the scaled ILW (
1.5
),
suitable to study their deep-water and shallow-water limits, respectively,
involves rather intricate and lengthy algebraic / combinatorial computations
and takes a non-trivial portion of this work.
Indeed,
one of the main novelties
of this work is
to provide the first rigorous justification for convergence of
(suitably constructed) conservation laws of the (scaled) ILW
to those of BO and KdV
in the respective limits,
in particular exhibiting the 2-to-1 collapse in the shallow-water limit;
see Theorems
1.3
and
1.4
.
Such a 2-to-1 collapse in the shallow-water limit
also appears
at the level of measure convergence
as well as dynamical convergence,
which is
a completely new phenomenon in the statistical study of dispersive PDEs.

Remark 1.1
.

In
[
12
]
, Bourgain proved
invariance of the Gibbs measure for KdV
associated with the Hamiltonian
(= the
H^{1}
-level conservation law).
In
[
118
,
119
]
,
Zhidkov constructed invariant generalized Gibbs measures
for KdV
associated with the
H^{\kappa}
-level conservation laws
for
\kappa\geq 3
.
See also
[
98
,
80
,
81
,
82
,
87
,
51
]

for invariance of the white noise for KdV
(corresponding to the
\kappa=0
case).
However, the
\kappa=2
case seems to be missing in the literature.
As a byproduct of our analysis,
we establish invariance of the
generalized Gibbs measure associated
with the
H^{2}
-conservation law of KdV,
thus completing the picture for KdV;
see Theorem
1.10
(ii).
See also a discussion in
[
113
, Subsection 1.3]
.
In recent works
[
112
,
14
]
,
new kinds of invariant measures
for BO and KdV have been constructed,
based on more refined properties
of these equations
(namely, the Birkhoff coordinates and the perturbation determinant, respectively).
It would be of interest to investigate such
construction for the (scaled) ILW.

Remark 1.2
.

In the current work,
the singular nature of the
shallow-water regime
appears in various contexts.
First of all, we note that 2-to-1 collapse occurs in
the shallow-water convergence
of the shallow-water conservation laws (Theorem
1.4
),
the corresponding generalized Gibbs measures
(Theorem
1.7
),
and the invariant scaled ILW dynamics
(Theorem
1.10
).
In particular, we observe regularity jumps
in all of these three settings.
Moreover, we show that, in the deep-water regime,
the generalized Gibbs measures
with different depth parameters
(including the limiting one)
are all equivalent,
while
the situation is much more complicated
in the shallow-water regime;
(i) the generalized Gibbs measure with
0<\delta<\infty

and the limiting generalized Gibbs measure (with
\delta=0
)
are singular,
(ii) the odd-order generalized Gibbs measures
with different depth parameters
are singular,
and
(iii) the even-order generalized Gibbs measures
with different depth parameters
are equivalent.
As a consequence,
the shallow-water generalized Gibbs measures
converge only weakly as
\delta\to 0
, while the deep-water
generalized Gibbs measures
converge in total variation as
\delta\to\infty
.
All these phenomena mentioned above
are completely new.

We also mention a recent work
[
17
]
,
exhibiting the singular nature of the shallow-water regime
on the real line; see also Remark
1.14
.

1.2.
Conservation laws of ILW

Before we start our statistical study of the (scaled) ILW,
we first need to carry out detailed analysis on
the derivation of suitable
(polynomial) conservation laws of the (scaled) ILW
along with their convergence properties
in the deep-water and shallow-water limits.
It is worthwhile to note that
ILW (
1.1
) and the scaled ILW (
1.5
) differ only by the scaling (
1.4
).
Hence, once we obtain conservation laws of ILW (
1.1
)
in a form suitable to study their deep-water limits
(as
\delta\to\infty
),
the scaling transform (
1.4
)
yields
the corresponding
conservation laws of the scaled ILW (
1.5
).
However,
if we simply use such conservation laws,
then we
encounter multiple divergent terms
(with negative powers of
\delta
)
in the shallow-water limit
(
\delta\to 0
).
While it is possible to manually
group divergent terms to obtain a (conditionally) convergent limit
for the first few conservation laws,
the number of divergent terms grows
larger for higher order conservation laws
and it immediately becomes intractable.
In order to overcome this issue,
we will separately derive conservation laws of the scaled ILW
in a form suitable to
study their shallow-water limits.

We first consider the deep-water regime.
In this case, we follow
the work
[
102
]

by Satsuma, Ablowitz, and Kodama
and
derive conservation laws of
ILW
(
1.1
)
via the Bäcklund transform (
3.1
);
see also
[
1
]
.
While the derivation of conservation laws for ILW is well known
in the literature,
there seems to be no proof on convergence of the conservation laws
(beyond a formal proof, claiming that
such convergence should follow from convergence of
the Bäcklund transforms).
Our main task is to derive conservation laws
of ILW in the “correct” form such that
they converge to BO conservation laws in the deep-water limit
and, moreover,
the quadratic part is sign-definite, which is crucial
in defining generalized Gibbs measures
associated with these deep-water conservation laws;
see
Section
3

for details.
As a result, we obtain the following conservation laws
for ILW:
7
7

7

In the following,
we impose the mean-zero assumption for all the equations appearing in this paper,
and hence we will not refer to the conservation of mean in the remaining part of the paper.

\displaystyle\text{mean:}\ \ \int_{\mathbb{T}}udx

and
\big\{E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)\big\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
, where
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}:=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}

and
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
is given by

(1.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}E^{\delta}_{0}(u)&=\frac{1}{2}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}},\\
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)&=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{k}a_{k,\ell}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{\frac{k-\ell}{2}}u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{k}{2}}}+R^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u),\quad k\in\mathbb{N},\end{split}

for some
positive
8
8

8

The positivity
of the coefficients
a_{k,\ell}
plays an important role in
defining
the base Gaussian measures
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

in (
1.25
).

constants
a_{k,\ell}
(see (
3.40
))
such that

(1.8)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{k}a_{k,\ell}=1,

where, for odd
k
and even
\ell
, we used the convention:

(1.9)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{\frac{k-\ell}{2}}u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{k}{2}}}=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{x}^{\frac{\ell}{2}}u\cdot(\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x})^{k-\ell}\partial_{x}^{\frac{\ell}{2}}udx

by noting that

\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}
is a self-adjoint and positive operator with
an even multiplier
(Lemma
2.2
).
Here,
the interaction potential

R^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
consists of cubic and higher order terms in
u
;
see (
3.62
).
For readers’ convenience, we write down the case
k=1,2
:

(1.10)

\displaystyle\begin{split}E^{\delta}_{\frac{1}{2}}(u)&=\frac{1}{2}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{\frac{1}{2}}u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\frac{1}{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u^{3}dx,\\
E^{\delta}_{1}(u)&=\frac{1}{8}\|u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{1}}+\frac{3}{8}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{1}}+\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{1}{4}u^{4}+\frac{3}{4}u^{2}\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}u+\frac{1}{4\delta}u^{3}dx.\end{split}

Note that
E^{\delta}_{\frac{1}{2}}(u)
is the Hamiltonian for ILW (
1.1
).
The positivity of the constants
a_{k,\ell}
ensures that the quadratic part
of the conservation law is sign-definite.
Moreover,
in view of
Lemma
2.2
, we have

(1.11)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{k}a_{k,\ell}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{\frac{k-\ell}{2}}u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{k}{2}}}\sim_{k,\delta}\|u\|_{\dot{H}^{\frac{k}{2}}}^{2},

where the equivalence is uniform in
2\leq\delta<\infty
.
Namely,

E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
is indeed an
H^{\frac{k}{2}}
-level conservation law
of ILW.

As
\delta\to\infty
,
the operator
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
converges to the Hilbert transform
\mathcal{H}

(see Lemma
2.2
).
Then, by formally taking a limit as
\delta\to\infty
in (
1.7
)
with (
1.8
),
we obtain the following conservation laws
for BO (
1.3
):

(1.12)

\displaystyle\begin{split}E^{\text{\rm BO}}_{0}(u)&=E^{\infty}_{0}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}},\\
E^{\text{\rm BO}}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)&=E^{\infty}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\|u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{k}{2}}}+R^{\text{\rm BO}}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u),\quad k\in\mathbb{N},\end{split}

where once again
the interaction potential

R^{\text{\rm BO}}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)=R^{\infty}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

consists of cubic and higher order terms in
u
.
For example, when
k=1,2
, we have

\displaystyle E^{\text{\rm BO}}_{\frac{1}{2}}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\frac{1}{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u^{3}dx,

\displaystyle E^{\text{\rm BO}}_{1}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{1}}+\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{1}{4}u^{4}+\frac{3}{4}u^{2}\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}udx.

We point out that these quantities

E^{\text{\rm BO}}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

can also be directly constructed
from
the Bäcklund transform (
3.17
)
for BO (see (
3.18
) and (
3.41
)),
and thus they are indeed conserved under the flow of BO.

Theorem 1.3

(deep-water regime)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
Then,
given
0<\delta<\infty
,
the quantity
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

defined in (
1.7
)
is conserved under the flow of ILW (
1.1
).
9
9

9

In discussing
conservation of a quantity under a given equation,
we always assume that
a solution
u
is sufficiently smooth
such that both the quantity and the flow make sense.

Similarly,
the quantity
E^{\text{\rm BO}}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

defined in (
1.12
)
is conserved under the flow of BO (
1.3
).
Moreover,
for any
u\in H^{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbb{T})
, we have

(1.13)

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to\infty}E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)=E_{\frac{k}{2}}^{\text{\rm BO}}(u).

See
Proposition
3.8

for a proof of Theorem
1.3
.
We prove Theorem
1.3

by first deriving microscopic conservation laws
\chi_{n}^{\delta}
and
\chi_{n}^{\text{\rm BO}}
for ILW and BO
via the Bäcklund transforms (
3.1
) and (
3.17
), respectively.
Our goal is to derive conservation laws in the “correct” form as described above.
In fact,
a straightforward derivation of ILW conservation laws
via
the microscopic conservation laws
\chi_{n}^{\delta}

yields
quadratic terms of different regularities
(not just the
\dot{H}^{\frac{k}{2}}
-norms appearing in (
1.7
); see Lemma
3.6
)
which may not be suitable for constructing (weighted)
Gaussian measures.
Moreover,
in our recursive construction of conservation laws,
we ensure that the constants
a_{k,\ell}

are all positive,
guaranteeing that the quadratic part of
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
is sign-definite.
This plays a crucial role
in constructing the Gaussian measures
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
in (
1.25
)
and studying their further properties (Proposition
5.1
).
Similar comments apply to the shallow-water regime.

As for BO,
identities such as
\mathcal{H}^{2}=-\textup{Id}

(on mean-zero functions)
make it relatively straightforward to derive conservation laws from a recursive formulation.
As for ILW, however,
there is no such identity for
the operator
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
.
Moreover, there are terms with negative powers of
\delta

(such as the last term of
E^{\delta}_{1}(u)
in (
1.10
); see also (
3.62
))
which all vanish in the deep-water limit.
As a result,
the number of terms (including quadratic ones;
compare the quadratic parts in (
1.7
)
and (
1.12
))
becomes much larger than the BO case,
requiring
careful combinatorial computations.
Further details on the structure of these conservation laws can be found in Section
3
.

In order to prove the desired deep-water convergence (
1.13
),
we introduce the notion of the
deep-water rank

in Definition
3.3
(see also Definition
3.7
)
which allows us to organize the combinatorially complex structure
of the monomials appearing in the deep-water conservation laws.
Moreover, following
the recent works
[
47
,
19
,
15
]
,
we employ a perturbative viewpoint
by introducing
the perturbation operator
\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
:
10
10

10

The perturbation operator considered
in
[
47
,
19
,
15
]
is
slightly different from
\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
in (
1.14
)
and is given by

\mathcal{T}_{\delta}\partial_{x}-\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}
, where
\mathcal{T}_{\delta}
is the Tilbert transform defined in (
2.22
).

(1.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}=\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}-\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}.

In
[
47
,
19
,
15
]
,
by rewriting
ILW (
1.1
)
as

(1.15)

\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}^{2}u=\partial_{x}(u^{2})+\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}\partial_{x}u

and exploiting a strong smoothing property of the perturbation operator

\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
(see Lemma 2.2 in
[
47
]

and Lemma 2.3 in
[
19
]
),
the authors of the aforementioned works established results on
low-regularity well-posedness of ILW, deep-water convergence,
and
long-time behavior of ILW solutions.
For our analysis,
we use this perturbative viewpoint
to replace the recursive relation (
3.4
)
for the microscopic conservation laws
\chi_{n}^{\delta}
,
involving
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
,
by the recursive relation (
3.33
),
involving
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
.
Together with the deep-water rank,
the strong vanishing of
\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
(uniform in frequencies) as
\delta\to\infty
(see (
2.10
))
will then allow us to prove (
1.13
).

Next,
we consider the shallow-water regime.
As mentioned above, the conservation laws

E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
in (
1.7
)
under the scaling (
1.4
)
yield conservation laws of the scaled ILW (
1.5
).
They are, however, not suitable for studying the shallow-water convergence,
since multiple terms diverge as
\delta\to 0
.
In order to overcome this difficulty,
we use an alternative Bäcklund transform (
4.1
)
derived in the works

[
36
,
59
]

by Gibbons and Kupershmidt,
from which we obtain
a recursive relation
for generating shallow-water microscopic conservation laws
h_{n}^{\delta}
;
see (
4.5
).
There are two issues in directly using

\int_{\mathbb{T}}h_{n}^{\delta}dx
to construct shallow-water conservation laws:

(i)

the quadratic part comes with terms of different regularities,
just as we saw in the deep-water regime,

(ii)

the odd-order conservation laws

\int_{\mathbb{T}}h_{2n+1}^{\delta}dx
vanish
in the shallow-water limit; see Lemma
4.3
(i).
This in particular causes an issue
in pursuing our statistical study
since the corresponding generalized Gibbs measures
would not have any meaningful limit.

In order to overcome these issues,
we construct new
microscopic conservation laws

\widetilde{h}_{n}^{\delta}
from
h_{n}^{\delta}
(see (
4.36
)),
which
allows us to construct
a full set of shallow-water conservation laws
with non-trivial shallow-water limits.
As a result,
we arrive at the following conservation laws
\big\{\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)\big\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
for the scaled ILW:

(1.16)

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{0}(v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|v\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=1\\
\text{odd}\end{subarray}}^{2\kappa-1}\widetilde{a}_{2\kappa-1,\ell}\,\delta^{\ell-1}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{\frac{\ell}{2}}v\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}}+\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v),

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{2\kappa}\widetilde{a}_{2\kappa,\ell}\,\delta^{\ell}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{\frac{\ell}{2}}v\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\kappa}}+\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)

for
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
,
where, for odd
k
and
\ell
, we used the convention:

(1.17)

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{\frac{\ell}{2}}v\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{k}{2}}}=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{x}^{\frac{k-\ell}{2}}v\cdot(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\partial_{x})^{\ell}\partial_{x}^{\frac{k-\ell}{2}}vdx

by noting that

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\partial_{x}
is a self-adjoint and positive operator with
an even multiplier
(Lemma
2.2
);
see also (
1.9
)
for an analogous convention.
Here,

\widetilde{a}_{k,\ell}
is
a positive constant (see (
4.59
))
with the following normalization on the leading coefficients:
11
11

11

The normalization
\widetilde{a}_{2\kappa-1,1}=3

is needed to cancel the factor
\frac{1}{3}
which appears
in the shallow-water limit of
in\delta^{-1}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(n)
; see Lemma
2.3
.
See also (
1.34
) and (
1.38
).

(1.18)

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}_{2\kappa-1,1}=3\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widetilde{a}_{2\kappa,0}=1,

and the interaction potentials

\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)

and
\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)

consist of cubic and higher order terms in
v
;
see (
4.92
).
Here, we relabelled the indices
for
the conservation laws
(from
\frac{k}{2}
to
\kappa-\frac{1}{2}

and
\kappa
), since the structure of the conservation law

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)

depends
sensitively
on the parity of
k

(namely,
k=2\kappa-1
or
k=2\kappa
,
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
)
in the current shallow-water regime.
Moreover,
this relabelling allows us to
emphasize that

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)

and
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)

appear in a pair; see (
1.20
).
See
Section
4

for further details on the derivation and the structure of the
shallow-water
conservation laws
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)

and
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)

in (
1.16
).

By taking a formal limit of the Bäcklund transform
(
4.1
) for the scaled ILW as
\delta\to 0
,
we obtain
the Bäcklund transform
(
4.19
) for KdV
(= the Gardner transform),
from which we derive the following conservation laws for KdV:

(1.19)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{0}(v)&=\widetilde{E}^{0}_{0}(v)=\frac{1}{2}\|v\|^{2}_{L^{2}},\\
\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v)&=\widetilde{E}^{0}_{\kappa}(v)=\frac{1}{2}\|v\|_{\dot{H}^{\kappa}}^{2}+\widetilde{R}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v),\quad\kappa\in\mathbb{N},\end{split}

where
the interaction potential

\widetilde{R}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v)
consists of cubic and higher order terms in
v
;
see (
4.60
).

We now state a result on convergence properties of
the shallow-water conservation laws,
which is one of the main novelties of the current work.

Theorem 1.4

(shallow-water regime)
.

Let
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then,
given
0<\delta<\infty
,
the quantities

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{0}(v)
,

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)
,
and
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)

defined in (
1.16
)
are conserved under the flow of the scaled ILW (
1.5
).
Similarly,
the quantities

\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{0}(v)
and

\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v)

defined in (
1.19
)
are conserved under the flow of KdV (
1.6
).
Moreover,
for any
v\in H^{\kappa}(\mathbb{T})
, we have

(1.20)

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)=\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v).

Theorem
1.4
establishes a
novel 2-to-1 collapse
of the shallow-water conservation laws
to the KdV conservation laws,
presenting
the first construction of a complete family of
shallow-water conservation laws with non-trivial shallow-water limits;
see Remark
1.5
below.
From (
1.20
) with (
1.16
),
we see that while there is no regularity jump
in the convergence of

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)

to

\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v)
,
there is a regularity jump by
\frac{1}{2}

in the convergence of

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)

to

\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v)
,
exhibiting a singular nature of the shallow-water limit.
See Figure
1
.

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)
\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v)
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)
\delta\to 0
\delta\to 0

Figure 1.
While there is no regularity jump
in the convergence of

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)

to

\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v)
,
there is a regularity jump by
\frac{1}{2}

in the convergence of

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)
.

See
Propositions
4.10

and
4.11

for a proof of Theorem
1.4
.
As mentioned above,
we prove Theorem
1.4

by starting with the Bäcklund transform (
4.1
),
which generates microscopic conservation laws
h_{n}^{\delta}

(see (
4.5
)).
We then
construct new
microscopic conservation laws

\widetilde{h}^{\delta}_{n}
from
h^{\delta}_{n}
(see (
4.36
))
which allows us to overcome the issues (i) and (ii) mentioned above;
see Lemma
4.6
and
Propositions
4.10

and
4.11
.
This process and the analysis on the structure
of
\widetilde{h}_{n}^{\delta}

involve lengthy algebraic, combinatorial computations.
In order to handle the combinatorial complexity,
we introduce the notion of the
shallow-water rank

in Definition
4.7
(see also Definition
4.9
).
Furthermore, as in the deep-water case, we also employ a perturbative viewpoint
by introducing the perturbation operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\delta}
:

(1.21)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\delta}=\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}+\frac{1}{3}\partial_{x}.

We point out that our argument for proving
the shallow-water convergence (
1.20
)
is more subtle than the deep-water case
due to
the slower convergence of the perturbation operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\delta}

(in fact, of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\delta}\partial_{x}
which is not uniform in frequencies; see Lemma
2.3
(iv))
and also insufficient powers of
\delta
(tending to
0
).

Remark 1.5
.

(i)
The numbers of
the microscopic conservation laws
for the scaled ILW and KdV coincide.
However,
upon integration over the torus,
the odd-order microscopic conservation laws for KdV vanish
(see Lemma
4.3
(i)),
while those for the scaled ILW (whether
h_{n}^{\delta}
defined in (
4.5
)
or
\widetilde{h}_{n}^{\delta}
defined in (
4.36
)) do not vanish,
which
leaves KdV with half as many (non-trivial) conservation laws as the scaled ILW.

(ii) The family
\big\{\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)\big\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}

(and

\big\{\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v)\big\}_{\kappa\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
)
is the only possible polynomial conservation laws
of the scaled ILW (and KdV, respectively)
in the sense that
any other polynomial conservation laws are given
as their linear combination;
see
[
56
, Theorem 4]

and

[
59
, Theorems 2.16 and 2.28]
.

1.3.
Generalized Gibbs measures
associated with the conservation laws of ILW

We are now ready to state our results
on the statistical study of ILW.
In this subsection, we discuss
the construction and convergence properties
of generalized Gibbs measures associated with the conservation laws
of the (scaled) ILW.
We discuss the dynamical problem in the next subsection.

We first consider the deep-water regime,
including the BO case.
Given
0<\delta\leq\infty
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
let
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

be as in (
1.7
) and (
1.12
).
Our first goal is to construct a generalized Gibbs measure
associated with
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

whose formal density
is given by
12
12

12

Hereafter, we use

Z_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}
, etc. to denote various normalization constants
which may vary line by line.

(1.22)

\displaystyle\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(du)

\displaystyle=Z_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}^{-1}\exp\Big(-E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)\Big)du

as a probability measure on periodic functions / distributions.
When
k=0
, this corresponds to the white noise on
\mathbb{T}
with formal density:

(1.23)

\displaystyle\rho_{0}(du)=Z_{0}^{-1}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}du.

Since it is independent of
0<\delta\leq\infty
,
there is nothing to study regarding its convergence as
\delta\to\infty
.
Moreover, the white noise is supported
on
H^{-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})
,
where well-posedness of ILW and BO is completely open.
Hence, in the remaining part of this paper,
we focus on
k\geq 1
; see Remark
1.13
.
See also
[
80
,
82
,
4
,
87
,
85
]

for a further discussion on the white noise.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then, from (
1.22
) with (
1.7
) and (
1.12
),
we can formally write
\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
as
the following weighted Gaussian measure:

(1.24)

\displaystyle\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(du)

\displaystyle=Z_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}^{-1}\exp\Big(-R^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)\Big)d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u),

where

\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
is the base Gaussian measure with formal density:

(1.25)

\displaystyle\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(du)=Z_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}^{-1}\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{k}a_{k,\ell}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{\frac{k-\ell}{2}}u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{k}{2}}}\bigg)du.

More precisely,

\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

is defined as the induced probability measure under the map:

(1.26)

\displaystyle\omega\in\Omega\longmapsto X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\omega)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{g_{n}(\omega)}{\big(T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}}e_{n},

where

\mathbb{Z}^{*}=\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
,
e_{n}(x)=e^{inx}
,

\{g_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
is a sequence of independent standard
13
13

13

By convention,
we assume that
g_{n}

has mean 0 and variance
2
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.
complex-valued Gaussian random variables
on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})

conditioned that
g_{-n}=\overline{g_{n}}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
,
and

(1.27)

\displaystyle T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{k}a_{k,\ell}|n|^{k}|\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{\delta}}(n)|^{k-\ell}

with
\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{\delta}}(n)
as in (
1.2
).
In view of (
1.11
)
(see also (
5.1
)),
one can easily show that
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

is supported on
H^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})

but that
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}\big(H^{\frac{k-1}{2}}(\mathbb{T})\big)=0
.
When
\delta=\infty
, it follows from (
1.8
)
with
\lim_{\delta\to\infty}\mathcal{G}_{\delta}=\mathcal{H}

that

T_{\infty,\frac{k}{2}}(n)=|n|^{k}
,
and thus
the base Gaussian measure is given by

(1.28)

\displaystyle\mu^{\infty}_{\frac{k}{2}}(du)=Z_{\infty,\frac{k}{2}}^{-1}\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{2}\|u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{k}{2}}}\bigg)du.

The interaction potential

R^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
, consisting of cubic and higher order terms in
u
,
is not sign-definite (see (
3.62
) below), exhibiting super-Gaussian growth.
As a result, the expression in (
1.24
)
can not be normalized to be a probability measure.
Following the work
[
62
,
12
,
90
]
on the construction of focusing Gibbs measures,
we instead consider a weighted Gaussian measure with an
L^{2}
-cutoff.
More precisely,
let
\eta\in C^{\infty}([0,\infty);[0,1])

be a smooth cutoff function
supported on
[0,2]

with
\eta\equiv 1
on
[0,1]
.
Then, given
0<\delta\leq\infty
and
K>0
, we aim to construct the
deep-water
generalized Gibbs measure of the form:
14
14

14

A precise
value of
K>0
does not play any role in the remaining part of this paper
and hence, for simplicity of notation,
we suppress the
K
-dependence of

\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

and
F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

in (
1.29
).
We apply the same convention to various objects throughout the paper.

(1.29)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(du)&=Z^{-1}_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}\,F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)\\
:\!&=Z^{-1}_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}\,\eta_{K}\big(\|u\|_{L^{2}}\big)\exp\Big(-R^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)\Big)d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u),\end{split}

where
\eta_{K}(\,\cdot\,)=\eta(K^{-1}\,\cdot\,)
.
When
k=1
, the support of

\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
is outside
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})

and hence we need to replace the
L^{2}
-cutoff by a renormalized
L^{2}
-cutoff.
Since the
k=1
is already treated in
[
110
,
89
,
64
]
,
we focus on
k\geq 2
and suppress this issue; see
[
64
]

for a further discussion
on the
k=1
case.

We construct the generalized Gibbs measure
\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
as a (unique) limit
of its frequency-truncated versions.
Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbf{P}_{N}
be the Dirichlet projector onto spatial frequencies
\{|n|\leq N\}
;
see (
2.2
).
Then,
define the truncated generalized Gibbs measure

\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}
by

(1.30)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}(du)&=Z^{-1}_{\delta,\frac{k}{2},N}\,F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)\\
&=Z^{-1}_{\delta,\frac{k}{2},N}\,\eta_{K}\big(\|\mathbf{P}_{N}u\|_{L^{2}}\big)\exp\Big(-R^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)\Big)d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u).\end{split}

The following theorem shows
that, as
N\to\infty
,

\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}
converges in total variation
to

\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
in (
1.29
)
with a uniform rate of convergence
for
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
Moreover, we establish
deep-water convergence (as
\delta\to\infty
) of

\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
.

Theorem 1.6

(deep-water regime)
.

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer and
K>0
. Then, the following statements hold.

(i)
Let
0<\delta\leq\infty
.
Then, given any finite
p\geq 1
, we have

(1.31)

\lim_{N\to\infty}F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)=F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)\quad\text{in }L^{p}(d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}),

where the convergence is uniform in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
As a consequence, as
N\to\infty
,
the truncated generalized Gibbs measure
\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}
in (
1.30
) converges, in the sense
of (
1.31
), to the limiting generalized Gibbs measure
\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
in (
1.29
).
In particular,
\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}
converges to
\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
in total variation,
and
the limiting generalized Gibbs measure
\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
is
equivalent
to the Gaussian measure with the
L^{2}
-cutoff

\eta_{K}\big(\|u\|_{L^{2}}\big)d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
.

(ii)

For
0<\delta<\infty
, the generalized Gibbs measures
\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
and

\rho^{\infty}_{\frac{k}{2}}
are equivalent.
Moreover,
as
\delta\to\infty
,

\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
converges to
\rho^{\infty}_{\frac{k}{2}}
in total variation.

In
[
64
]
, the second and third authors with G. Zheng
proved an analogue of Theorem
1.6

for
k=1
, corresponding to the Gibbs measures.
Theorem
1.6
extends the result in
[
64
]

to any
k\geq 2
.
In
[
113
]
,
Tzvetkov and Visciglia
constructed
the generalized Gibbs measures associated with
the higher order conservation laws of the BO equation (
1.3
).
Their measures were constructed
with smooth cutoffs on the lower order conservation laws

E^{\text{\rm BO}}_{\frac{j}{2}}(u)
, for
j=0,\ldots,k-1

(with a renormalization for
j=k-1
).
In Theorem
1.6
,
we only impose an
L^{2}
-cutoff,
thus providing
a significant simplification.
We also point out that
our construction of the generalized Gibbs measure
\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

only with an
L^{2}
-cutoff
also simplifies the invariance argument in the proof of Theorem
1.9

as compared to
[
114
,
115
]
;
see Remarks
1.11
and
8.7
below.
A similar comment applies to the shallow-water regime.
Our simplified construction of the generalized Gibbs measure
is based on
the Boué-Dupuis variational formula
[
10
,
116
]
,
recently popularized by Barashkov and Gubinelli
[
2
]
.
See also
the (non-)construction of focusing Gibbs measures in
[
83
,
89
,
84
,
107
]

via the variational formula.

The construction of the generalized Gibbs measure
\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

for each fixed
0<\delta\leq\infty

follows from
establishing a uniform (in
N
) estimate on the truncated densities

F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)

in
L^{p}(d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})
.
On the other hand,
in order to
prove the deep-water convergence in Theorem
1.6
(ii),
we need to establish an
L^{p}(d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})
-estimate
on

F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)
,

uniformly in both
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta\gg 1
.
Our detailed analysis
in Section
3

on the structure
of the deep-water conservation laws
and
a very robust variation formula
(Lemma
5.5
)
allow us to
achieve these goals, working
only with the
L^{2}
-cutoff.
We also
note that
the base Gaussian measures
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
are different
for different values of
\delta
.
In order to overcome this issue, we indeed
work with
the random variable
X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
in (
1.26
)
and
estimate

F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})
in
L^{p}(\Omega)
,
uniformly in both
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta\gg 1
.

Once we obtain such a uniform
L^{p}
-bound,
the desired deep-water convergence
essentially follows from establishing
the corresponding deep-water convergence of
the Gaussian measure
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
;
see Proposition
5.1
,
where we in fact prove convergence of
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
in a stronger topology
(namely, in the Kullback-Leibler divergence
defined in Definition
2.11
).
As for equivalence of the generalized Gibbs measures
with different depth parameters,
we first prove
equivalence of the Gaussian measures
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

and
\mu^{\infty}_{\frac{k}{2}}

via
Kakutani’s theorem (Lemma
2.9
),
using the Gaussian Fourier series representation (
1.26
).
Then, the claimed equivalence
of
\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
and

\rho^{\infty}_{\frac{k}{2}}

follows
from Theorem
1.6
(i).

We now turn to the shallow-water regime.
Due to the more complicated structure of the shallow-water conservation laws in (
1.16
)
and the singular nature of their shallow-water convergence,
the construction and convergence of the generalized Gibbs measures
associated with these conservation laws require more
careful analysis.
For
0<\delta<\infty
, we first define the base Gaussian measure
\widetilde{\mu}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
,

k\in\mathbb{N}
,
associated with the quadratic part
of the shallow-water conservation law

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)
in (
1.16
),
by setting, for
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
,

(1.32)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{\mu}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(dv)&=\widetilde{Z}^{-1}_{\delta,\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=1\\
\text{odd}\end{subarray}}^{2\kappa-1}\widetilde{a}_{2\kappa-1,\ell}\,\delta^{\ell-1}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{\frac{\ell}{2}}v\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}}\bigg)dv,\\
\widetilde{\mu}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(dv)&=\widetilde{Z}^{-1}_{\delta,\kappa}\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{2\kappa}\widetilde{a}_{2\kappa,\ell}\,\delta^{\ell}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{\frac{\ell}{2}}v\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\kappa}}\bigg)dv.\end{split}

Namely,

\widetilde{\mu}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}

and
\widetilde{\mu}^{\delta}_{\kappa}
are defined as the induced probability measures under the following maps:

(1.33)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\omega\in\Omega&\longmapsto\widetilde{X}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(\omega)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{g_{n}(\omega)}{\big(\widetilde{T}_{\delta,\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(n)\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}}e_{n},\\
\omega\in\Omega&\longmapsto\widetilde{X}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(\omega)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{g_{n}(\omega)}{\big(\widetilde{T}_{\delta,\kappa}(n)\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}}e_{n},\end{split}

respectively,
where

\{g_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
is as in (
1.26
)
and

(1.34)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{T}_{\delta,\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(n)&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=1\\
\text{odd}\end{subarray}}^{2\kappa-1}\widetilde{a}_{2\kappa-1,\ell}\,\delta^{\ell-1}|n|^{2\kappa-1}|\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}_{\delta}(n)|^{\ell},\\
\widetilde{T}_{\delta,\kappa}(n)&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{2\kappa}\widetilde{a}_{2\kappa,\ell}\,\delta^{\ell}|n|^{2\kappa}|\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}_{\delta}(n)|^{\ell}.\end{split}

Here,
\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}}(n)
is the multiplier for
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
in (
1.5
),
given by

(1.35)

\displaystyle\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}}(n)=\delta^{-1}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(n),

where
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(n)
is as in (
1.2
).
In view of (
6.6
),
one can show that
\widetilde{\mu}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
is supported on
H^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})

but that
\widetilde{\mu}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}\big(H^{\frac{k-1}{2}}(\mathbb{T})\big)=0
.

As in the deep-water regime, we restrict our attention to
k\geq 2

in the following such that the Gaussian measure
\widetilde{\mu}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

is supported on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
.
Then, given
K>0
,
we define the shallow-water generalized Gibbs measure

\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

associated with
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)
in (
1.16
)
by setting

(1.36)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(dv)&=\widetilde{Z}^{-1}_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}\,\widetilde{F}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)d\widetilde{\mu}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)\\
:\!&=\widetilde{Z}^{-1}_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}\,\eta_{K}\big(\|v\|_{L^{2}}\big)\exp\Big(-\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)\Big)d\widetilde{\mu}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v).\end{split}

Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
we also define their frequency-truncated version:

(1.37)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}(dv)&=\widetilde{Z}^{-1}_{\delta,\frac{k}{2},N}\,\widetilde{F}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}v)d\widetilde{\mu}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v).\end{split}

Let us now discuss the
\delta=0
case.
We note that, in taking a limit of

\widetilde{T}_{\delta,\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(n)

(and
\widetilde{T}_{\delta,\kappa}(n)
) in (
1.34
)
as
\delta\to 0
,
all the terms except for
\ell=1
(and
\ell=0
, respectively)
vanish.
In fact, we have

(1.38)

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\widetilde{T}_{\delta,\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(n)=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\widetilde{T}_{\delta,\kappa}(n)=n^{2\kappa}.

See
Lemma
6.2
.
Hence, for
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
,
we define the generalized Gibbs measure

\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\kappa}

associated with
the KdV conservation laws
\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v)
in (
1.19
)
by setting

(1.39)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\kappa}(dv)&=\widetilde{Z}^{-1}_{0,\kappa}\,\widetilde{F}^{0}_{\kappa}(v)d\widetilde{\mu}^{0}_{\kappa}(v)\\
&=\widetilde{Z}^{-1}_{0,\kappa}\,\eta_{K}\big(\|v\|_{L^{2}}\big)\exp\Big(-\widetilde{R}^{0}_{\kappa}(v)\Big)d\widetilde{\mu}^{0}_{\kappa}(v)\end{split}

along with their truncated version:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\kappa,N}(dv)=\widetilde{Z}^{-1}_{0,\kappa,N}\,\widetilde{F}^{0}_{\kappa}(\mathbf{P}_{N}v)d\widetilde{\mu}^{0}_{\kappa}(v),

where

(1.40)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}^{0}_{\kappa}:=\mu^{\infty}_{\kappa}

is the Gaussian measure
defined in
(
1.28
) with
k=2\kappa
.
Namely,

\widetilde{\mu}^{0}_{\kappa}

is the induced probability measure under the map:

(1.41)

\displaystyle\omega\in\Omega\longmapsto\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\kappa}(\omega)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{g_{n}(\omega)}{\big(\widetilde{T}_{0,\kappa}(n)\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}}e_{n}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{g_{n}(\omega)}{|n|^{\kappa}}e_{n}.

We now state our main result
on the construction and further properties
of the shallow-water generalized Gibbs measures
\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
in (
1.36
).

Theorem 1.7

(shallow-water regime)
.

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer and
K>0
. Then, the following statements hold.

(i)
Let

0\leq\delta<\infty
.
Then, given any finite
p\geq 1
, we have

(1.42)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\widetilde{F}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}v)=\widetilde{F}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)\quad\text{in }L^{p}(d\widetilde{\mu}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}),

where the convergence is uniform in
0<\delta\leq 1
.
As a consequence, as
N\to\infty
, the truncated generalized Gibbs measure

\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}
in (
1.37
) converges, in the sense
of (
1.42
), to the limiting generalized Gibbs measure

\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
in (
1.36
).
In particular,
\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}
converges to
\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
in total variation,
and the limiting generalized Gibbs measure

\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
is
equivalent
to the Gaussian measure with the
L^{2}
-cutoff

\eta_{K}\big(\|v\|_{L^{2}}\big)d\widetilde{\mu}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)
.
When
k
is even, the results extend to
\delta=0
.

(ii)

Let
0<\delta<\infty
.
Then, the generalized Gibbs measures
\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

and

\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil}

are singular.
Moreover, as
\delta\to 0
,
the measure
\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
converges weakly to
\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil}
.
Here,
\lceil x\rceil
denotes
the smallest integer greater than or equal to
x
.

(iii)

Let
0<\delta_{1},\delta_{2}<\infty
.
When
k
is odd, the odd-order generalized Gibbs measures
\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta_{1}}_{\frac{k}{2}}

and

\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta_{2}}_{\frac{k}{2}}

are singular.
On the other hand,
when
k
is even,
the even-order generalized Gibbs measures

\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta_{1}}_{\frac{k}{2}}
and

\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta_{2}}_{\frac{k}{2}}
are equivalent.

Theorem
1.7
manifests the singular nature
of the shallow-water convergence in several ways.
First of all,
Theorem
1.7
establishes a
2-to-1 collapse
of the generalized Gibbs measures

\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}
and
\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\kappa}

in the shallow-water limit.
In particular,
in the convergence
of
\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}

to
\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\kappa}
, there is a regularity jump on the supports of the measures.
A similar regularity jump
of the supports was observed
in
[
64
]
at the level of the scaled ILW Gibbs measure
\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{1}{2}}

(constructed with a renormalized
L^{2}
-cutoff)
converging to the KdV Gibbs measure
\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{1}
(with a shifted
L^{2}
-cutoff);
see
[
64
]
for further details.
Moreover, for
0<\delta<\infty
,
the generalized Gibbs measures

\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}

and
\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\kappa}

are
singular with respect to their shallow-water
limit
\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\kappa}
.
As a consequence, while the shallow-water convergence takes place,
the convergence holds
only weakly
,
exhibiting a sharp contrast to the convergence in total variation observed in the deep-water limit.
We point out that, while
there is no regularity jump in the convergence of
\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\kappa}

to

\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\kappa}
,
stronger convergence (such as that in total variation)
is not possible due to their singularity.
Lastly,
Theorem
1.7
(iii)
exhibits an interesting dichotomy
on singularity / equivalence
of the odd-order and even-order generalized Gibbs measures
with different (positive) depth parameters.

The proof of Theorem
1.7
(i)
is analogous to that of
Theorem
1.6
(i) in the deep-water regime.
In particular, a key step is
establishing a uniform (in
N
and also in
0\leq\delta\leq 1
)
bound on
the truncated densities

\widetilde{F}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)

in
L^{p}(d\widetilde{\mu}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})
.
As for Theorem
1.7
(ii) and (iii),
we establish analogous results
for the base Gaussian measures
(Proposition
6.1
),
which are then used to deduce
the claimed results
for the generalized Gibbs measures,
following the argument in
[
64
]
.

Remark 1.8
.

In
[
64
, Theorem 1.5]
,
singularity between the shallow-water (renormalized) Gibbs measure

\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{1}{2}}
,
0<\delta<\infty
,
and its shallow-water limit

\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\frac{1}{2}}

was established, but
there was no discussion
about singularity / equivalence
of the Gibbs measures with different (positive) depth parameters.
By noting that Proposition
6.1

on singularity of the base Gaussian measures
with different depth parameters
also holds for
k=1
,
we can extend Theorem
1.7
(iii)
to the
k=1
case, establishing
singularity
of the scaled ILW Gibbs measures with different depth parameters.

1.4.
Dynamical problem

We now state our main results
on the dynamical problems.
Given a Banach space
B
,
we endow the space

C(\mathbb{R};B)

with the
compact-open topology in time
(= the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets
in time).

Theorem 1.9

(deep-water regime)
.

Let
k\geq 3
be an integer. Then, the following statements hold.

(i)

Let
0<\delta\leq\infty
.
Then,
the weighted Gaussian measure
\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

in (
1.29
)
is invariant under the flow of ILW (
1.1
)
when
0<\delta<\infty

and under the flow of BO (
1.3
) when
\delta=\infty
.

(ii)

Given
0<\delta\leq\infty
,
there exists an
H^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon}
-valued random variable

u^{\delta}_{0}

with
\Law(u^{\delta}_{0})=\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

such that, as
\delta\to\infty
,
u^{\delta}_{0}
converges almost surely to
u^{\infty}_{0}

in
H^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})
.
Moreover,
as
\delta\to\infty
,
the unique global solution
u^{\delta}
to ILW (
1.1
)

(
with the depth parameter
\delta
)

with
u^{\delta}|_{t=0}=u^{\delta}_{0}

converges almost surely
in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}))

to the unique global solution
u
to BO (
1.3
)
with
u|_{t=0}=u^{\infty}_{0}
.

Theorem 1.10

(shallow-water regime)
.

(i)

Let
k\geq 3
be an integer.
Given
0<\delta<\infty
,
the weighted Gaussian measure
\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

in (
1.36
)
is invariant under the flow of the scaled ILW (
1.5
).

(ii)

Let
\kappa\geq 2
be an integer.
The weighted Gaussian measure
\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\kappa}

in (
1.39
)
is invariant under the flow of KdV (
1.6
).

(iii)

Let
\kappa\geq 2
be an integer.
Given
0<\delta<\infty
,
there exists an
H^{\kappa-1-\varepsilon}
-valued random variable

v^{\delta}_{0}

with
\Law(v^{\delta}_{0})=\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}

such that, as
\delta\to 0
,
v^{\delta}_{0}
converges almost surely
in
H^{\kappa-1-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})

to
v_{0}
with
\Law(v_{0})=\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\kappa}
.
Moreover,
as
\delta\to 0
,
the unique global solution
v^{\delta}
to the scaled ILW (
1.5
)

(
with the depth parameter
\delta
)

with
v^{\delta}|_{t=0}=v^{\delta}_{0}

converges almost surely
in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{\kappa-1-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}))

to the unique global solution
v
to KdV (
1.6
)
with
v|_{t=0}=v_{0}
.

(iv)

Let
\kappa\geq 2
be an integer.
Given
0<\delta<\infty
,
there exists an
H^{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}
-valued random variable

v^{\delta}_{0}

with
\Law(v^{\delta}_{0})=\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\kappa}

such that, as
\delta\to 0
,
v^{\delta}_{0}
converges almost surely
in
H^{k-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})

to
v_{0}
with
\Law(v_{0})=\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\kappa}
.
Moreover,
as
\delta\to 0
,
the unique global solution
v^{\delta}
to the scaled ILW (
1.5
)

(
with the depth parameter
\delta
)

with
v^{\delta}|_{t=0}=v^{\delta}_{0}

converges almost surely
in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}))

to the unique global solution
v
to KdV (
1.6
)
with
v|_{t=0}=v_{0}
.

Theorems
1.9
and
1.10

establish the first construction of invariant dynamics
(with uniqueness)
for the (scaled) ILW equation
and its convergence to invariant BO / KdV dynamics.
Compare these results with the dynamical results in
[
64
]
,
where the authors treated the
k=1
case
but uniqueness was missing due to the use of a compactness argument
(in the probabilistic setting).
Furthermore,
in Theorem
1.10
(iii) and (iv),
we
establish a 2-to-1 collapse at the level of invariant dynamics,
which seems to be the first such result in the literature.
We, however, emphasize that Theorems
1.9

and
1.10
are certainly not a main challenge in this paper
and that it is of much more significance
to construct the “correct”
conservation laws in Theorems
1.3

and
1.4
, from which everything follows.

While a typical study of Gibbs measures
and the corresponding dynamics
leads to studying functions of low regularity,
we restrict our attention to
k\geq 3

and thus the dynamical problems we consider
are not low-regularity well-posedness problems.
In fact,
Molinet and Vento
[
74
]

proved
(unconditional) global well-posedness of
ILW (
1.1
) (and hence of the scaled ILW (
1.5
)) in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
s\geq\frac{1}{2}
.
In particular,
ILW (and the scaled ILW) is deterministically globally well-posedness
on the support of
the generalized Gibbs measure
\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

(and
\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
, respectively),
provided that
k\geq 3
.
See Remark
1.13
.

Let us briefly discuss the strategy for proving
Theorems
1.9
and
1.10
.
We focus on the deep-water regime.
Consider the following truncated ILW dynamics:

(1.43)

\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}u=\mathbf{P}_{N}\partial_{x}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)^{2}.

As observed in the previous works
[
118
,
77
,
114
,
115
]
,
the main difficulty comes from the non-conservation of

E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)
under the truncated dynamics:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\Phi_{N}(t)(u_{0}))\neq 0,\qquad k\geq 2,

where
\Phi_{N}(t)=\Phi_{N}(t;\delta)
denotes the solution map for the truncated ILW (
1.43
),
sending
u_{0}
to a solution
u(t)=\Phi_{N}(t)(u_{0})
at time
t
.
This in turn
implies
non-invariance of the truncated measure

\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}

defined in (
1.30
).

In order to overcome this difficulty,
we follow the strategy introduced in

[
114
,
115
]

and prove almost invariance of the
truncated measure

\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}

under the truncated dynamics (
1.43
);
see Proposition
8.4
,
where we need to take a time derivative of
the truncated density
F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\Phi_{N}(t)(u))
,
appearing in (
1.30
).
At this point,
our construction of the truncated generalized Gibbs measure

\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}

only with an
L^{2}
-cutoff
leads to a simplification;
see Remark
1.11
.
Then, as in
[
114
,
115
]
,
the crucial ingredient is the following
probabilistic asymptotic conservation of

E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\Phi_{N}(t)(u))
at time
t=0
:

(1.44)

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\bigg\|\frac{d}{dt}E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\Phi_{N}(t)(u))\Big|_{t=0}\bigg\|_{L^{p}(d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})}=0

for any finite
p\geq 1
;
see Proposition
7.1
.
Thanks to our
detailed analysis
on the structure
of the deep-water conservation laws
(see Subsection
3.4
)
with
a perturbative viewpoint,
(
1.44
)
follows from a straightforward adaptation
of the argument in
[
113
,
114
,
115
]
.
We, however, point out that some care is needed
in the shallow-water regime, since
the rate of convergence (as
N\to\infty
)
for the shallow-water probabilistic asymptotic conservation
for
0<\delta<\infty

(Proposition
7.3
)
diverges as
\delta\to 0
;
see Remark
7.2
and Subsection
7.3
.

Once we establish (
1.44
),
the desired invariance (Theorem
1.9
(i)) follows
from (
1.44
) and a PDE approximation argument
(Lemma
8.5
).
The deep-water convergence
in Theorem
1.9
(ii)
then
follows from the (weak) convergence of the generalized Gibbs measures
(Theorem
1.6
), the
Skorokhod
representation theorem (Lemma
2.10
),
and the deterministic deep-water convergence result
on the ILW dynamics
by the second author
[
63
]
.
As a consequence of the
unconditional well-posedness by Molinet and Vento
[
74
]

(including the case of the limiting BO equation),
the deep-water convergence holds (pathwise) unconditionally,
namely, in the entire class
C(\mathbb{R};H^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}))

without any auxiliary function space.
A similar comment holds in the shallow-water limit.

Before we conclude this introduction by stating several remarks,
let us emphasize that the main novelties of this paper
lie in

•

the construction of appropriate conservation laws
of the (scaled) ILW
and their deep-water and shallow-water convergence
presented in Subsection
1.2
, and

•

the construction of the generalized Gibbs measures
associated with these (scaled) ILW conservation laws
and their deep-water and shallow-water convergence
presented in Subsection
1.3
,

with a particular emphasis on the shallow-water convergence,
where we establish a 2-to-1 collapse in each setting.
We note that
the dynamical results (Theorems
1.9
and
1.10
)
follow from a straightforward
combination of the strategy in
[
114
,
115
]

with Theorems
1.6

and
1.7
.

Remark 1.11
.

In proving almost invariance of the
truncated generalized Gibbs measure

\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}
,
we just need to study the time derivative of

E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\Phi_{N}(t)(u))

thanks to the conservation of the
L^{2}
-norm under the
truncated dynamics (
1.43
)
and our simplified construction of the generalized Gibbs measures
only with an
L^{2}
-cutoff.
On the other hand,
in
[
114
,
115
,
28
]
,
it was necessary to estimate the time derivatives
of (smooth) cutoff functions on the lower order conservation laws;
see, for example, the proof of Proposition 5.4 in
[
114
]
.
We point out that, in
[
114
,
115
]
,
it was crucial to use
smooth
cutoff functions
on the lower order conservation laws precisely for this reason,
but that
our main results on the measure construction,
invariance, and convergence
also hold even if we replace the smooth cutoff function
\eta_{K}\big(\|u\|_{L^{2}}\big)

by a sharp
L^{2}
-cutoff
\mathbf{1}_{\{\|u\|_{L^{2}}\leq K\}}

as in
[
62
,
12
,
90
]
.
See also Remarks
5.12
,

6.12
,
and
8.7
.

Remark 1.12
.

Theorem
1.10
(ii) establishes
invariance of the generalized Gibbs measure
associated with the
H^{2}
-conservation law
for KdV, which is missing in the literature.

Remark 1.13
.

(i)
In the current paper, we restrict our attention to
k\geq 3

in studying the dynamical problems.
This is due to the fact that we rely on the deterministic
global well-posedness of the (scaled) ILW
in
H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T})
by Molinet and Vento
[
74
]
.
In a recent work
[
19
]
with Pilod,
we in fact proved
global well-posedness
of the (scaled) ILW in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})

by viewing ILW as a perturbation of BO
and applying the gauge transform for BO.
Thus, in order to handle the
k=2
case,
using the deterministic global well-posedness in
[
19
]
,
we need to adapt the argument in
[
28
]

to the ILW setting.
We plan to address this issue
together with the
k=1
case
in a forthcoming work
by combining the ideas from

[
28
,
27
]
and
[
19
,
15
]
.

(ii) The
k=0
case corresponds to the white noise in (
1.23
).
It is known that the white noise is
invariant under the KdV dynamics;
see
[
98
,
80
,
81
,
82
,
87
,
51
]
.
On the other hand,
despite
the
recent breakthrough results
by
Gérard, Kappeler, and Topalov

[
35
]

and by
Killip, Laurens, and Vişan
[
50
]

on deterministic well-posedness of BO in
the entire subcritical regime

H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
s>-\frac{1}{2}
,
the question of invariance of the white noise for BO (and ILW)
remains a challenging open problem.

Remark 1.14
.

After the appearance of the first version of this paper,
there have been intensive research activities on the ILW equation;
see
[
32
,
46
,
20
,
17
]
.

In
[
32
]
,
Gassot and Laurens
studied well-posedness of ILW (
1.1
)
in negative Sobolev spaces on
\mathbb{T}
.
By combining
the perturbative viewpoint (
1.15
)
following
[
47
,
19
,
15
,
31
]
,
the Birkhoff map for BO
[
35
]
,
and the quantity

\beta(\kappa;u)

(related to the perturbation determinant)
defined in

[
50
, Proposition 4.3]
,
for given
0<\delta<\infty
,
they proved that ILW (
1.1
)
is globally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
s>-\frac{1}{2}
,
and that deep-water convergence
holds in the same regularity range.
Note that these results in
[
32
]

hold only in the sense of
sensible weak solutions as defined in

[
94
,
95
,
3
]
.
Since the approach in
[
32
]
relies on
the work
[
35
]
for the periodic setting,
the corresponding results
in negative Sobolev spaces on the real line are still open at this point.

In
[
46
]
,
Harrop-Griffiths, Killip, and Vişan,
made a
substantial progress in understanding the complete integrability structure
of ILW (
1.1
)
in terms of the Lax pair structure and the perturbation determinant,
and developed a

non-perturbative
approach
for
both
the deep-water and shallow-water regimes
on
both
geometries (namely, on the torus and the line).
In particular,
they established
uniform-in-time a priori
H^{s}
bounds for solutions to the (scaled) ILW
and their equicontinuity properties;
see
[
46
]
for further details.

In
[
20
]
,
the authors with T. Zhao
established shallow-water convergence
of the scaled ILW (
1.5
)
to KdV (
1.6
) in
L^{2}
on both geometries.
Their approach was based on combining
the complete integrability approach as in
[
46
]

and an infinite iteration of normal form reductions,
introduced and developed in
[
40
,
60
,
95
]
,
which was completely new in the literature.

In
[
17
]
,
the authors with B. Harrop-Griffiths
investigated
regularity properties of the solution map
for the scaled ILW (
1.5
)
on the real line.
By decomposing the dynamics into
the low frequency part and the residual part,
they showed that, when the depth parameter is sufficiently small,
the solution map for the low frequency part
is analytic in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
while the
solution map for the residual part fails to be
C^{2}
.
Moreover, they established
shallow-water convergence in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

of the low frequency dynamics
to KdV.
This explained
how this regularity gain
of the solution map
propagates in frequency,
namely, from the low frequency dynamics
supported on
\{|\xi|\leq\delta^{-1}\}

to KdV,
which
illustrates another aspect of the singular nature
of the
shallow-water limit.

Remark 1.15
.

In a recent preprint
[
21
]
,
the second and third authors with Chouk, Gubinelli, and J. Li
studied well-posedness issues of the following
modulated
ILW equation
(
0<\delta\leq\infty
):

(1.45)

\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}u\cdot\partial_{t}w=\partial_{x}(u^{2})

and the modulated scaled ILW equation
(
0\leq\delta<\infty
):

(1.46)

\partial_{t}v-\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}v\cdot\partial_{t}w=\partial_{x}(v^{2}),

where
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}

are as in
(
1.2
) and (
1.5
).
Here,
w:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
is an arbitrary continuous function of time,
called a
modulation
,
satisfying a certain irregularity condition;
see
[
21
, Definition 1.1]

for the definition of irregularity in this context.
When
\delta=\infty
,
(
1.45
) corresponds to
the modulated BO:

(1.47)

\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}^{2}u\cdot\partial_{t}w=\partial_{x}(u^{2}),

while, when
\delta=0
,
(
1.46
) corresponds to
the modulated KdV:

(1.48)

\partial_{t}v+\partial_{x}^{3}v\cdot\partial_{t}w=\partial_{x}(v^{2}).

It was shown in
[
21
]
that,
given
any

s\in\mathbb{R}
,
the following statements hold, provided that
the modulation
w
is sufficiently irregular:

(i)

For each
0<\delta\leq\infty
,
the modulated ILW (
1.45
)
is locally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
.
For each
0\leq\delta<\infty
,
the modulated scaled ILW (
1.46
)
is locally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
.

(ii)

(deep-water convergence).
Given
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
let
u^{\delta}
,
0<\delta<\infty
, and
u
denote the local-in-time solutions
to the modulated ILW equation (
1.45
)
and the modulated BO (
1.47
)
with
u^{\delta}|_{t=0}=u|_{t=0}=u_{0}
, respectively.
Then, as
\delta\to\infty
,

u^{\delta}
converges to
u
in

C([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
,
where
\tau\in(0,T]
denotes the local existence time of the modulated BO
(
1.47
).

(iii)

(shallow-water convergence).
Given
v_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
let
v^{\delta}
,
0<\delta<\infty
, and
v
denote the local-in-time solutions
to the modulated scaled ILW equation (
1.43
)
and the modulated KdV
(
1.48
)
with
v^{\delta}|_{t=0}=v|_{t=0}=v_{0}
, respectively.
Then, as
\delta\to 0
,

v^{\delta}
converges to
v
in

C([0,\tau];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
,
where
\tau\in(0,T]
denotes the local existence time of the modulated KdV
(
1.48
).

Thanks to the modulation
w
,
these results significantly improve
the corresponding results in the unmodulated case
(provided that
the modulation
w
is sufficiently irregular),
thus exhibiting new examples of

regularization by noise
;
see
[
21
]
for a further discussion
on regularization by noise.
See also
[
16
,
41
]

for further results on the modulated ILW (
1.45
).

1.5.
Organization of the paper

In Section
2
, we introduce notations and go over
deterministic and stochastic preliminary tools.
Sections
3
and
4

are devoted to the construction of
the deep-water and shallow-water conservation laws
in (
1.7
) and (
1.16
), respectively,
where we present proofs of Theorems
1.3
and
1.4

and provide more detailed structures of
these conservation laws which play an important role in later sections.
This part occupies the major part of the paper.
In Sections
5
and

6
, we go over the construction
of the generalized Gibbs measures, thus providing proofs of Theorems
1.6
and
1.7
.
After establishing the probabilistic asymptotic conservation
in Section
7
, we establish invariance of the generalized Gibbs measures
and the claimed dynamical convergence in Theorems

1.9
and
1.10

in Section
8
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notations and function spaces

We start by introducing some notations. Let
A\lesssim B
denote an estimate of the form
A\leq CB
for some constant
C>0
. We write
A\sim B
if
A\lesssim B
and
B\lesssim A
, while
A\ll B
will denote
A\leq cB
, for some small constant
c>0
.
We may write
\lesssim_{q}
and
\sim_{q}
to
emphasize the dependence of the implicit constant on an external parameter
q
.
We will use the shorthand notation

(2.1)

\displaystyle n_{1\cdots k}:=n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}.

We set

\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}:=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
,

\mathbb{Z}^{*}:=\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
,
and
we let
\lceil x\rceil
denote
the smallest integer greater than or equal to
x
.
We use
C>0
to denote various constants, which may vary line by line,
and we may use subscripts to signify dependence on external parameters.
We use
\varepsilon>0
to denote an arbitrarily small constant.
Various constants depend on
\varepsilon
but,
for simplicity of notation, we suppress such
\varepsilon
-dependence.

Throughout this paper, we fix a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
. The realization
\omega\in\Omega
is often omitted in writing. We will use
\Law(X)
to denote the law of a random variable
X
.

Our convention for the Fourier transform is as follows:

\displaystyle\widehat{f}(n)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(x)e_{-n}dx\quad\text{and}\quad f(x)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(f)(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\widehat{f}(n)e_{n}(x)

for
f
on
\mathbb{T}
, where
e_{n}(x)=e^{inx}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}|\widehat{f}(n)|^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{fg}(n)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}\\
n=n_{1}+n_{2}\end{subarray}}\widehat{f}(n_{1})\widehat{g}(n_{2}).

Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
\mathbf{P}_{N}
the Dirichlet projector onto spatial frequencies
\{|n|\leq N\}
defined as follows:

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{N}f(x):=(D_{N}\ast f)(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{0<|n|\leq N}\widehat{f}(n)e^{inx},

where
D_{N}(x)=\sum\limits_{|n|\leq N}e^{inx}
is the Dirichlet kernel.
We set
\mathbf{P}_{>N}=\textup{Id}-\mathbf{P}_{N}
.

Given
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
1\leq p\leq\infty
,
we define the
L^{p}
-based Sobolev space
W^{s,p}(\mathbb{T})
via the norm:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{W^{s,p}}:=\|\langle\partial_{x}\rangle^{s}f\|_{L^{p}}=\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\langle n\rangle^{s}\widehat{f}(n))\|_{L^{p}_{x}},

where

\langle\partial_{x}\rangle=(1-\partial_{x}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}

and
\langle n\rangle=(1+n^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
When
p=2
, we set
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})=W^{s,2}(\mathbb{T})
.

Let
\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}
denote the projection onto non-zero frequencies.
Then, we set

L^{r}_{0}(\mathbb{T})=\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}L^{r}(\mathbb{T})
,

W^{s,r}_{0}(\mathbb{T})=\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}W^{s,r}(\mathbb{T})
,
and
H^{s}_{0}(\mathbb{T})=\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
.
Namely, they are the subspaces of
L^{r}(\mathbb{T})
,
W^{s,r}(\mathbb{T})
,
and
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
, consisting of mean-zero functions, respectively.

We will often use the short-hand notations
such as
L^{q}_{T}H^{s}_{x}
and
L^{p}_{\omega}H^{s}_{x}
for
L^{q}\big([-T,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{T})\big)
and
L^{p}(\Omega;H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
, respectively.

Lemma 2.1
.

(i) (interpolation).
Let
s,s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}

such that
0\leq s_{0}\leq s
.
Then, we have

(2.3)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{H^{s_{0}}}

\displaystyle\leq\|u\|^{\frac{s-s_{0}}{s}}_{L^{2}}\|u\|_{H^{s}}^{\frac{s_{0}}{s}}.

(ii) (fractional Leibniz rule).

Let
s>0
and
1<p_{j},q_{j},r\leq\infty
such that
\frac{1}{p_{j}}+\frac{1}{q_{j}}=\frac{1}{r}
,
j=1,2
.
Then,
we have

(2.4)

\|\langle\partial_{x}\rangle^{s}(fg)\|_{L^{r}}\lesssim\|\langle\partial_{x}\rangle^{s}f\|_{L^{p_{1}}}\|g\|_{L^{q_{1}}}+\|f\|_{L^{p_{2}}}\|\langle\partial_{x}\rangle^{s}g\|_{L^{q_{2}}}.

See
[
6
]
for the fractional Leibniz rule on the torus; see also

[
40
, Lemma 3.4 (i)]
.

2.2.
On the multiplier operators

In this paper, we work with various multiplier operators
in both the deep-water and shallow-water regimes.
In this subsection,
we state several lemmas on key multipliers.

Given
0<\delta<\infty
,
let
\mathfrak{K}_{\delta}(n)
be the multiplier of
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}

given by

(2.5)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{\delta}(n)

\displaystyle=in\widehat{\mathcal{G}_{\delta}}(n)=n\coth(\delta n)-\delta^{-1},\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*},

where
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(n)
are as in (
1.2
).
When
\delta=\infty
, we set

\mathfrak{K}_{\infty}(n)=|n|
.
This multiplier plays an important role
in the deep-water analysis.
We recall the following lemma on the basic properties
of
\mathfrak{K}_{\delta}(n)
; see Lemma 2.1 in
[
64
]
for a proof.

Lemma 2.2
.

Given
0<\delta\leq\infty
,
let
\mathfrak{K}_{\delta}(n)
be as in (
2.5
).
Then, we have

\displaystyle\max\Big(0,|n|-\frac{1}{\delta}\Big)<\mathfrak{K}_{\delta}(n)=\mathfrak{K}_{\delta}(-n)<|n|=\mathfrak{K}_{\infty}(n),

for any
0<\delta\leq\infty
and
