n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.
In particular, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{\delta}(n)\sim_{\delta}|n|

for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
, where the implicit constant is independent of

2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
Furthermore, for
each fixed
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
,
\mathfrak{K}_{\delta}(n)
is strictly increasing in
\delta\geq 1
and converges to
|n|
as
\delta\to\infty
.

Given
0<\delta<\infty
,
let
\mathfrak{L}_{\delta}(n)
be the multiplier of
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\partial_{x}

given by

(2.6)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{\delta}(n)=in\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}}(n)=\delta^{-1}\mathfrak{K}_{\delta}(n)=\delta^{-1}(n\coth(\delta n)-\delta^{-1}),

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}

and
\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}_{\delta}(n)
are as in (
1.5
)
and (
1.35
), respectively.
When
\delta=0
, we set

\mathfrak{L}_{0}(n)=\frac{1}{3}n^{2}
.
This multiplier plays an important role
in the shallow-water analysis.
See (the proof of)
[
64
, Lemma 2.3]
for a proof
of the following lemma.
(Some of the bounds also follow
from
Lemma
2.2

with
\mathfrak{L}_{\delta}(n)=\delta^{-1}\mathfrak{K}_{\delta}(n)
.)

Lemma 2.3
.

The following statements hold.

(i)

0<\mathfrak{L}_{\delta}(n)<\min(\frac{1}{3}n^{2},\frac{1}{\delta}|n|)
for
any
0<\delta<\infty
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.

(ii)

We have

(2.7)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{\delta}(n)=2n^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k^{2}\pi^{2}+\delta^{2}n^{2}}.

In particular,
for each fixed
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
,

\mathfrak{L}_{\delta}(n)
is decreasing in
\delta
,
and
\lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathfrak{L}_{\delta}(n)=\frac{1}{3}n^{2}=\mathfrak{L}_{0}(n)
.

(iii)

For any
0<\delta<\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{\delta}(n)\gtrsim\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\delta}|n|,&\text{if }\delta|n|\gg 1,\\
n^{2},&\text{if }\delta|n|\lesssim 1.\end{cases}

(iv)

Given
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
, define
\mathfrak{h}(\delta,n)
by
setting
n^{2}\mathfrak{h}(\delta,n)=n^{2}-3\mathfrak{L}_{\delta}(n)
.
Then,
we have

(2.8)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}(\delta,n)=6\delta^{2}n^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k^{2}\pi^{2}(k^{2}\pi^{2}+\delta^{2}n^{2})}\in(0,1],

(2.9)

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{h}^{2}(\delta,n)=\infty,\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathfrak{h}(\delta,n)=0

for any
0<\delta<\infty
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.

We also state basic bounds on the perturbation operator
\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}

in (
1.14
) and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
in (
1.5
).

Lemma 2.4
.

The following bounds hold.

(i)

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}}(n)
be the multiplier of the perturbation operator

\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}=\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}-\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}

defined in (
1.14
).
Then, we have

(2.10)

\displaystyle|\widehat{\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}}(n)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\delta}

for any
0<\delta<\infty
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.

(ii)

Let
\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}_{\delta}(n)
be as in (
1.35
).
Then, we have

(2.11)

\displaystyle|\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}}(n)|\leq\min\Big(\frac{1}{\delta},\frac{1}{3}|n|\Big)

for any
0<\delta<\infty
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.

(iii)

Let
1<r<\infty
.
Then,
given
0<\delta\leq\infty
,
the Hilbert transform
\mathcal{H}

and the perturbation operator
\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
in (
1.14
)
are bounded on
L^{r}_{0}(\mathbb{T})=\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}L^{r}(\mathbb{T})
, consisting
of mean-zero functions in
L^{r}(\mathbb{T})
,
satisfying
the bounds
:

(2.12)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{H}\|_{L^{r}_{0}\to L^{r}_{0}},

\displaystyle\leq C_{r}<\infty,

(2.13)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}\|_{L^{r}_{0}\to L^{r}_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{r}\delta^{-1}<\infty.

Moreover, given
0<\delta\leq\infty
,
the operator
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}=\delta\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
in (
1.2
)
and (
1.5
)
is bounded on
L^{r}_{0}(\mathbb{T})

with
the bound
:

(2.14)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\|_{L^{r}_{0}\to L^{r}_{0}}=\|\delta\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\|_{L^{r}_{0}\to L^{r}_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{r}<\infty

and we also have

(2.15)

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\|_{W^{1,r}_{0}\to L^{r}_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{r}<\infty,

where

W^{1,r}_{0}(\mathbb{T})=\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}W^{1,r}(\mathbb{T})
,
consisting of mean-zero functions in
W^{1,r}(\mathbb{T})
.
In (
2.12
), (
2.13
), (
2.14
), and (
2.15
), the constant

C_{r}
is independent of
0<\delta\leq\infty
.

Remark 2.5
.

From the series representation (
2.17
) of

\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(n)=\delta\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}_{\delta}(n)
below,
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}|\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(n)|=\lim_{\delta\to 0}|\delta\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}_{\delta}(n)|\lesssim\lim_{\delta\to 0}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\delta|n|}{k^{2}\pi^{2}}\sim\lim_{\delta\to 0}\delta|n|=0

for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.

Proof of Lemma
2.4
.

For (
2.10
), see the proof of Lemma 2.3 in
[
19
]
,
while (
2.11
) follows from Lemma
2.3
(i).

We now turn to (iii).
Let

\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};[0,1])
such that

\varphi(\xi)=0
for
|\xi|\leq\frac{1}{2}
and

\varphi(\xi)=1
for
|\xi|\geq 1
.
Define a function
m_{1}(\xi)
on
\mathbb{R}
by setting

m_{1}(\xi)=\varphi(\xi)(-i\textup{sgn}(\xi))
.
It is easy to check that

|\partial_{\xi}^{k}m_{1}(\xi)|\lesssim_{k}|\xi|^{-k}

for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then,
by the Mihlin multiplier theorem on
\mathbb{R}
(see
[
38
, Theorem 6.2.7 and (6.2.14)]
),
the Fourier multiplier operator
T_{m_{1}}

with multiplier
m_{1}
is bounded on
L^{r}(\mathbb{R})
,
1<r<\infty
.
Noting that
m_{1}(n)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
agrees with the multiplier for the Hilbert transform
\mathcal{H}
on
\mathbb{T}
,
boundedness of
\mathcal{H}
on
L^{r}_{0}(\mathbb{T})

with the bound (
2.12
)
follows from
the transference
(
[
38
, Theorem 4.3.7]
),
since

m_{1}(\xi)
is continuous and thus is regulated everywhere (in the
sense of
[
38
, Definition 4.3.6]
).

From (
1.14
), we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{\delta}(n)=n\big(\coth(\delta n)-\textup{sgn}(n)\big)-\delta^{-1}=\frac{2|n|}{e^{2\delta|n|}-1}-\delta^{-1}

for
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.
Let
m_{2}(\xi)=\varphi(\xi)\big(\frac{2|\xi|}{e^{2\delta|\xi|}-1}-\delta^{-1}\big)
.
We easily see that
|\partial_{\xi}^{k}m_{2}(\xi)|\lesssim_{k}\delta^{-1}|\xi|^{-k}

for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
.
Hence, the bound (
2.13
) follows from
the Mihlin multiplier theorem on
\mathbb{R}

and the transference as discussed above.

From (
1.2
), we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(n)=-i\bigg(1+\frac{2}{e^{2\delta n}-1}-\frac{1}{\delta n}\bigg).

By setting
m_{3}(\xi)=-i\varphi(\xi)\big(1+\frac{2}{e^{2\delta\xi}-1}-\frac{1}{\delta\xi}\big)
,
it follows from the Mihlin multiplier theorem on
\mathbb{R}

and the transference as discussed above that

(2.16)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\|_{L^{r}_{0}\to L^{r}_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{r,\delta}^{(1)}<\infty,

where the constant

C_{r,\delta}^{(1)}
is independent of
1\lesssim\delta\leq\infty
.

In order to prove uniform boundedness
of
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
for small
\delta
,
we need to use a series representation.
From (
2.6
) and (
2.7
) in Lemma
2.3
,
we have

(2.17)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(n)=\delta\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}_{\delta}(n)=\frac{\delta\mathfrak{L}_{\delta}(n)}{in}

\displaystyle=-2i\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\delta n}{k^{2}\pi^{2}+\delta^{2}n^{2}}.

Let
m_{4}(\xi)=-2i\varphi(\xi)\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\delta\xi}{k^{2}\pi^{2}+\delta^{2}\xi^{2}}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle|m_{4}(\xi)|=2\varphi(\xi)\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\pi^{2}(\frac{k}{\delta|\xi|})^{2}+1}\frac{1}{\delta|\xi|}\leq 2\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dx}{\pi^{2}x^{2}+1}\lesssim 1

for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
.
Similarly, a direct computation shows that

|\partial_{\xi}^{k}m_{4}(\xi)|\lesssim_{\delta,k}|\xi|^{-k}

for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
,
where the implicit constant is independent of
0<\delta\lesssim 1
.
Hence, from
the Mihlin multiplier theorem on
\mathbb{R}

and the transference as discussed above,
we obtain

(2.18)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\|_{L^{r}_{0}\to L^{r}_{0}}=\|\delta\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\|_{L^{r}_{0}\to L^{r}_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{r,\delta}^{(2)}<\infty,

where the constant

C_{r,\delta}^{(2)}
is independent of
0<\delta\lesssim 1
.
Hence, the bound (
2.14
) follows from (
2.16
)
and (
2.18
).

Lastly, the bound (
2.15
) follows from a similar discussion as above,
using the series representation (
2.17
), written as

\displaystyle(in)^{-1}\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}}_{\delta}(n)=-2\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k^{2}\pi^{2}+\delta^{2}n^{2}}.

This concludes the proof of Lemma
2.4
.
∎

Lastly, we recall several identities.
First, recall Cotlar’s identity
[
24
]
:

(2.19)

\displaystyle 2\mathcal{H}\big((\mathcal{H}u)u\big)=(\mathcal{H}u)^{2}-u^{2}

for a function
u
on
\mathbb{R}
.
From (
2.19
) and the “polarization”
2uv=(u+v)^{2}-u^{2}-v^{2}
,
we obtain the following identity:

(2.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}\big((\mathcal{H}u)v+u\mathcal{H}v\big)=(\mathcal{H}u)(\mathcal{H}v)-uv

for functions
u
and
v
on
\mathbb{R}
.
In the current periodic setting,
we instead have

(2.21)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}\big((\mathcal{H}u)v+u\mathcal{H}v\big)=\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}\big((\mathcal{H}u)(\mathcal{H}v)-uv\big)

for mean-zero functions
u
and
v

on
\mathbb{T}
,
since we have
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}
by our convention; see
Footnote
1
.
However, by noting
that
(\mathcal{H}u)(\mathcal{H}v)-uv
has mean zero on
\mathbb{T}

(recall that

\mathcal{H}
has a symbol

-i\textup{sgn}(n)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n\neq 0}
),
we see that (
2.21
) reduces to (
2.20
) even on
\mathbb{T}
,
provided that
u
and
v
have mean zero on
\mathbb{T}
.

The following lemma establishes an analogous but slightly different identity
for the Tilbert transform
\mathcal{T}_{\delta}
.

Lemma 2.6
.

Given
0<\delta<\infty
,
let
\mathcal{T}_{\delta}

be the Tilbert transform defined as the Fourier multiplier operator
with
multiplier

(2.22)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{T}_{\delta}}(n)=\begin{cases}-i\coth(\delta n),&n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*},\\
0,&n=0.\end{cases}

Then, we have

(2.23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\delta}=\mathcal{T}_{\delta}-\delta^{-1}\partial_{x}^{-1}\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}

and

(2.24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\delta}\big((\mathcal{T}_{\delta}u)v+u\mathcal{T}_{\delta}v\big)=\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}\big((\mathcal{T}_{\delta}u)(\mathcal{T}_{\delta}v)-uv\big)

for mean-zero functions
u
and
v

on
\mathbb{T}
,
where
\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}
denotes the projection onto non-zero frequencies.

Proof.

The identity (
2.23
) follows from (
1.2
)
and (
2.22
), while the identity (
2.24
)
follows from a direct computation. We omit details.
∎

2.3.
On discrete convolutions

Next, we recall the following basic lemma on a discrete convolution.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
satisfy

\alpha\geq\beta\geq 0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\quad\alpha+\beta>1.

Then, we have

\sum_{n=n_{1}+n_{2}}\frac{1}{\langle n_{1}\rangle^{\alpha}\langle n_{2}\rangle^{\beta}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\langle n\rangle^{\beta-\lambda}}

for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
where
\lambda=\max(1-\alpha,0)
when
\alpha\neq 1
and
\lambda=\varepsilon
when
\alpha=1
for any
\varepsilon>0
.

Lemma
2.7
follows
from elementary computations.
See, for example,

[
37
, Lemma 4.2]
and
[
75
, Lemma 4.1]
.

2.4.
Tools from stochastic analysis

We first recall the Wiener chaos estimate,
which is a consequence of the hypercontractivity
of the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck semigroup due to Nelson
[
78
]
.
See, for example,
[
105
, Theorem I.22]
.
See also
[
106
, Proposition 2.4]
.

Lemma 2.8

(Wiener chaos estimate)
.

Let
\mathbf{g}=\{g_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be an independent family of standard complex-valued Gaussian random variables satisfying
g_{-n}=\overline{g_{n}}
. Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\{Q_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of polynomials in
\mathbf{g}
of degree at most
k
. Then, for any finite
p\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\bigg\|\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}Q_{j}(\mathbf{g})\bigg\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq(p-1)^{\frac{k}{2}}\bigg\|\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}Q_{j}(\mathbf{g})\bigg\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

Next, we recall
Kakutani’s theorem
[
49
]
in the Gaussian
setting
(or the Feldman-Hájek theorem
[
30
,
45
]
;
see also
[
26
, Theorem 2.9]
).
For the following version of Kakutani’s theorem,
see Lemma 3.2 in
[
64
]
.

Lemma 2.9

(Kakutani’s theorem)
.

Let
\{A_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
and
\{B_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
be two sequences of independent, real-valued, mean-zero Gaussian random variables with
\mathbb{E}[A^{2}_{n}]=a_{n}>0
and
\mathbb{E}[B_{n}^{2}]=b_{n}>0
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
. Then, the laws of the sequences
\{A_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
and
\{B_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
are equivalent if and only if

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\bigg(\frac{a_{n}}{b_{n}}-1\bigg)^{2}<\infty.

It they are not equivalent, then they are singular.

We now state the Skorokhod representation
theorem along a continuous parameter.
While
[
8
, Theorem 6.7]

is stated for convergence along a discrete parameter,
a straightforward modification (in particular, in

[
8
, line 2 on p. 71]
)
yields the following lemma.
See also
[
86
]

for an application of
the Skorokhod representation
theorem along a continuous parameter.

Lemma 2.10

(Skorokhod representation theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a complete
separable metric space
(
i.e. a Polish space
)
.
Fix

a,b\in\mathbb{R}
with
a<b
.
Suppose that
probability measures

\{\rho_{\delta}\}_{\delta\in[a,b)}

on
\mathcal{M}
converge weakly to a probability measure
\rho

as
\delta\to b
.
Then, there exist a probability space
(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathbb{P}})

and random variables
X_{\delta},X:\widetilde{\Omega}\to\mathcal{M}

such that

\displaystyle\Law(X_{\delta})=\rho_{\delta}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Law(X)=\rho,

and
X_{\delta}
converges
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely to
X
as
\delta\to b
.

Lastly, we recall the notion
of the total variation distance and the
Kullback-Leibler divergence (= relative entropy).
See Subsection 2.3 in
[
64
]

for a further discussion on
various modes of convergence
for probability measures
and random variables;
see also

[
97
, Chapter 3]
,

[
108
, Chapter 2]
,
and

[
29
]
.

Definition 2.11
.

(i)
Given two
probability measures

\mu
and
\nu

on a measurable space
(E,\mathcal{E})
,
the total variation distance

d_{\rm TV}
of

\mu
and
\nu
is given by

(2.25)

\displaystyle d_{\rm TV}(\mu,\nu):=\sup\big\{|\mu(A)-\nu(A)|:A\in\mathcal{E}\big\}.

This metric induces a much stronger topology than the one induced by the weak convergence;
see
[
64
]
for such an example.

(ii)
Given two probability measures
\mu
and
\nu
,
the Kullback-Leibler divergence
d_{\rm KL}(\mu,\nu)
between
\mu
and
\nu

is defined by

(2.26)

\displaystyle d_{\rm KL}(\mu,\nu)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\int_{E}\log\frac{d\mu}{d\nu}d\mu,&\text{if }\mu\ll\nu,\\
\infty,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

which is nothing but the relative entropy of
\mu
with respect to
\nu
.

While the total variation distance
is a metric, the Kullback-Leibler divergence is not a metric.
For example,
d_{\rm KL}(\,\cdot,\cdot\,)
is not symmetric, and moreover,
the symmetrized version
d_{\rm KL}(\mu,\nu)+d_{\rm KL}(\mu,\nu)
is not a metric,
either.
If
\mu
and
\nu
are product measures
of the form
\mu=\bigotimes_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mu_{n}

and
\nu=\bigotimes_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\nu_{n}
, then we have

(2.27)

\displaystyle d_{\rm KL}(\mu,\nu)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}d_{\rm KL}(\mu_{n},\nu_{n}).

Lastly, we recall
Pinsker’s inequality;
see Lemma 2.5 in
[
108
]

for a proof.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
d_{\rm TV}
and
d_{\rm KL}
be as in Definition
2.11
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle d_{\rm TV}(\mu,\nu)\leq\frac{\sqrt{d_{\rm KL}(\mu,\nu)}}{\sqrt{2}}.

In particular, convergence in
the Kullback-Leibler divergence
implies convergence in total variation.

3.
Deep-water conservation laws

In this section, we present a proof of
Theorem
1.3
.
Namely,
we construct conservation laws in the deep-water regime
and establish their
deep-water convergence; see Proposition
3.8
.
Moreover, we need to ensure that these deep-water conservation laws
are suitable for constructing the associated generalized Gibbs measures
along with their
deep-water convergence
(Theorem
1.6
)
whose details are presented in
Section
5
.

In Subsection
3.1
,
we
start with the Bäcklund transform (
3.1
)
for ILW
and derive a recursive formulation for microscopic conservation laws
\chi_{n}
;
see (
3.4
).
In Subsection
3.2
,
we then study the structure
of the microscopic conservation laws
\chi_{n}
.
In particular,
we introduce the notion of the
deep-water rank
of a monomial
(Definition
3.3
)
and also carry out detailed analysis on
the linear and quadratic parts of the microscopic conservation laws
(Lemmas
3.5
and
3.6
).
In Subsection
3.3
,
we define deep-water conservation laws
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

and, by exploiting a perturbative viewpoint (see (
3.33
)),
we establish
convergence of the
deep-water conservation laws
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

to the corresponding conservation laws for the BO equation (
1.3
)
(Proposition
3.8
).
We then take a closer look at
the cubic terms in
u

and determine their structures
(Lemmas
3.9
and
3.10
).
This analysis on the cubic terms plays an important role in
the construction of the associated generalized Gibbs measures;
see Section
5
for further details. Finally, in Subsection
3.4
,
by putting everything together,
we state the final structure
of the deep-water conservation laws
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
.

In this and the next sections, we often suppress the dependence
on the depth parameter
0<\delta<\infty

for simplicity of notation
and, unless otherwise stated, we assume that all the functions under consideration
are smooth.

3.1.
Bäcklund transforms and microscopic conservation laws

We first recall
the Bäcklund transform for ILW (
1.1
)
derived in
[
102
]
(modulo constants);
see also
[
53
,
61
,
66
]
.
Let
0<\delta<\infty
and
\mu>0
.
Given a (smooth) solution
u
to ILW (
1.1
),
the Bäcklund transform yields a function
U=U(u;\mu)

such that the pair
(u,U)

satisfies the following system:
15
15

15

The derivation in
[
102
]

is on the real line but it equally applies to the current
mean-zero periodic setting.

(3.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}2u&=\mu(e^{U}-1)+(\mathcal{G}_{\delta}-i)\partial_{x}U+\delta^{-1}U,\\
U_{t}&=\mu(e^{U}-1)U_{x}+\mathcal{G}_{\delta}U_{xx}+U_{x}\mathcal{G}_{\delta}U_{x}+\delta^{-1}UU_{x}.\end{split}

Indeed, given a smooth function
u
on
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
,
by assuming that
U
is of the form

(3.2)

\displaystyle U=\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\mu^{-n}\chi_{n},

where

\{\chi_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=\{\chi_{n}^{\delta}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

is a sequence
of smooth functions, independent of
\mu>0
,
we can use the first equation in (
3.1
)
to construct a smooth function
U=U(u;\mu)

as follows.
Define the operator
\mathcal{L}_{0}=\mathcal{L}_{0}(\delta)
by setting

(3.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}=(\mathcal{G}_{\delta}-i)\partial_{x}+\delta^{-1}.

Then,
by rewriting the first equation in
(
3.1
)
as

\displaystyle\mu U=2u-\mu\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}U^{n}-\mathcal{L}_{0}U,

substituting (
3.2
), and
comparing the powers of
\mu
,
we obtain the following recursive definition for
\chi_{n}=\chi^{\delta}_{n}
in terms of
u
:

(3.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\chi_{1}&=2u,\\
\chi_{n}&=-\sum_{j=2}^{n}\frac{1}{j!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\dots,n_{j}\in\mathbb{N}\\
n_{1\cdots j}=n\end{subarray}}\chi_{n_{1}}\cdots\chi_{n_{j}}-\mathcal{L}_{0}\chi_{n-1},\quad n\geq 2,\end{split}

where the notation
n_{1\cdots j}=n_{1}+\cdots+n_{j}
is as in (
2.1
).
Hence,
using (
3.2
) and (
3.4
),
we can construct
U
from
u
.

The following lemma on equivalence of ILW (
1.1
)
and its Bäcklund transform (
3.1
)
is well known but
seems to be
part of the folklore whose proof we could not find.
For readers’ convenience,
we present its proof,
where, in the converse direction,
we impose the additional assumption (
3.2
).

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
0<\delta<\infty
.
Given
\mu\geq 0
, let
u:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
and
U:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C}

be smooth functions, satisfying the
first equation in (
3.1
).
If

U
satisfies
the second equation in (
3.1
),
then

u=u(U)

satisfies ILW (
1.1
).
Conversely,
if
u
satisfies ILW (
1.1
)
and if, in addition,
U
is of the form (
3.2
),
then
U=U(u;\mu)
,
\mu>0
,
satisfies
the second equation in (
3.1
).

Remark 3.2
.

It is a priori not clear to us, if, given a smooth function
u
,
we can find
U
which satisfies the first equation in (
3.1
).
The extra assumption (
3.2
)
indeed guarantees existence of such
U

as explained above.

Proof of Lemma
3.1
.

From
the first equation in (
3.1
)
with Lemma
2.6

and the anti self-adjointness of
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}

(which in particular implies that
U_{x}\mathcal{G}_{\delta}U_{x}
has mean zero on
\mathbb{T}
),
a direct (but rather lengthy) computation yields

(3.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&2(\partial_{t}u-\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}u-2u\partial_{x}u)\\
&\quad=(\mu e^{U}+(\mathcal{G}_{\delta}-i)\partial_{x}+\delta^{-1})\big[U_{t}-\mu(e^{U}-1)U_{x}-\mathcal{G}_{\delta}U_{xx}-U_{x}\mathcal{G}_{\delta}U_{x}-\delta^{-1}UU_{x}\big].\end{split}

Hence, if
U
satisfies the second equation
in (
3.1
), then we see that
u
satisfies (
1.1
).

Next, we prove the converse direction.
Given a smooth function
U
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
,
define
\mathcal{L}^{U}=\mathcal{L}^{U}(\mu)
by

(3.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{U}=\mu e^{U}+(\mathcal{G}_{\delta}-i)\partial_{x}+\delta^{-1}=\mu e^{U}+\mathcal{L}_{0},

where
\mathcal{L}_{0}
is as in (
3.3
).
Suppose that
u
is a smooth solution to (
1.1
)
and that
U=U(u;\mu)
of the form (
3.2
) is defined by the first equation in (
3.1
)
(as explained above).
Then, from (
3.5
) and (
3.6
), we have

(3.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{U}F(U)=0,

where

F(U)=F(U;\mu)
denotes the expression in the brackets
on the right-hand side of (
3.5
).
Our goal is to show that
F(U)=0
.
In the following, we carry out analysis for fixed
t\in\mathbb{R}

but we suppress the
t
-dependence
for simplicity of notation.

From (
3.7
) with (
3.6
), we have

(3.8)

\displaystyle 0=\langle\mathcal{L}^{U}F,\mathcal{L}_{0}F\rangle_{L^{2}}=\langle\mu e^{U}F,\mathcal{L}_{0}F\rangle_{L^{2}}+\|\mathcal{L}_{0}F\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Then, from (
3.7
) with (
3.6
) and (
3.8
),
we obtain

(3.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&=\|\mathcal{L}^{U}F\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\mu^{2}\|e^{U}F\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+2\Re\langle\mu e^{U}F,\mathcal{L}_{0}F\rangle_{L^{2}}+\|\mathcal{L}_{0}F\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\\
&=\mu^{2}\|e^{U}F\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\|\mathcal{L}_{0}F\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.\end{split}

On the other hand,
from the assumption (
3.2
)
and (
3.5
)
(in particular, by expanding
e^{U}
in the Taylor series),
we have

(3.10)

\displaystyle F(U)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mu^{-n}f_{n}

for some smooth functions
f_{n}
, independent of
\mu>0
.
Expanding
U
and
F
through their series representations
(
3.2
) and (
3.10
), we have

(3.11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}e^{U}F&=\bigg(1+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j!}U^{j}\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mu^{-n}f_{n}\bigg)\\
&=\bigg(1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mu^{-n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{j!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\dots,n_{j}=1\\
n_{1\cdots j}=n\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\chi_{n_{1}}\cdots\chi_{n_{j}}\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mu^{-n}f_{n}\bigg)\\
&=\mu^{-1}f_{1}+\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\mu^{-n}\bigg(f_{n}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{12}=n\\
1\leq n_{1}\leq n-1\end{subarray}}f_{n_{1}}\widetilde{U}_{n_{2}}\bigg)\\
&=:\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mu^{-n}A_{n},\end{split}

where

(3.12)

\displaystyle\begin{split}A_{1}&=f_{1},\qquad A_{n}=f_{n}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{12}=n\\
1\leq n_{1}\leq n-1\end{subarray}}f_{n_{1}}\widetilde{U}_{n_{2}},\quad n\geq 2,\\
\widetilde{U}_{n}&=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{j!}\sum_{n_{1\cdots j}=n}\chi_{n_{1}}\cdots\chi_{n_{j}},\quad n\in\mathbb{N}.\end{split}

Then,
by substituting (
3.10
) and (
3.11
) in (
3.9
),
we see that the coefficient
of each power of
\mu
must be
0
.
From the coefficient of
\mu^{0}
with (
3.12
), we obtain

(3.13)

\displaystyle f_{1}=A_{1}=0.

From the coefficient of
\mu^{-1}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}A_{1}\overline{A_{2}}+A_{2}\overline{A_{1}}dx=0

which is satisfied in view of (
3.13
).
From the coefficient of
\mu^{-2}
, we have

(3.14)

\displaystyle 0=\int_{\mathbb{T}}A_{1}\overline{A_{3}}+|A_{2}|^{2}+A_{3}\overline{A_{1}}dx-\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\mathcal{L}_{0}f_{1}|^{2}\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{T}}|A_{2}|^{2}dx,

where we used (
3.13
) at the second equality.
Hence, from the definition of
A_{2}
in
(
3.12
) with (
3.13
) and (
3.14
),
we conclude that

(3.15)

\displaystyle f_{2}=A_{2}=0.

In view of (
3.13
) and (
3.15
),
we see that the coefficient of
\mu^{-3}
in (
3.9
) is
0
.

We now proceed by induction and show

f_{n}=0
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Given
n\geq 3
, assume that
f_{k}=0
for any
1\leq k\leq n-1
.
Then,
from (
3.12
),
we see that

(3.16)

\displaystyle A_{k}=0

for any
1\leq k\leq n-1
.
Then, from the coefficient of

\mu^{-2n+2}

in (
3.9
), we obtain

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\sum_{n_{12}=2n}\int_{\mathbb{T}}A_{n_{1}}\overline{A_{n_{2}}}dx-\sum_{n_{12}=2n-2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{L}_{0}f_{n_{1}}\overline{\mathcal{L}_{0}f_{n_{2}}}dx=\int_{\mathbb{T}}|A_{n}|^{2}dx,

where
we used the inductive hypothesis and (
3.16
) at the second equality.
Namely, we have
A_{n}=0

and thus, we obtain from (
3.12
)
and the inductive hypothesis that
f_{n}=0

for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Therefore,
from (
3.10
),
we conclude that
F(U)=0
.
Namely,
U

satisfies
the second equation in (
3.1
).
∎

Suppose that
U
satisfies (
3.1
).
Then,
from the second equation in (
3.1
),
we see that the mean of
U
is conserved.
16
16

16

In this paper, we impose
the mean-zero assumption on a solution
u

to ILW (
1.1
) (and BO (
1.3
) when
\delta=\infty
), but we do
not

impose such a condition on
U
which is constructed from
u
as in (
3.2
) and (
3.4
).
A similar comment applies to the shallow-water case.

Then, by assuming that
U

is of the form (
3.2
), we formally obtain

\displaystyle 0=\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{T}}Udx=\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\mu^{-n}\cdot\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{n}dx

for any
\mu>0
.
By comparing the coefficients of
\mu^{-n}

on both sides,
we then conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{n}dx=0.

Namely,
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\chi_{n}
is a microscopic conservation law for ILW (
1.1
).
In Subsection
3.3
,
we will construct deep-water conservation laws
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

by taking suitable linear combinations of
(the real part of)
\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{n}dx
;
see Proposition
3.8
.

Recall from
Lemma
2.2

that
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
converges to
\mathcal{H}
as
\delta\to\infty
.
Thus, in the deep-water limit (
\delta\to\infty
),
the Bäcklund transform (
3.1
) for ILW
reduces to that for BO (
1.3
):

(3.17)

\displaystyle\begin{split}2u&=\mu(e^{U}-1)+\mathcal{H}U_{x}-iU_{x},\\
U_{t}&=\mu(e^{U}-1)U_{x}+\mathcal{H}U_{xx}+U_{x}\mathcal{H}U_{x}.\end{split}

Note that
Lemma
3.1
also holds for
\delta=\infty

with the same proof.
By taking
\delta\to\infty
in (
3.4
) and (
3.3
),
we obtain the following recursive definition
for the BO microscopic conservation laws
\chi_{n}^{\text{\rm BO}}
:

(3.18)

\displaystyle\chi_{1}^{\text{\rm BO}}

\displaystyle=2u,

\displaystyle\chi_{n}^{\text{\rm BO}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j=2}^{n}\frac{1}{j!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\dots,n_{j}\in\mathbb{N}\\
n_{1\cdots j}=n\end{subarray}}\chi_{n_{1}}^{\text{\rm BO}}\cdots\chi_{n_{j}}^{\text{\rm BO}}-(\mathcal{H}-i)\partial_{x}\chi_{n-1}^{\text{\rm BO}},\quad n\geq 2.

3.2.
On the deep-water microscopic conservation laws

In this subsection,
we study the structure of the deep-water microscopic conservation laws
\chi_{n}
.
From the recursive formulation (
3.4
),
we see that
\chi_{n}
consists of monomial terms
in
u
with
\partial_{x}
and
\delta^{-1}
.
17
17

17

Also with
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
.
However, we do not need to keep track of the number of
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
in the following.

We first introduce the notion of the
deep-water rank
of a monomial
p(u)
,
which plays an important role
in studying the structure of the deep-water conservation laws
constructed
in the next subsection.
See
[
66
, p. 118]

for a similar concept
in a
simpler context of the BO equation.

Definition 3.3
.

Given a monomial
p(u)
,
define
\#u
,
\#\partial_{x}
, and
\#\delta^{-1}
by

\displaystyle\#u

\displaystyle=\#u(p(u))=\text{homogeneity of $p(u)$ in $u$},

\displaystyle\#\partial_{x}

\displaystyle=\#\partial_{x}(p(u))=\text{number of (explicit) appearance of $\partial_{x}$ in $p(u)$},

\displaystyle\#\delta^{-1}

\displaystyle=\#\delta^{-1}(p(u))=\text{number of (explicit) appearance of $\delta^{-1}$ in $p(u)$}.

We then define the
deep-water rank
of a monomial
p(u)
, denoted by
\ord(p)
, by setting

(3.19)

\displaystyle\ord(p)=\#u+\#\partial_{x}+\#\delta^{-1}.

For simplicity, we refer to the deep-water rank as the rank in the following.
18
18

18

In Definition
4.7
,
we also define the shallow-water rank, which we also refer to as the rank.
Since we never consider the deep-water and shallow-water regimes
at the same time, there is no confusion in using this convention.
The same comment applies to the notation
\ord(p)
introduced in (
3.19
)
and (
4.46
).

If all monomials in a given polynomial
p(u)
have the same rank, say
n
,
then we say that the polynomial
p(u)
(and its integral over
\mathbb{T}
) has rank
n
.

The following lemma characterizes the rank of
\chi_{n}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
0<\delta<\infty
. Given any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
the deep-water microscopic conservation law

\chi_{n}=\chi_{n}^{\delta}

defined in (
3.4
)
has rank
n
.

Proof.

We prove the claim by induction.
Since
\chi_{1}=2u
,
the claim holds when
n=1
.
Now, given
n\geq 2
, assume that the claim holds for
\chi_{k}

for any
1\leq k\leq n-1
.
Hence,
\chi_{k}
has rank
k

for any
1\leq k\leq n-1
.
Then, in view of Definition
3.3

with (
3.3
), we
see that
the monomials
\chi_{n_{1}}\cdots\chi_{n_{j}}
(with
n_{1}+\cdots+n_{j}=n
)
and
\mathcal{L}_{0}\chi_{n-1}
, appearing in (
3.4
),
all have rank
n
,
and thus the claim holds.
Therefore, by induction,
we conclude the proof of Lemma
3.4
.
∎

In the next subsection,
by taking a suitable linear combination of
\chi_{n}
,
1\leq n\leq k+2
,
we will construct deep-water conservation laws

E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

in the form (
1.7
),
where
all quadratic (in
u
) terms
have exactly
k
derivatives
with
positive
coefficients.
We point out that the positivity
of the coefficients is crucial in
constructing
the base Gaussian measures
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

in (
1.25
).
Thus, our main goal in this subsection
is to obtain
a precise
description
of the quadratic part of the conservation law
\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{n}dx
;
see Lemma
3.6
.
For this purpose, we first need to understand the structure
of the linear part of
\chi_{n}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
0<\delta<\infty
. Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
L_{n}=L_{n}^{\delta}
be the linear
(
in
u
)

part
of

\chi_{n}=\chi_{n}^{\delta}

defined in (
3.4
).
Then, we have

(3.20)

\displaystyle L_{n}=2(-\mathcal{L}_{0})^{n-1}u

for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
In particular, under the current mean-zero assumption on
u
, we have

(3.21)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}L_{n}dx=0

for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

When
n=1
, it follows from (
3.4
) that
L_{1}=2u
.
First, note that each monomial in

\chi_{n}
is at least linear in
u
.
Thus, the first term on the right-hand side of the second equation in (
3.4
)
is at least quadratic in
u
.
Hence, it follows from (
3.4
)
that

\displaystyle L_{n}=-\mathcal{L}_{0}L_{n-1}

for any
n\geq 2
,
which yields (
3.20
), since
L_{1}=2u
.
The second claim (
3.21
) follows
from (
3.20
) with (
3.3
)
and the mean-zero assumption on
u
.
∎

The following lemma
provides a description of
the quadratic part of the macroscopic conservation law
\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{n}dx

(whose quadratic part turns out to be real-valued).

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
0<\delta<\infty
. Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
Q_{n}=Q_{n}^{\delta}
be the quadratic

(
in
u
)

part
of
\chi_{n}=\chi_{n}^{\delta}

defined in (
3.4
).
Then, we have

Q_{1}=0
and

(3.22)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}Q_{n}dx

\displaystyle=2(-1)^{n+1}\sum_{m=0}^{n-2}\frac{1}{\delta^{n-2-m}}\binom{n}{m+2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\textup{even}\end{subarray}}^{m}\binom{m+1}{\ell+1}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{\frac{m-\ell}{2}}u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{m}{2}}}

for any
n\geq 2
,
where we used the convention (
1.9
).

Before proceeding to a proof of Lemma
3.6
,
we recall the following combinatorial formulas.
For non-negative integers
q,\ell,m,n
satisfying
n\geq q\geq 0
, we have

(3.23)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\binom{\ell-k}{m}\binom{q+k}{n}

\displaystyle=\binom{\ell+q+1}{m+n+1},

(3.24)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}\binom{k}{m}

\displaystyle=\binom{\ell+1}{m+1},

where it is understood that

(3.25)

\displaystyle\binom{k}{m}=0\quad\text{ for $k<m$}.

For (
3.23
), see

[
39
, (5.26) on p. 169]
.
The second identity (
3.24
)
follows
from setting
n=q=0
in (
3.23
).

Proof of Lemma
3.6
.

From (
3.4
), we have
Q_{1}=0
and

(3.26)

\displaystyle Q_{n}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n_{12}=n}L_{n_{1}}L_{n_{2}}-\mathcal{L}_{0}Q_{n-1}

for
n\geq 2
.

We first study the first term on the right-hand side of (
3.26
).
By Lemma
3.5
, (
3.3
),
and integration by parts with the anti self-adjointness of
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
, we have

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\sum_{n_{12}=n}L_{n_{1}}L_{n_{2}}dx

\displaystyle=-2\sum_{n_{12}=n}(-1)^{n-2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\mathcal{L}_{0}^{n_{1}-1}u)(\mathcal{L}_{0}^{n_{2}-1}u)dx

\displaystyle=2(-1)^{n+1}\sum_{n_{12}=n}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(i\partial_{x}+\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}+\delta^{-1})^{n_{1}-1}(-i\partial_{x}+\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}+\delta^{-1})^{n_{2}-1}udx

\displaystyle=2(-1)^{n+1}\sum_{n_{12}=n}\sum_{m_{1}=0}^{n_{1}-1}\sum_{m_{2}=0}^{n_{2}-1}\frac{1}{\delta^{{n_{12}-2-m_{12}}}}\binom{n_{1}-1}{m_{1}}\binom{n_{2}-1}{m_{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(i\partial_{x}+\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x})^{m_{1}}(-i\partial_{x}+\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x})^{m_{2}}udx

\displaystyle=2(-1)^{n+1}\sum_{m=0}^{n-2}\frac{1}{\delta^{n-2-m}}\sum_{n_{12}=n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq m_{1}\leq n_{1}-1\\
0\leq m_{2}\leq n_{2}-1\\
m=m_{12}\end{subarray}}\binom{n_{1}-1}{m_{1}}\binom{n_{2}-1}{m_{2}}

(3.27)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(i\partial_{x}+\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x})^{m_{1}}(-i\partial_{x}+\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x})^{m_{2}}udx.

For fixed
0\leq m\leq n-2
, we can write the inner sums as

(3.28)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{n_{12}=n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq m_{1}\leq n_{1}-1\\
0\leq m_{2}\leq n_{2}-1\\
m=m_{12}\end{subarray}}\binom{n_{1}-1}{m_{1}}\binom{n_{2}-1}{m_{2}}\\
&\quad\quad\times\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(i\partial_{x}+\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x})^{m_{1}}(-i\partial_{x}+\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x})^{m_{2}}udx\\
&\quad=\sum_{n_{12}=n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq m_{1}\leq n_{1}-1\\
0\leq m_{2}\leq n_{2}-1\\
m=m_{12}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\ell_{1}\leq m_{1}\\
0\leq\ell_{2}\leq m_{2}\end{subarray}}\binom{n_{1}-1}{m_{1}}\binom{n_{2}-1}{m_{2}}\\
&\quad\quad\times\binom{m_{1}}{\ell_{1}}\binom{m_{2}}{\ell_{2}}(-1)^{\ell_{2}}i^{\ell_{12}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{m-\ell_{12}}\partial_{x}^{m}udx.\end{split}

In view of the anti self-adjointness of

\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
and
\partial_{x}
, we see that the integral on the right-hand side vanishes unless

\ell_{12}=\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}
is even.
Then, we have

(3.29)

\displaystyle\eqref{quad4}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{m}(-1)^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\alpha_{n,m,\ell}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{m-\ell}\partial_{x}^{m}udx,

where the coefficient
\alpha_{n,m,\ell}
is given by

\displaystyle\alpha_{n,m,\ell}=\bigg[\sum_{n_{12}=n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq m_{1}\leq n_{1}-1\\
0\leq m_{2}\leq n_{2}-1\\
m=m_{12}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\ell_{1}\leq m_{1}\\
0\leq\ell_{2}\leq m_{2}\\
\ell_{12}=\ell\end{subarray}}\binom{n_{1}-1}{m_{1}}\binom{n_{2}-1}{m_{2}}\binom{m_{1}}{\ell_{1}}\binom{m_{2}}{\ell_{2}}(-1)^{\ell_{2}}\bigg].

By (
3.25
)
and applying (
3.23
) for the sum in
n_{1}
(with

k=n_{1}
,
q=0
,
\ell=n-2
,
m=m-m_{1}
, and
n=m_{1}
) and
then in
m_{1}
(with
k=m_{1}
,
q=0
,
\ell=m
,
m=\ell-\ell_{1}
, and
n=\ell_{1}
), we have

\displaystyle\alpha_{n,m,\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq m_{1}\leq m\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\ell_{1}\leq m_{1}\\
0\leq\ell-\ell_{1}\leq m-m_{1}\end{subarray}}\sum_{n_{1}=m_{1}+1}^{n-1+m_{1}-m}\binom{n_{1}-1}{m_{1}}\binom{n-n_{1}-1}{m-m_{1}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{XXXXXXXX}\times\binom{m_{1}}{\ell_{1}}\binom{m-m_{1}}{\ell-\ell_{1}}(-1)^{\ell-\ell_{1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq m_{1}\leq m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\ell_{1}\leq m_{1}\\
0\leq\ell-\ell_{1}\leq m-m_{1}\end{subarray}}\binom{m_{1}}{\ell_{1}}\binom{m-m_{1}}{\ell-\ell_{1}}

\displaystyle\hphantom{XXXXXXXX}\times(-1)^{\ell-\ell_{1}}\sum_{n_{1}=0}^{n-2}\binom{n_{1}}{m_{1}}\binom{n-2-n_{1}}{m-m_{1}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell}\binom{n-1}{m+1}\sum_{\ell_{1}=0}^{\ell}\sum_{m_{1}=\ell_{1}}^{m-(\ell-\ell_{1})}\binom{m_{1}}{\ell_{1}}\binom{m-m_{1}}{\ell-\ell_{1}}(-1)^{\ell_{1}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell}\binom{n-1}{m+1}\binom{m+1}{\ell+1}\sum_{\ell_{1}=0}^{\ell}(-1)^{\ell_{1}}

(3.30)

\displaystyle=\binom{n-1}{m+1}\binom{m+1}{\ell+1},

where we used the fact that
\ell
is even in the last step.
Hence, it follows from (
3.29
), (
3.30
),
and
integration by parts, using the facts that
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}
is self-adjoint and
that
\ell
is even
with the convention (
1.9
),
that

(3.31)

\displaystyle\eqref{quad4}=\binom{n-1}{m+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{m}\binom{m+1}{\ell+1}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{\frac{m-\ell}{2}}u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{m}{2}}}.

Therefore, putting (
3.26
), (
3.27
), (
3.28
), and (
3.31
) together
with (
3.3
),
we obtain the following recursive relation:

(3.32)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{T}}Q_{n}dx&=2(-1)^{n+1}\sum_{m=0}^{n-2}\frac{1}{\delta^{n-2-m}}\binom{n-1}{m+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{m}\binom{m+1}{\ell+1}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{\frac{m-\ell}{2}}u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{m}{2}}}\\
&\quad-\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{\mathbb{T}}Q_{n-1}dx\\
&=:A_{n}-\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{\mathbb{T}}Q_{n-1}dx,\end{split}

for
n\geq 2
and
Q_{1}=0
.
Let
\beta_{n,m,\ell}=2\binom{n-1}{m+1}\binom{m+1}{\ell+1}
.
Then, from (
3.32
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}Q_{n}dx

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{n-2}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{\delta^{j}}A_{n-j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{n-2}(-1)^{n-j+1+j}\sum_{m=0}^{n-j-2}\frac{1}{\delta^{n-j-2-m+j}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{m}\beta_{n-j,m,\ell}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{\frac{m-\ell}{2}}u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{m}{2}}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}\sum_{m=0}^{n-2}\frac{1}{\delta^{n-2-m}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{m}\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{n-2-m}\beta_{n-j,m,\ell}\bigg)\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{\frac{m-\ell}{2}}u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{m}{2}}}

\displaystyle=2(-1)^{n+1}\sum_{m=0}^{n-2}\frac{1}{\delta^{n-2-m}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{m}\binom{m+1}{\ell+1}\binom{n}{m+2}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{\frac{m-\ell}{2}}u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{m}{2}}},

where, in the last step, we used
(
3.25
) and (
3.24
):

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n-2-m}\beta_{n-j,m,\ell}

\displaystyle=2\binom{m+1}{\ell+1}\sum_{j=0}^{n-2-m}\binom{n-1-j}{m+1}=2\binom{m+1}{\ell+1}\sum_{j=m+1}^{n-1}\binom{j}{m+1}

\displaystyle=2\binom{m+1}{\ell+1}\binom{n}{m+2}.

This proves (
3.22
).
∎

3.3.
Construction and convergence of the deep-water conservation laws

In this subsection,
by taking a linear combination of
\Re\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{n}dx
,
we construct deep-water conservation laws
and
prove
their convergence to the corresponding
BO conservation laws,
where, in the latter,
a perturbative viewpoint plays a crucial role;
see Proposition
3.8
.

Given
0<\delta<\infty
,
let
\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
be the perturbation operator defined in (
1.14
).
Then,
from (
3.3
) and (
1.14
), we can rewrite (
3.4
) as

(3.33)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\chi_{1}&=2u,\\
\chi_{n}&=-\sum_{j=2}^{n}\frac{1}{j!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\dots,n_{j}\in\mathbb{N}\\
n_{1\cdots j}=n\end{subarray}}\chi_{n_{1}}\cdots\chi_{n_{j}}\\
&\quad-(\mathcal{H}-i)\partial_{x}\chi_{n-1}-(\delta^{-1}+\mathcal{Q}_{\delta})\chi_{n-1},\quad n\geq 2.\end{split}

We introduce the classes
\mathcal{P}_{n}(u)

of (monic)
19
19

19

Here, “monic” means that the fundamental form in (
3.34
)
is monic.
monomials in
u
,
adapted to our perturbative viewpoint (
3.33
).
Compare these with the classes of monomials
introduced in
[
113
, Section 2]

in the context of the BO equation,
where there is no perturbation operator
\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
with
\int_{\mathbb{T}}udx=0
.
Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
define
the classes
\mathcal{P}_{n}(u)
of monomials by setting

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{1}(u)

\displaystyle=\Big\{\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{1}}\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}^{\beta_{1}}\partial_{x}^{\gamma_{1}}u:\alpha_{1}\in\{0,1\},\,\beta_{1},\gamma_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\Big\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{2}(u)

\displaystyle=\Big\{\big[\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{1}}\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}^{\beta_{1}}\partial_{x}^{\gamma_{1}}u\big]\big[\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{2}}\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}^{\beta_{2}}\partial_{x}^{\gamma_{2}}u\big]:\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\{0,1\},\,\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\Big\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{n}(u)

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{\ell}}\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}^{\beta_{\ell}}p_{j_{\ell}}(u):k\in\{2,\ldots,n\},\,j_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N},\,j_{1\cdots k}=n,

\displaystyle\hphantom{lXXXXXXXXXXX}p_{j_{\ell}}(u)\in\mathcal{P}_{j_{\ell}}(u),\,\alpha_{\ell}\in\{0,1\},\,\beta_{\ell}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\bigg\}.

Here, we used the fact that,
in (
3.33
), the Hilbert transform comes with
\partial_{x}
,
namely,
\mathcal{H}
always acts on mean-zero functions
and that

\mathcal{H}^{2}=-\textup{Id}

on mean-zero functions; see also Footnote
1
.
Given a monomial
p(u)\in\mathcal{P}_{n}(u)
,
we define
its fundamental form
\widetilde{p}(u)\in\mathcal{P}_{n}(u)

which is obtained
by dropping all the
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
operators
appearing in
p(u)
.

Given a monomial
p(u)\in\mathcal{P}_{n}(u)
,
suppose that its fundamental form
\widetilde{p}(u)
is given by

(3.34)

\displaystyle\widetilde{p}(u)=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{x}^{\gamma_{j}}u.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\#u(p(u))=n\qquad\text{and}\qquad\#\partial_{x}(p(u))=\gamma_{1}+\dots+\gamma_{n}

in the sense of Definition
3.3
.
With our perturbative viewpoint (
3.33
), we also let

\displaystyle\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}

\displaystyle=\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}(p(u))=\text{number of appearance of $\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}$ in $p(u)$}.

Then, from Lemma
3.4
and (
3.19
) with (
1.14
), we have
20
20

20

Recall from Definition
3.3

that
\#\partial_{x}
counts the number of
explicit
appearance of
\partial_{x}
.
Namely, it does not count
\partial_{x}
appearing in the definition (
1.14
) of
\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
.

(3.35)

\displaystyle n=\ord(p)=\#u+\#\partial_{x}+\#\delta^{-1}+\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}

for any monomial
p(u)
in
the
n
th microscopic conservation law
\chi_{n}
.
Moreover, given a monomial
p(u)\in\mathcal{P}_{n}(u)

with its fundamental form
\widetilde{p}(u)
as in (
3.34
),
we let
|p(u)|
denote the maximum number of (explicit) derivatives
(applied to a single factor
u
)
by setting

\displaystyle|p(u)|

\displaystyle=\max_{j=1,\dots,n}\gamma_{j},

where
\gamma_{j}
is as in (
3.34
).

We are now ready to define the deep-water conservation laws
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

and present a proof of
Theorem
1.3
.
Recall that we are working under the mean-zero assumption on
u

throughout the paper.

Proposition 3.8
.

(i)

Let
0<\delta<\infty
.
Given
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
define
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
recursively by

(3.36)

\displaystyle E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{4b_{k}}\bigg[\Re\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{k+2}^{\delta}dx-4(-1)^{k+1}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\frac{1}{\delta^{k-j}}\binom{k+2}{j+2}b_{j}E^{\delta}_{\frac{j}{2}}(u)\bigg]

with the understanding that the second sum vanishes when
k=0
,
where
\chi_{n}^{\delta}
is the microscopic conservation law defined in (
3.4
)
and
b_{k}
is given by

(3.37)

\displaystyle b_{k}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\textup{even}\end{subarray}}^{k}\binom{k+1}{\ell+1}.

Then,

E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
is conserved under the flow of ILW (
1.1
).
Moreover,

(i.a)

E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
has
rank
k+2

in the sense of Definition
3.3
,

(i.b)

the quadratic part of
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

does not depend on
\delta
in an explicit manner and
is given by

(3.38)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2b_{k}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\textup{even}\end{subarray}}^{k}\binom{k+1}{\ell+1}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{\frac{k-\ell}{2}}u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{k}{2}}},

where we used the convention (
1.9
).

In particular, we have

(3.39)

\displaystyle E^{\delta}_{0}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

and, defining
a_{k,\ell}
by

(3.40)

\displaystyle a_{k,\ell}=\frac{1}{b_{k}}\binom{k+1}{\ell+1},

the conservation law
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
in (
3.36
)
reduces to the form
(
1.7
) with (
1.8
).

(ii)

By extending the definition (
3.36
) to the case
\delta=\infty
,
we obtain the following conservation laws for BO (
1.3
)
:

(3.41)

\displaystyle E^{\text{\rm BO}}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{4b_{k}}\Re\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{k+2}^{\text{\rm BO}}dx,

where
\chi_{n}^{\text{\rm BO}}

is the microscopic conservation law for BO defined in (
3.18
).
Moreover,

(ii.a)

E^{\text{\rm BO}}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
has
rank
k+2
with
\#\delta^{-1}=0
,

(ii.b)

E^{\text{\rm BO}}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

is of the form (
1.12
).

Given
0<\delta<\infty
,
we have

(3.42)

\displaystyle E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)=E_{\frac{k}{2}}^{\text{\rm BO}}(u)+\mathcal{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u),

where
\mathcal{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

consists of the terms in
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

which depend on
\delta
in an explicit manner
and the terms with
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}
, where
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}
is replaced by
the perturbation operator
\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
defined in (
1.14
).
Moreover, we have

(3.43)

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)=0,\qquad\text{namely,}\qquad\lim_{\delta\to\infty}E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)=E_{\frac{k}{2}}^{\text{\rm BO}}(u)

for any
u\in H^{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbb{T})
.

Proof.

Proceeding inductively,
we see that

E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
in (
3.36
)
is defined as a linear combination of the conserved quantities
\Re\int\chi_{n}dx
.
Hence,

E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
is also conserved under the flow of ILW (and of BO when
\delta=\infty
).

From (
3.36
) and (
3.4
) with (
3.3
), we see that (
3.39
) holds
and thus
the claim (i.a) holds true when
k=0
.
We proceed by induction and assume that the claim (i.a)
holds for any
0\leq j\leq k-1
.
On the one hand,
it follows from Lemma
3.4
that

\Re\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{k+2}\,dx
has rank
k+2
.
On the other hand, by the inductive hypothesis,
we see that

\delta^{-(k-j)}E^{\delta}_{\frac{j}{2}}(u)
has rank
(k-j)+(j+2)=k+2
.
Hence, we conclude from (
3.36
) that
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
has rank
k+2
.
The claim (ii.a) follows from Lemma
3.4

(which also holds for
\delta=\infty
).

Next, we prove the claim (i.b).
Once again, we proceed by induction.
In view of (
3.39
),
the claim (i.b) holds when
k=0
.
Now,
assume that the claim (i.b)
holds for
E^{\delta}_{\frac{j}{2}}(u)
,
0\leq j\leq k-1
.
Then,
from (
3.36
)
and the inductive hypothesis,
the quadratic part in
u
of
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

is given by

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{4b_{k}}\bigg[\Re\int_{\mathbb{T}}Q_{k+2}dx-2(-1)^{k+1}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\frac{1}{\delta^{k-j}}\binom{k+2}{j+2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\textup{even}\end{subarray}}^{j}\binom{j+1}{\ell+1}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{\frac{j-\ell}{2}}u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{j}{2}}}\bigg],

which agrees with (
3.38
)
in view of
Lemma
3.6
,
thus proving the claim (i.b).
The claim (ii.b) directly follows from
(
3.41
) and Lemma
3.6
(which
also holds for
\delta=\infty
)
with

\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{\frac{m-\ell}{2}}u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{m}{2}}}\Big|_{\delta=\infty}=\|u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{m}{2}}}

and (
3.37
).

Let
\chi_{n,0}
be the
\delta
-free
part of
\chi_{n}
.
Namely,

\chi_{n,0}

is the collection of the monomials in
\chi_{n}

which do not depend on
\delta

in an explicit manner or on
\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
.
Then, from (
3.33
),
we see that

\chi_{n,0}
satisfies the following recursive relation:

(3.44)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\chi_{1,0}&=2u,\\
\chi_{n,0}&=-\sum_{j=2}^{n}\frac{1}{j!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\dots,n_{j}\in\mathbb{N}\\
n_{1\cdots j}=n\end{subarray}}\chi_{n_{1},0}\cdots\chi_{n_{j},0}-(\mathcal{H}-i)\partial_{x}\chi_{n-1,0},\quad n\geq 2.\end{split}

By comparing (
3.44
) and (
3.18
), we see
that

(3.45)

\displaystyle\chi_{n,0}=\chi_{n}^{\text{\rm BO}}

for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Now, let
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},0}(u)
be
the
\delta
-free part of

E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

defined in (
3.36
).
Then, from (
3.36
) and (
3.41
) with (
3.45
), we obtain

(3.46)

\displaystyle E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},0}(u)=\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{4b_{k}}\bigg[\Re\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{k+2,0}\,dx\bigg]=E_{\frac{k}{2}}^{\text{\rm BO}}(u).

Hence, from (
3.42
), (
3.46
),
and the definition of

E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},0}(u)
, we have

(3.47)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)=E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)-E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},0}(u),

where all the terms in

\mathcal{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
must involve
at least one power of
\delta^{-1}
or
\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
.

It remains to prove (
3.43
).
When
k=0
,
we have
E^{\delta}_{0}(u)=E_{0}^{\text{\rm BO}}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

and thus there is nothing to prove.
In the following, we assume
k\geq 1
.
From
(
3.42
) with
Parts (i.a) and (ii.a)
(see also (
3.35
)),
(
3.47
),
and (
3.21
) in Lemma
3.5
,
we see that any monomial in
\mathcal{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
satisfies

(3.48)

\displaystyle\#u+\#\partial_{x}+\#\delta^{-1}+\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}=k+2,\quad\#\delta^{-1}+\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}\geq 1,\quad\text{and}\quad\#u\geq 2,

where it is understood that

\#\partial_{x}
counts the number of
explicit
appearance of
\partial_{x}

(namely, it does not count
\partial_{x}
appearing in
\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
).
In particular, we have

(3.49)

\displaystyle\#\partial_{x}\leq k-1.

Thus, each monomial in

\mathcal{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
is of the form

\delta^{-m}\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(u)dx
for some

0\leq m\leq k

and

p(u)\in\mathcal{P}_{j}(u)

with

j=\#u\in\{2,\dots,k+2-m\}
.
Then, by integration by parts
with Definitions
3.3
and
3.7

and (
3.49
), we have

(3.50)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)&=\sum_{m=1}^{k}\sum_{j=2}^{k+2-m}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k+2-m-j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p(u)\in\mathcal{P}_{j}(u)\\
\eqref{DE11}\text{ holds}\\
\#\partial_{x}(p)=\ell\\
|p(u)|\leq\lceil\frac{k-1}{2}\rceil\end{subarray}}\delta^{-m}c(p)\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(u)dx\\
&\quad+\sum_{j=2}^{k+1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k+1-j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p(u)\in\mathcal{P}_{j}(u)\\
\eqref{DE11}\text{ holds}\\
\#\partial_{x}(p)=\ell\\
\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}(p)\geq 1\\
|p(u)|\leq\lceil\frac{k-1}{2}\rceil\end{subarray}}c(p)\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(u)dx\end{split}

for some constants
c(p)\in\mathbb{R}
, independent of
\delta
,
where
\lceil x\rceil
denotes
the smallest integer greater than or equal to
x
.
As for the second term on the right-hand side of (
3.50
),
we have
\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}\geq 1
since it does not depend on
\delta
in an explicit manner
but is part of
\mathcal{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

(which must involve at least one power of
\delta^{-1}
or
\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
).

Fix
p(u)\in\mathcal{P}_{j}(u)

with
|p(u)|\leq\lceil\frac{k-1}{2}\rceil
, appearing on the right-hand side of (
3.50
).
(i) When
k=1
, we have

\lceil\frac{k-1}{2}\rceil=0
and thus there is no explicit
\partial_{x}

on any factor of
u
in
p(u)
.
Then,
by
Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality,
we have

(3.51)

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(u)dx\bigg|\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\big(1+\|u\|_{H^{\frac{k}{2}}}\big)^{k+1}.

(ii) When
k\geq 3
is odd, it follows from (
3.49
)
that there are at most two factors of
u
in
p(u)
with

\lceil\frac{k-1}{2}\rceil=\frac{k-1}{2}
derivatives,
while all the other factors have at most

\frac{k-3}{2}<\frac{k}{2}-\frac{1}{2}
derivatives.
In this case,
by viewing the product on the physical side as
an iterated convolution on the Fourier side
and
applying
Young’s inequality
and
Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality,
21
21

21

Here, we work on the Fourier side
such that we can drop
\mathcal{H}
and apply (
2.10
) when
\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}(p)\geq 1

by
simply putting
all the terms in an absolute value on the Fourier side.
This is due to the fact that these operators may act on a product.
Namely, it is not enough to apply
the
L^{2}
-boundedness of
\mathcal{H}
or (
2.10
)
to the right-hand sides of
(
3.52
) and (
3.54
).
A similar comment applies to the proof of Proposition
4.11
.

we have

(3.52)

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(u)dx\bigg|\lesssim\big(1+\|u\|_{H^{\frac{k-1}{2}}}\big)^{2}\big(1+\|u\|_{\mathcal{F}L^{\frac{k-3}{2},1}}\big)^{k-1}\lesssim\big(1+\|u\|_{H^{\frac{k}{2}}}\big)^{k+1},

where
\mathcal{F}L^{s,r}(\mathbb{T})
denotes the Fourier-Lebesgue space defined by the norm:

(3.53)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\mathcal{F}L^{s,r}}=\|\langle n\rangle^{s}\widehat{u}(n)\|_{\ell^{r}_{n}}.

(iii) When
k\geq 2
is even, it follows from (
3.49
)
that the maximum number of derivatives on any factor is

\lceil\frac{k-1}{2}\rceil=\frac{k}{2}
.
Moreover, there can be at most one term with

\frac{k}{2}
-many derivatives, while
all the other terms have at most

\frac{k}{2}-1
derivatives.
Hence, proceeding as in (
3.52
), we have

(3.54)

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(u)dx\bigg|\lesssim\big(1+\|u\|_{H^{\frac{k}{2}}}\big)\big(1+\|u\|_{\mathcal{F}L^{\frac{k}{2}-1,1}}\big)^{k}\lesssim\big(1+\|u\|_{H^{\frac{k}{2}}}\big)^{k+1}.

In all the cases (i), (ii), and (iii),
if, in addition, we have
\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}(p(u))\geq 1
,
in view of (
2.10
),
we obtain a power of
\delta^{-1}
in (
3.51
),
(
3.52
), and (
3.54
).
Hence, from (
3.50
), (
3.51
), (
3.52
), and (
3.54
)
with
\#\delta^{-1}+\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}\geq 1
, we obtain

(3.55)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)|\lesssim\frac{1}{\delta}\big(1+\|u\|_{H^{\frac{k}{2}}}\big)^{k+1}\longrightarrow 0,

as
\delta\to\infty
.
This proves (
3.43
) for
k\geq 1
.
This concludes the proof of Proposition
3.8
.
∎

In the remaining part of this subsection,
we take a closer look at the structure of the cubic terms in the
deep-water conservation law

E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then, the cubic
(
in
u
)
part in
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

consists of monomials of the form

(3.56)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta^{\sigma}}\int\prod_{j=1}^{3}\big(\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}^{\alpha_{j}}(\mathcal{H}\partial_{x})^{\beta_{j}}\partial_{x}^{\gamma_{j}}u\big)dx

for some

\sigma,\alpha_{j},\beta_{j},\gamma_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
\sigma+\sum_{j=1}^{3}(\alpha_{j}+\beta_{j}+\gamma_{j})=k-1
.

Proof.

From (
3.36
), we know that
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

is given by a linear combination of

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta^{k-j}}\int\Re\chi_{j+2}dx

for
j=0,\ldots,k
.
Then, (
3.56
) follows
from (
3.33
) (which in particular implies that
\mathcal{H}
must come with
\partial_{x}
),
(
3.35
),
and noting that if
\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
,
\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}
, or
\partial_{x}
acts on the product
of two factors, then by integration by parts, we can move it onto the
remaining factor
(thanks to the fact that
\#u=3
).
∎

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Integrating by parts, we can rewrite the cubic terms in
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

to guarantee that they have at most
m=\lceil\frac{k-1}{2}\rceil
derivatives on each factor.
While
most contributions have at most
m-1
derivatives on each factor,
we need to pay particular attention to the terms
where there is a factor with
m
derivatives
(even after integration by parts).
Let

B^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
denote the collection
of the worst contributions from the cubic terms in
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
,
defined by

(i)

when
k=2m
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

(3.57)

\displaystyle B^{\delta}_{\frac{2m}{2}}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p(u)\in\mathcal{P}_{3}(u)\\
\widetilde{p}(u)=u\partial_{x}^{m-1}u\partial_{x}^{m}u\\
\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}(p)=0\end{subarray}}c(p)\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(u)dx,

(ii)

when
k=2m+1
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

\displaystyle B^{\delta}_{\frac{2m+1}{2}}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p(u)\in\mathcal{P}_{3}(u)\\
\widetilde{p}(u)=u\partial_{x}^{m}u\partial_{x}^{m}u\\
\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}(p)=0\end{subarray}}c(p)\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(u)dx

for some constants
c(p)\in\mathbb{R}
, independent of
\delta
,
where
\widetilde{p}(u)
denotes the fundamental form
of a monomial
p(u)

in the sense of Definition
3.7
.

We have the following characterization of
B^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
.

Lemma 3.10
.

(i)

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}

and
0<\delta<\infty
.
Then, there exist
c_{0},c(p)\in\mathbb{R}
, independent of
\delta
, such that

(3.58)

\displaystyle B^{\delta}_{\frac{2m}{2}}(u)

\displaystyle=c_{0}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}^{m-1}u)(\partial_{x}^{m}u)dx+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p(u)\in\mathcal{P}_{3}(u)\\
|p(u)|\leq m-1\\
\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}(p)=0\end{subarray}}{c}(p)\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(u)dx,

where

\mathcal{P}_{3}(u)
,
|p(u)|
, and
\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}(p)
are as in Definition
3.7
.

(ii)

Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}

and
0<\delta<\infty
.
Then,
B^{\delta}_{\frac{2m+1}{2}}(u)
is given by

(3.59)

\displaystyle B^{\delta}_{\frac{2m+1}{2}}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\vec{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})\in\mathcal{A}}c_{\vec{\alpha}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{1}}u)(\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{2}}\partial_{x}^{m}u)(\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{3}}\partial_{x}^{m}u)dx

for some constants
c_{\vec{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{R}
,
independent of
\delta
,
where the index set
\mathcal{A}
is given by

(3.60)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\big\{(0,0,0),(0,0,1),(0,1,1)\big\}.

Proof.

(i)
From (
3.57
)
and (
3.35
),
we see that
any monomial in
B^{\delta}_{\frac{2m}{2}}(u)
satisfies

\#\delta^{-1}=\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}=0
.
Namely, it must come from the
\delta
-free
part
E^{\delta}_{\frac{2m}{2},0}(u)
,
and hence from
E_{\frac{2m}{2}}^{\text{\rm BO}}(u)

in view of (
3.46
).
Then, (
3.58
) follows
from
[
114
, Proposition 2.2]
.

(ii) Similarly,
any monomial in
B^{\delta}_{\frac{2m+1}{2}}(u)
satisfies

\#\delta^{-1}=\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}=0

and thus
must come from the
\delta
-free
part
E^{\delta}_{\frac{2m+12}{2},0}(u)
,
and hence from
E_{\frac{2m+12}{2}}^{\text{\rm BO}}(u)

in view of (
3.46
).
Then, (
3.59
) follows
from

[
115
, Proposition 8.1]
.
∎

3.4.
Structure of the deep-water conservation laws

We conclude this section by stating the final structure
of the deep-water conservation law
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

defined in (
3.36
).

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
Then, from (the proof of) Proposition
3.8
,
we
see that
the deep-water conservation law
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

defined in (
3.36
)
is indeed given by (
1.7
):

(3.61)

\displaystyle\begin{split}E^{\delta}_{0}(u)&=\frac{1}{2}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}},\\
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)&=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{k}a_{k,\ell}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{\frac{k-\ell}{2}}u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{k}{2}}}+R^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u),\quad k\in\mathbb{N},\end{split}

with
a_{k,\ell}
as in (
3.40
).
Here, the interaction potential
R^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
,
satisfying
\#u\geq 3
, is given by

(3.62)

\displaystyle R^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)=A^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{k}{2}}(u)+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{\delta^{k-\ell}}A^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{\ell}{2}}(u),

where
A^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{\ell}{2}}(u)
is defined as follows, depending on the parity of
\ell
;
when
\ell=2m
is even, we have

\displaystyle A^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{2m}{2}}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p(u)\in\mathcal{P}_{3}(u)\\
\widetilde{p}(u)=u\partial_{x}^{m-1}u\partial_{x}^{m}u\\
\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}(p)=0\end{subarray}}c(p)\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(u)dx

(3.63)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p(u)\in\mathcal{P}_{j}(u),\\
j=3,\ldots,2m+2\\
\#\partial_{x}(p)+\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}(p)=2m+2-j\\
|p(u)|\leq m-1\end{subarray}}\hskip-14.22636ptc(p)\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(u)dx

with suitable constants
c(p)\in\mathbb{R}
,
independent of
\delta
,
while,
when
\ell=2m+1
is odd, we have

\displaystyle A^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{2m+1}{2}}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p(u)\in\mathcal{P}_{3}(u)\\
\widetilde{p}(u)=u\partial_{x}^{m}u\partial_{x}^{m}u\\
\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}(p)=0\end{subarray}}c(p)\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(u)dx

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p(u)\in\mathcal{P}_{3}(u)\\
\widetilde{p}(u)=\partial_{x}^{\kappa}u\partial_{x}^{m-1}u\partial_{x}^{m}u,\,\kappa=0,1\\
\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}(p)=1-\kappa\end{subarray}}c(p)\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(u)dx

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p(u)\in\mathcal{P}_{4}(u)\\
\widetilde{p}(u)=u^{2}\partial_{x}^{m-1}u\partial_{x}^{m}u\\
\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}(p)=0\end{subarray}}c(p)\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(u)dx

(3.64)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p(u)\in\mathcal{P}_{j}(u),\\
j=3,\ldots,2m+3\\
\#\partial_{x}(p)+\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}(p)=2m+3-j\\
|p(u)|\leq m-1\end{subarray}}\hskip-28.45274ptc(p)\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(u)dx

with the understanding that, when
m=0
,
there is no contribution from the second, third, and fourth
terms on the right-hand side of (
3.64
).
In particular, we have
22
22

22

Note that there is no
\mathcal{H}
in (
3.65
)
since
\mathcal{H}
must come with
\partial_{x}
(before integration by parts)
but
\#\partial_{x}=0
.

(3.65)

\displaystyle A^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{1}{2}}(u)=c_{0}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u^{3}dx

for some constant
c_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
independent of
\delta
.
Here, we used the following facts:

•

When
\ell=2m
and
|p(u)|=m
,
it follows from Proposition
3.8
(i.a)
(see also (
3.48
)) with (
3.62
) that

\displaystyle\#\partial_{x}+\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}=k+2-\#u-\#\delta^{-1}\leq 2m-1,

which implies that
\widetilde{p}(u)=u\partial_{x}^{m-1}u\partial_{x}^{m}u

(after integration by parts)
since, if
\widetilde{p}(u)=\partial_{x}^{\kappa-1}u\partial_{x}^{m-\kappa}u\partial_{x}^{m}u

for some
2\leq\kappa\leq\frac{m+1}{2}
,
then integration by parts allows us to reduce
to the case
|p(u)|\leq m-1
.

•

Similarly, when
\ell=2m+1
and
|p(u)|=m
,
we have

\displaystyle\#\partial_{x}+\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}=k+2-\#u-\#\delta^{-1}\leq 2m+3-\#u.

Thus, when
\#u=4
, we must have
\widetilde{p}(u)=u^{2}\partial_{x}^{m-1}u\partial_{x}^{m}u
.
When
\#u=3
, we have
either
\widetilde{p}(u)=u\partial_{x}^{m}u\partial_{x}^{m}u

or
\partial_{x}^{\kappa}u\partial_{x}^{m-1}u\partial_{x}^{m}u

with
\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}=1-\kappa
,
\kappa=0,1

(after integration by parts).

Moreover, it follows from
Lemma
3.10
that
the leading contribution
of the cubic terms in
A^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{2m}{2}}(u)

is given by

\displaystyle c_{0}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}^{m-1}u)(\partial_{x}^{m}u)dx

for some
c_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
while
the leading contribution
of the cubic terms in
A^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{2m+1}{2}}(u)

is given by

\displaystyle\sum_{\vec{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})\in\mathcal{A}}c_{\vec{\alpha}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{1}}u)(\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{2}}\partial_{x}^{m}u)(\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{3}}\partial_{x}^{m}u)dx

for some constants
c_{\vec{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{R}
,
where

\mathcal{A}=\{(0,0,0),(0,0,1),(0,1,1)\}

is as in (
3.60
).

4.
Shallow-water conservation laws

In this section, we present a proof of
Theorem
1.4
.
Namely,
we construct conservation laws in the shallow-water regime
and establish their shallow-water convergence;
see Propositions
4.10
and
4.11
.
In particular, we establish a 2-to-1 collapse of the shallow-water conservation laws
to the KdV conservation laws,
as stated in (
1.20
).
The construction
of a full set of
shallow-water conservation laws with non-trivial shallow-water limits
is particularly important
in order to study the shallow-water convergence problem
for the associated
generalized Gibbs measures;
see
Section
6

for further details.

As explained in Section
1
,
while the scaling transform in (
1.4
) applied to the deep-water conservation laws
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
constructed in Section
3

yields conservation laws for the scaled ILW (
1.5
),
they are not suitable for studying
the shallow-water convergence problems.
In order to overcome this issue,
we start with an alternative Bäcklund transform (
4.1
) for the scaled ILW and derive a recursive formulation for the microscopic conservation laws
h_{n}

in Subsection
4.1
.
In Subsection
4.2
, we then study the structure of the microscopic conservation laws
h_{n}
and,
by taking suitable linear combinations of
h_{n}
,
we construct alternative
microscopic conservation laws

\widetilde{h}_{n}
(see (
4.36
)),
whose quadratic parts, upon integration over
\mathbb{T}
,
have fixed numbers of derivatives;
see Lemma
4.6
.
Moreover, these new
microscopic conservation laws

\widetilde{h}_{n}

guarantee non-trivial shallow-water limits
for the associated shallow-water conservation laws.
In Subsection
4.3
, we then define the shallow-water conservation laws
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)
and establish a 2-to-1 collapse of the shallow-water conservation laws
(Propositions
4.10

and
4.11
). Lastly, by combining the earlier results, we state the final structure of the shallow-water conservation laws
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)

in Subsection
4.4
.
Analogously to the deep-water case studied in the previous section,
we introduce the notion of the
shallow-water rank
(Definition
4.7
),
which is more complicated than the deep-water rank, reflecting
the more challenging nature of the shallow-water problem.
This shallow-water rank
plays a crucial role in understanding the structure
of the shallow-water conservation laws,
thus allowing us to prove the desired 2-to-1 collapse.

4.1.
Bäcklund transforms and microscopic conservation laws

We first recall the Bäcklund transform for the
scaled ILW (
1.5
) derived in
[
36
,
59
]
(modulo constants).
Let
0<\delta<\infty
and
\mu>0
. Given a (smooth) solution
v
to the scaled ILW (
1.5
), the Bäcklund transform yields a function
V=V(v;\mu)
such that the pair
(v,V)
satisfies the following system:

(4.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}v&=\frac{1}{2\delta^{2}}\big\{2i\mu\delta V-(1-i\mu^{-1}\delta)(e^{2i\mu\delta V}-1)\big\}+\mu V_{x}+i\mu\delta\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}V_{x},\\
V_{t}&=\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\big\{2i\mu\delta V-(1-i\mu^{-1}\delta)(e^{2i\mu\delta V}-1)\big\}V_{x}+\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}V_{xx}+2i\mu\delta V_{x}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}V_{x},\end{split}

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
is as in (
1.5
).
For now, assume that
V
is of the form

(4.2)

V=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mu^{n}h_{n},

where
\{h_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}=\{h^{\delta}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
is a sequence of smooth functions, independent of
\mu>0
.
Then, by first rewriting the first equation in (
4.1
) as

(4.3)

V=-v-\frac{1}{2\delta^{2}}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j!}(2i\mu\delta V)^{j}+\frac{i\mu^{-1}}{2\delta}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j!}(2i\mu\delta V)^{j}+\mu\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}V,

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}=\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}(\delta)
is given by

(4.4)

\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}=(1+i\delta\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta})\partial_{x},

substituting (
4.2
), and comparing powers of
\mu
, we obtain the following recursive definition for
h_{n}=h^{\delta}_{n}
in terms of
v
:

(4.5)

\displaystyle h_{0}

\displaystyle=-v,

\displaystyle h_{1}

\displaystyle=-i\delta h_{0}^{2}+\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}h_{0}=-i\delta v^{2}-\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}v,

\displaystyle h_{n}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\delta^{2}}\sum_{j=2}^{n}\frac{(2i\delta)^{j}}{j!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\ldots,n_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
n_{1\cdots j}=n-j\end{subarray}}h_{n_{1}}\cdots h_{n_{j}}

\displaystyle+\frac{i}{2\delta}\sum_{j=2}^{n+1}\frac{(2i\delta)^{j}}{j!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\ldots,n_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
n_{1\cdots j}=n+1-j\end{subarray}}h_{n_{1}}\cdots h_{n_{j}}+\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}h_{n-1},\quad n\geq 2,

where
n_{1\cdots j}=n_{1}+\cdots+n_{j}
is as in (
2.1
).
Hence, as in the deep-water case,
given a smooth function
v
on
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
,
by recursively constructing
h_{n}
via (
4.5
)
and using (
4.2
), we
can
construct a smooth function
V=V(v;\mu)
.

In the following lemma,
we establish
equivalence between
the scaled ILW (
1.5
) and its Bäcklund transform (
4.1
). As its proof does not seem to be readily available in the literature, we include it for readers’ convenience.
As in the deep-water case, we impose the additional assumption (
4.2
)
in the converse direction.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
0<\delta<\infty
. Given
\mu>0
, let
v:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
and
V:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C}
be smooth functions, satisfying the first equation in (
4.1
). If
V
satisfies the second equation in (
4.1
), then
v=v(V)
satisfies the scaled ILW (
1.5
). Conversely, if
v
satisfies the scaled ILW (
1.5
) and if, in addition,
V
is of the form (
4.2
), then
V=V(v;\mu)
satisfies the second equation in (
4.1
).

Remark 4.2
.

(i)
As in deep-water case,
given a smooth function
v
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T}
.
it is in general not clear to us
how to find a function
V
satisfying the first equation in (
4.1
)
without the extra assumption (
4.2
).

(ii) Fix
0<\delta<\infty
and
\mu>0
.
With
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
as in (
4.4
),
define
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}=\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\delta,\mu)
by

\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}=\textup{Id}-\mu\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}=\textup{Id}-\mu(1+i\delta\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta})\partial_{x}.

Then, we can rewrite (
4.3
) as

(4.6)

V=\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}^{-1}\bigg(-v-\frac{1}{2\delta^{2}}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j!}(2i\mu\delta V)^{j}+\frac{i\mu^{-1}}{2\delta}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j!}(2i\mu\delta V)^{j}\bigg),

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}^{-1}
is the Fourier multiplier operator with multiplier:

\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}^{-1}}(n)=\begin{cases}\big(1-i\delta^{-1}\mu(\delta n+\delta n\coth(\delta n)-1)\big)^{-1},&n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*},\\
1,&n=0.\end{cases}

Note that
|\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}^{-1}}(n)|\leq 1
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Fix
T>0
and
v\in C([-T,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
for some
s\gg 1
. Then,
for sufficiently small
\mu=\mu(\|v\|_{C_{T}H^{s}_{x}},\delta)>0
, a standard contraction argument shows that there exists a unique solution
V=V(v;\mu)
to (
4.6
) in the ball

B\subset C([-T,T];H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))

of radius
\sim\|v\|_{C_{T}H^{s}_{x}}
centered at the origin. We should, however, note that,
even if
v
satisfies the scaled ILW (
1.5
), it is not clear if
V
defined in (
4.6
) satisfies the second equation in (
4.1
).

We now present a proof of Lemma
4.1
.

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

We proceed as in the proof of Lemma
3.1
.
From the first equation in (
4.1
) and Lemma
2.6

with
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}=\delta^{-1}\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
,
a direct computation yields

(4.7)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}v-\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}v-2v\partial_{x}v

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}^{V}\Big[V_{t}-\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\big\{2i\mu\delta V-(1-i\mu^{-1}\delta)(e^{2i\mu\delta V}-1)\big\}V_{x}-\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}V_{xx}-2i\mu\delta V_{x}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}V_{x}\Big]

where the operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}^{V}=\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}^{V}(\mu)
is given by

(4.8)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}^{V}

\displaystyle=-(1+i\mu\delta^{-1})e^{2i\mu\delta V}+i\mu\big((\delta\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}-i)\partial_{x}+\delta^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=-(1+i\mu\delta^{-1})e^{2i\mu\delta V}+i\mu\mathcal{L}_{0}

with
\mathcal{L}_{0}
as in (
3.3
).
Hence, if
V
satisfies the second equation in (
4.1
),
then it follows from (
4.7
) that
v
satisfies the scaled ILW (
1.5
).

Next, we prove the converse direction.
Suppose that
v
is a smooth solution to (
1.5
)
and that
V=V(v;\mu)
of the form (
4.2
) is defined by the first equation in (
4.1
)
(as explained above).
Then, from (
4.7
) and (
4.8
), we have

(4.9)

\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}^{V}\widetilde{F}(V)=0,

where
\widetilde{F}(V)=\widetilde{F}(V;\mu)
denotes the expression in the brackets on the right-hand side of (
4.7
).
In the following, we show that
\widetilde{F}(V)=0
.
For simplicity, we will suppress the
t
-dependence. From (
4.9
) and (
4.8
), we have

(4.10)

0=\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}^{V}\widetilde{F},i\mu\mathcal{L}_{0}\widetilde{F}\rangle_{L^{2}}=-(1+i\mu\delta^{-1})\langle e^{2i\mu\delta V}\widetilde{F},i\mu\mathcal{L}_{0}\widetilde{F}\rangle_{L^{2}}+\mu^{2}\|\mathcal{L}_{0}\widetilde{F}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Then, arguing as in (
3.9
),
it follows from (
4.9
) with (
4.8
) and (
4.10
) that

(4.11)

0=\|\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}^{V}\widetilde{F}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=(1+\mu^{2}\delta^{-2})\|e^{2i\mu\delta V}\widetilde{F}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\mu^{2}\|\mathcal{L}_{0}\widetilde{F}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

From the assumption (
4.2
), we have

(4.12)

\widetilde{F}(V)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mu^{n}\widetilde{f}_{n}

for some smooth functions
\widetilde{f}_{n}
independent of
\mu>0
.
Then, from (
4.2
) and (
4.12
), we have

(4.13)

\displaystyle e^{2i\mu\delta V}\widetilde{F}

\displaystyle=\bigg(1+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j!}(2i\mu\delta V)^{j}\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mu^{n}\widetilde{f}_{n}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\bigg(1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mu^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{(2i\delta)^{j}}{j!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\ldots,n_{j}=0\\
n_{1\cdots j}=n-j\end{subarray}}^{\infty}h_{n_{1}}\cdots h_{n_{j}}\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mu^{n}\widetilde{f}_{n}\bigg)

\displaystyle=:\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mu^{n}B_{n},

where

(4.14)

\displaystyle B_{0}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{f}_{0},\qquad B_{n}=\widetilde{f}_{n}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{12}=n\\
0\leq n_{1}\leq n-1\end{subarray}}\widetilde{f}_{n_{1}}\widetilde{V}_{n_{2}},\quad n\in\mathbb{N},

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{(2i\delta)^{j}}{j!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\ldots,n_{j}=0\\
n_{1\cdots j}=n-j\end{subarray}}^{\infty}h_{n_{1}}\cdots h_{n_{j}},\quad n\in\mathbb{N}.

By substituting (
4.12
) and (
4.13
) in (
4.11
), we see that the coefficient of each power of
\mu
must be 0. From the coefficient of
\mu^{0}

with (
4.14
), we obtain

(4.15)

B_{0}=\widetilde{f}_{0}=0,

while from the coefficient of
\mu^{1}
, we have

2\Re\int_{\mathbb{T}}B_{0}\overline{B}_{1}dx=0,

which holds in view of (
4.15
).
From the coefficient of
\mu^{2}
, we obtain

(4.16)

0=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\delta^{-2}|B_{0}|^{2}+|B_{1}|^{2}+2\Re B_{0}\overline{B}_{2}-|\mathcal{L}_{0}\widetilde{f}_{0}|^{2}dx=\int_{\mathbb{T}}|B_{1}|^{2}dx,

where we used (
4.15
) at the second equality.
Hence, from the definition of
B_{1}
in (
4.14
)
with (
4.15
) and (
4.16
),
we obtain

\widetilde{f}_{1}=B_{1}=0.

We now proceed by induction and show that
\widetilde{f}_{n}=0
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
Given
n\geq 2
, assume that
\widetilde{f}_{k}=0
for
0\leq k\leq n-1
. Then, from (
4.14
), we see that

(4.17)

B_{k}=0

for
0\leq k\leq n-1
. From the coefficient of
\mu^{2n}
in (
4.11
), we get

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\sum_{n_{12}=2n}\int_{\mathbb{T}}B_{n_{1}}\overline{B}_{n_{2}}dx+\delta^{-2}\sum_{n_{12}=2n-2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}B_{n_{1}}\overline{B}_{n_{2}}-\sum_{n_{12}=2n-2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{L}_{0}\widetilde{f}_{n_{1}}\overline{\mathcal{L}_{0}\widetilde{f}_{n_{2}}}dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}|B_{n}|^{2}dx,

where we used
the inductive hypothesis and
(
4.17
) at the second equality.
Hence, we obtain
B_{n}=0
,
and,
from (
4.14
) and the inductive hypothesis, we conclude that
\widetilde{f}_{n}=0

for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
Therefore,
from (
4.12
),
we conclude that
\widetilde{F}(V)=0
.
Namely,
V

satisfies
the second equation in (
4.1
).
∎

Suppose that
V
satisfies (
4.1
). Then, from the second equation in (
4.1
)
with the fact that

V_{x}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}V_{x}
has mean zero on
\mathbb{T}

(which follows from the anti self-adjointness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
),
we see that the mean of
V
is conserved. If
V
is also of the form (
4.2
), we formally obtain

\displaystyle 0=\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{T}}Vdx=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mu^{n}\cdot\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{T}}h_{n}dx

for any
\mu>0
.
Since the coefficients of all powers of
\mu
must be 0, we conclude that

(4.18)

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{T}}h_{n}dx=0

for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
Namely, for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
h_{n}
is a microscopic conservation law for the scaled ILW (
1.5
).
As in the deep-water case,
we will define the shallow-water conservation laws
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
as
(the real part of)
suitable linear combinations
of

\int_{\mathbb{T}}h_{n}dx
; see (
4.36
) and Proposition
4.10
.

We conclude this subsection by recalling the Bäcklund transform
for KdV (
1.6
)
and its microscopic conservation laws.
Recall from Lemma
2.3
that
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\partial_{x}
converges to
-\frac{1}{3}\partial_{x}^{2}
as
\delta\to 0
. Thus, in the shallow-water limit (
\delta\to 0
), the Bäcklund transform (
4.1
) for the scaled ILW (
1.5
) converges to that for KdV (
1.6
), also known as the Gardner transform
[
67
]
:

(4.19)

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=-V+\mu^{2}V^{2}+\mu V_{x},

\displaystyle V_{t}

\displaystyle=-2(V-\mu^{2}V^{2})V_{x}-\frac{1}{3}V_{xxx}.

Note that Lemma
4.1
also holds for the case
\delta=0
.
Moreover, by setting
\delta=0
in (
4.4
) and (
4.5
)
we obtain the following recursive definition for the KdV microscopic conservation laws
h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{n}=h^{0}_{n}
:

(4.20)

\displaystyle h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{0}

\displaystyle=-v,

\displaystyle h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{1}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{x}v,

\displaystyle h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
n_{12}=n-2\end{subarray}}h^{\text{KdV}}_{n_{1}}h^{\text{KdV}}_{n_{2}}+\partial_{x}h^{\text{KdV}}_{n-1},\quad n\geq 2.

Arguing as above, we have

(4.21)

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{T}}h_{n}^{\text{\rm KdV}}dx=0

for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

The following lemma collects well-known properties
of
the KdV microscopic conservation laws
h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{n}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

(i)
Given any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have

(4.22)

\int_{\mathbb{T}}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2n+1}dx=0.

(ii)
Given
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, let
L^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{n}
and
Q^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{n}
denote
the linear and quadratic
(
in
v
)
parts of
h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{n}

defined in (
4.20
), respectively. Then, we have

(4.23)

L^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{n}=-\partial_{x}^{n}v.

Moreover, under the mean-zero assumption on
v
, we have

(4.24)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}L^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{n}dx

\displaystyle=0,

(4.25)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}Q^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2n}dx

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n-1}\|v\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{n-1}},\quad n\in\mathbb{N}.

Proof.

(i)
The identity (
4.22
)
is well known in the literature; see
[
59
, Proposition 2.7]

for a formal proof. See also
[
68
, (25) on p. 1207]
.
For readers’ convenience, we first present the argument in
[
59
,
68
]
.
Let
V
be as in (
4.2
) (with
\delta=0
