) and write it as

(4.26)

\displaystyle V=V_{+}+V_{-}:=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mu^{2n}h_{2n}^{\text{\rm KdV}}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mu^{2n+1}h_{2n+1}^{\text{\rm KdV}}.

By substituting (
4.26
) into
the first equation in (
4.19
)
and collecting the contributions coming from the odd powers of
\mu
,
we obtain

\displaystyle 0=-V_{-}+2\mu^{2}V_{+}V_{-}+\mu\partial_{x}V_{+}.

Then, by solving for
V_{-}
, we obtain

(4.27)

\displaystyle V_{-}=\frac{\mu\partial_{x}V_{+}}{1-2\mu^{2}V_{+}}=-\frac{1}{2\mu}\partial_{x}\log(1-2\mu^{2}V_{+}),

from which we conclude that

\displaystyle 0=\int_{\mathbb{T}}V_{-}dx=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mu^{2n+1}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{T}}h_{2n+1}^{\text{\rm KdV}}dx.

Since the coefficient of
\mu^{2n+1}
must be 0 for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we conclude (
4.22
).
We point out that the step in (
4.27
) is rather formal without any justification.

In the following, we present an alternative proof of (
4.22
), using the recurrence relation (
4.20
).
We claim that, for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the odd-order microscopic conservation laws
h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2n+1}
satisfy the following identity:

(4.28)

h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2n+1}=\partial_{x}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\frac{2^{j-1}}{j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\ldots,n_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
n_{1\cdots j}=n+1-j\end{subarray}}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2n_{1}}\cdots h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2n_{j}}\bigg),

from which (
4.22
) follows.

We proceed by induction on
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
When
n=0
, it follows from (
4.20
) that

(4.29)

h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{1}=\partial_{x}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{0}=\text{RHS of \eqref{hkdv2}}|_{n=0}.

When
n=1
, from (
4.20
) and (
4.29
), we have

\displaystyle h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{3}

\displaystyle=2h_{0}^{\text{\rm KdV}}h_{1}^{\text{\rm KdV}}+\partial_{x}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2}

\displaystyle=2h_{0}^{\text{\rm KdV}}\partial_{x}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{0}+\partial_{x}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\big((h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{0})^{2}+h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2}\big)

\displaystyle=\text{RHS of \eqref{hkdv2}}|_{n=1}.

Fix an integer
n\geq 2
and assume that (
4.28
) holds for
0\leq k\leq n-1
.
Then, from (
4.20
) and the inductive hypothesis, we have

(4.30)

\displaystyle\begin{split}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2n+1}&=2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
k_{12}=n-1\end{subarray}}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2k_{1}}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2k_{2}+1}+\partial_{x}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2n}\\
&=2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
k_{12}=n-1\end{subarray}}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2k_{1}}\partial_{x}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}+1}\frac{2^{j-1}}{j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1},\ldots,m_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
m_{1\cdots j}=k_{2}+1-j\end{subarray}}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2m_{1}}\cdots h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2m_{j}}\bigg)+\partial_{x}h_{2n}^{\text{\rm KdV}}.\end{split}

By rearranging the right-hand side of (
4.30
), we obtain

(4.31)

\displaystyle h_{2n+1}^{\text{\rm KdV}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{2^{j}}{j}\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{n-j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1},\ldots,m_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
m_{1\cdots j}=n-k_{1}-j\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j}\leq 1\\
\alpha_{1\cdots j}=1\end{subarray}}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2k_{1}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{j}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{\ell}}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2m_{\ell}}+\partial_{x}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{2^{j}}{j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1},\ldots,m_{j+1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
m_{1\cdots(j+1)}=n-j\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j+1}\leq 1\\
\alpha_{1\cdots j}=1\\
\alpha_{j+1}=0\end{subarray}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{j+1}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{\ell}}h_{2m_{\ell}}^{\text{\rm KdV}}+\partial_{x}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2n}.

Note that we have

(4.32)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1},\ldots,m_{j+1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
m_{1\cdots(j+1)}=n-j\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j+1}\leq 1\\
\alpha_{1\cdots j}=1\\
\alpha_{j+1}=0\end{subarray}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{j+1}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{\ell}}h_{2m_{\ell}}^{\text{\rm KdV}}

\displaystyle=j\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1},\ldots,m_{j+1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
m_{1\cdots(j+1)}=n-j\end{subarray}}(\partial_{x}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2m_{1}})h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2m_{2}}\cdots h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2m_{j+1}}

for
1\leq j\leq n
.
Similarly, we have

(4.33)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1},\ldots,m_{j+1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
m_{1\cdots(j+1)}=n-j\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j+1}\leq 1\\
\alpha_{1\cdots(j+1)}=1\end{subarray}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{j+1}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{\ell}}h_{2m_{\ell}}^{\text{\rm KdV}}

\displaystyle=(j+1)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1},\ldots,m_{j+1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
m_{1\cdots(j+1)}=n-j\end{subarray}}(\partial_{x}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2m_{1}})h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2m_{2}}\cdots h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2m_{j+1}}.

Hence, from
(
4.31
), (
4.32
), (
4.33
),
and the product rule,
we obtain

\displaystyle h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2n+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{2^{j}}{j+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1},\ldots,m_{j+1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
m_{1\cdots(j+1)}=n-j\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{j+1}\leq 1\\
\alpha_{1\cdots(j+1)}=1\end{subarray}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{j+1}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{\ell}}h_{2m_{\ell}}^{\text{\rm KdV}}+\partial_{x}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2n}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\bigg(\sum_{j=2}^{n+1}\frac{2^{j-1}}{j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1},\ldots,m_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
m_{1\cdots j}=n+1-j\end{subarray}}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2m_{1}}\cdots h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2m_{j}}\bigg)+\partial_{x}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2n}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\frac{2^{j-1}}{j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1},\ldots,m_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
m_{1\cdots j}=n+1-j\end{subarray}}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2m_{1}}\cdots h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2m_{j}}\bigg)

which agrees with the right-hand side of (
4.28
).
Therefore, by induction, we conclude that the identity (
4.28
)
holds for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

(ii)
From (
4.20
), we see that (
4.23
) holds for
n=0,1
.
Fix
n\geq 2
. Since
h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{n}
is at least linear in
v
,
it follows from (
4.20
) that

\displaystyle L^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{n}=\partial_{x}L^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{n-1},

which yields (
4.23
) by induction.
The identity (
4.24
) follows from (
4.23
) and the mean-zero assumption on
v

(which is needed for
n=0
).

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. From (
4.20
) and (
4.23
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}Q^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2n}dx

\displaystyle=\sum_{n_{12}=2n-2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}L_{n_{1}}^{\text{\rm KdV}}L_{n_{2}}^{\text{\rm KdV}}dx=\sum_{n_{1}=0}^{2n-2}(-1)^{n_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}v\partial_{x}^{2n-2}vdx

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n-1}\|v\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{n-1}},

which yields (
4.25
).
∎

4.2.
On the shallow-water microscopic conservation laws

In this subsection,
by taking linear combinations of
h_{n}
defined in (
4.5
),
we construct new
microscopic conservation laws

\widetilde{h}_{n}
(see (
4.36
)).
In the next subsection, we
use these new microscopic conservation laws

\widetilde{h}_{n}

to
construct the shallow-water conservation laws
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)
in (
4.53
). The introduction of
the new microscopic conservation laws

\widetilde{h}_{n}

allows us to guarantee that

(i)

all the quadratic terms in
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)

have exactly
k
derivatives with
positive
coefficients
(Proposition
4.10
; see also Lemma
4.6
),

(ii)

the shallow-water conservation laws
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)
have non-trivial limits as
\delta\to 0

(Proposition
4.11
).

While the point (i) already appeared in the deep-water regime,
the point (ii)
is a new difficulty specific to the shallow-water regime.
Indeed, in view of Lemma
4.3
(i),
if we simply use (the real part of)
\int_{\mathbb{T}}h_{n}dx
to define a shallow-water conservation law,
then we would get a trivial shallow-water limit
when
n
is odd in the sense that

\int_{\mathbb{T}}h_{2n+1}dx
converges to
0
as
\delta\to 0
.
This in particular causes an issue
in pursuing our statistical study
since the corresponding generalized Gibbs measure
would not have any meaningful limit.
As we see in the next subsection,
the new microscopic conservation laws
\widetilde{h}_{n}

allow us to construct
a full set of shallow-water conservation laws
with non-trivial shallow-water limits
as claimed in Theorem
1.4
.

We first state a lemma on
the structure of the linear part of
the microscopic conservation laws

h_{n}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
0<\delta<\infty
. Given
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, let
{L}_{n}={L}^{\delta}_{n}
be the linear
(
in
v
)
part of
h_{n}=h^{\delta}_{n}
defined in (
4.5
).
Then, we have

(4.34)

{L}_{n}=-(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0})^{n}v

for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
where
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
is as in (
4.4
). In particular, under the current mean-zero assumption on
v
, we have

(4.35)

\int_{\mathbb{T}}{L}_{n}dx=0

for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

Proof.

In view of (
4.5
),
the claims (
4.34
) and (
4.35
)
obviously hold when
n=0,1
.
Noting that each monomial in
h_{n}
is at least linear in
v
,
we see that
the linear contribution
on the right-hand side of the third equation in
(
4.5
) comes from the last term

\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}h_{n-1}
,
and thus we have

\displaystyle{L}_{n}=\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}{L}_{n-1}

for any
n\geq 2
, which yields (
4.34
) by induction. The second claim (
4.35
) follows from (
4.34
) with
the mean-zero assumption on
v

(which is needed for
n=0
)
and
(
4.4
).
∎

We now introduce
new microscopic conservation laws
\widetilde{h}_{n}=\widetilde{h}^{\delta}_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
by setting

(4.36)

\widetilde{h}_{n}=\frac{(-i\delta)^{\mathfrak{p}(n)}}{\delta^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{(i\delta)^{n-j}}h_{j},

where
\mathfrak{p}(n)
denotes the parity of an integer
n
defined by

(4.37)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}(n)=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if $n$ is even},\\
1,&\text{if $n$ is odd}.\end{cases}

As we see in Lemma
4.6
below,
the quadratic parts of (the integrals of) these new
microscopic conservation laws
\widetilde{h}_{n}

have simple structures.
In Proposition
4.10
,
we use
\widetilde{h}_{n}

to construct the shallow-water conservation laws

\widetilde{E}_{\frac{k}{2}}^{\delta}(v)
.

The next lemma provides a more convenient form for
\widetilde{h}_{n}

which we will use in the subsequent analysis.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
0<\delta<\infty
. Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the new microscopic conservation law
\widetilde{h}_{n}

defined in (
4.36
) can be written as follows
:

(4.38)

\displaystyle\widetilde{h}_{n}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(-i\delta)^{\mathfrak{p}(n)}}{2i\delta^{3}}\sum_{j=2}^{n+1}\frac{(2i\delta)^{j}}{j!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\ldots,n_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
n_{1\cdots j}=n+1-j\end{subarray}}h_{n_{1}}\ldots h_{n_{j}}

\displaystyle+\frac{(-i\delta)^{\mathfrak{p}(n)}}{\delta^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{(i\delta)^{n-j}}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}h_{j-1},

where
h_{j}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
, and
\mathfrak{p}(n)
are as in
(
4.5
),
(
4.4
), and (
4.37
), respectively.

Proof.

From (
4.36
) and (
4.5
), we have

(4.39)

\displaystyle\widetilde{h}_{1}=\frac{1}{i\delta}h_{1}=-h_{0}^{2}+\frac{1}{i\delta}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}h_{0},

which agrees with (
4.38
).

Let
n\geq 2
.
From (
4.5
), we have

(4.40)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{(i\delta)^{n-j}}h_{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(i\delta)^{n-1}}h_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=2}^{n}\frac{1}{(i\delta)^{n+2-j}}\sum_{k=2}^{j}\frac{(2i\delta)^{k}}{k!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
n_{1\cdots k}=j-k\end{subarray}}h_{n_{1}}\cdots h_{n_{k}}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=3}^{n+1}\frac{1}{(i\delta)^{n+2-j}}\sum_{k=2}^{j}\frac{(2i\delta)^{k}}{k!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}=0\\
n_{1\cdots k}=j-k\end{subarray}}^{\infty}h_{n_{1}}\cdots h_{n_{k}}+\sum_{j=2}^{n}\frac{1}{(i\delta)^{n-j}}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}h_{j-1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2i\delta}\sum_{j=2}^{n+1}\frac{(2i\delta)^{j}}{j!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\ldots,n_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
n_{1\cdots j}=n+1-j\end{subarray}}h_{n_{1}}\cdots h_{n_{j}}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{(i\delta)^{n-j}}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}h_{j-1},

where we used (
4.39
) at the last equality.
Then, (
4.38
) follows from (
4.36
) and (
4.40
).
∎

The following lemma
shows that the quadratic part of the macroscopic conservation law
\int_{\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{h}_{n}dx

has a particularly simple structure, involving only the
\dot{H}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}
-norm.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
0<\delta<\infty
. Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\widetilde{Q}_{n}=\widetilde{Q}_{n}^{\delta}
be the quadratic
(
in
v
)
part of
\widetilde{h}_{n}=\widetilde{h}_{n}^{\delta}
defined in (
4.36
).
Then, we have

(4.41)

\int\widetilde{Q}_{n}dx=(-1)^{\frac{n-2-\mathfrak{p}(n)}{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\mathfrak{p}(\ell)=\mathfrak{p}(n-1)\end{subarray}}^{n-1}\binom{n}{\ell}\delta^{\ell-1+\mathfrak{p}(n)}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{\frac{\ell}{2}}v\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}},\\

where

\mathfrak{p}(n)
is as in (
4.37
)
and we used the convention (
1.17
)
when
n
is even and
\ell
is odd.

Before proceeding to a proof of Lemma
4.6
,
we first recall the so-called Rothe-Hagen identity; see
[
39
, (5.63) on p. 202]
. For integers
m,n,p
with
p\geq 0
, we have

(4.42)

\sum_{\ell=0}^{p}\binom{m-1+\ell}{\ell}\binom{n+p-1-\ell}{p-\ell}=\binom{m+n+p-1}{p},

where we use
the convention (
3.25
).

Proof of Lemma
4.6
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
From Lemma
4.5
with (
4.4
), we have

(4.43)

\int_{\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{Q}_{n}dx=\frac{(-i\delta)^{\mathfrak{p}(n)}}{i\delta}\sum_{n_{12}=n-1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}L_{n_{1}}L_{n_{2}}dx,

where
L_{n}=L^{\delta}_{n}
denotes the linear (in
v
) part of
the original microscopic conservation law
h_{n}
.
Proceeding as in (
3.27
), (
3.28
), and (
3.29
)
with (
4.43
), Lemma
4.4
, (
4.4
), and integration by parts (along with the
anti self-adjointness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
), we have

(4.44)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{Q}_{n}dx&=\frac{(-i\delta)^{\mathfrak{p}(n)}}{i\delta}\sum_{n_{12}=n-1}(-1)^{n_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}v(1-i\delta\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta})^{n_{1}}(1+i\delta\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta})^{n_{2}}\partial_{x}^{n-1}vdx\\
&=\frac{(-i\delta)^{\mathfrak{p}(n)}}{i\delta}\sum_{n_{12}=n-1}\sum_{m_{1}=0}^{n_{1}}\sum_{m_{2}=0}^{n_{2}}\binom{n_{1}}{m_{1}}\binom{n_{2}}{m_{2}}(-1)^{n_{1}+m_{1}}\\
&\hphantom{XXXXXXXXXXXXXX}\times(i\delta)^{m_{12}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}v\,\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{m_{12}}\partial_{x}^{n-1}vdx\\
&=(-1)^{\frac{n-2-\mathfrak{p}(n)}{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\mathfrak{p}(\ell)=\mathfrak{p}(n-1)\end{subarray}}^{n-1}\alpha_{n,\ell}\,\delta^{\ell-1+\mathfrak{p}(n)}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{\frac{\ell}{2}}v\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}},\end{split}

where, in the last step, we used the convention (
1.17
)
when
n
is even and
\ell
is odd.
Here,
the coefficient
\alpha_{n,\ell}
is given by

\alpha_{n,\ell}=\sum_{n_{12}=n-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq m_{1}\leq n_{1}\\
0\leq m_{2}\leq n_{2}\\
\ell=m_{12}\end{subarray}}\binom{n_{1}}{m_{1}}\binom{n_{2}}{m_{2}}(-1)^{n_{1}+m_{1}}.

By a change of variables (
\widetilde{n}_{1}=n_{1}-m_{1}
)
and (
4.42
) (with
m=\widetilde{n}_{1}+1
,
\ell=m_{1}
,
n=n-\widetilde{n}_{1}-\ell
, and
p=\ell
), we have

(4.45)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\alpha_{n,\ell}&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq n_{1}\leq n-1\\
0\leq m_{1}\leq n_{1}\\
0\leq\ell-m_{1}\leq n-1-n_{1}\end{subarray}}\binom{n_{1}}{m_{1}}\binom{n-1-n_{1}}{\ell-m_{1}}(-1)^{n_{1}+m_{1}}\\
&=\sum_{0\leq\widetilde{n}_{1}\leq n-1-\ell}(-1)^{\widetilde{n}_{1}}\sum_{m_{1}=0}^{\ell}\binom{\widetilde{n}_{1}+m_{1}}{m_{1}}\binom{n-1-\widetilde{n}_{1}-m_{1}}{\ell-m_{1}}\\
&=\binom{n}{\ell}\sum_{0\leq\widetilde{n}_{1}\leq n-1-\ell}(-1)^{\widetilde{n}_{1}}\\
&=\binom{n}{\ell},\end{split}

where we used the fact that, in view of (
3.25
), the contribution
after the second equality vanishes
when

\widetilde{n}_{1}>n-1-\ell

and that
n-1-\ell
is even under the condition
\mathfrak{p}(\ell)=\mathfrak{p}(n-1)
.
Therefore,
the identity
(
4.41
) follows from (
4.44
)
and (
4.45
).
∎

We conclude this subsection by introducing
the
shallow-water rank
, which plays an important role
in understanding the structure of the shallow-water conservation laws.
See
[
68
,
56
]
for a similar concept
in a simpler context of the KdV equation.

Definition 4.7
.

Given a monomial
p(v)
,
define
\#v
,
\#\partial_{x}
,
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
, and
\#\delta

by

\displaystyle\#v

\displaystyle=\#v(p(v))=\text{homogeneity of $p(v)$ in $v$},

\displaystyle\#\partial_{x}

\displaystyle=\#\partial_{x}(p(v))=\text{number of (explicit) appearance of $\partial_{x}$ in $p(v)$},

\displaystyle\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}

\displaystyle=\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(p(v))=\text{number of (explicit) appearance of $\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}$ in $p(v)$},

\displaystyle\#\delta

\displaystyle=\#\delta(p(v))=\text{number of (explicit) appearance of $\delta$ in $p(v)$}.

We then define the
shallow-water rank
of a monomial
p(v)
, denoted by
\ord(p)
, by setting

(4.46)

\displaystyle\ord(p)=\#v+\frac{1}{2}\big(\#\partial_{x}+\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}-\#\delta\big).

For simplicity, we refer to the shallow-water rank as the rank in the following.
If all monomials in a given polynomial
p(v)
have the same rank, say
n
,
then we say that the polynomial
p(v)
(and its integral over
\mathbb{T}
) has rank
n
.

From
(
4.5
) and (
4.36
),
we see that
both
h_{n}
and
\widetilde{h}_{n}

consist of monomials in
v
with
\partial_{x}
,
\delta
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
.
The following lemma characterizes the rank of the microscopic conservation laws
h_{n}
and
\widetilde{h}_{n}
for the scaled ILW (
1.5
) and of the KdV microscopic conservation laws
h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{n}
defined in (
4.20
)

Lemma 4.8
.

(i)
Given any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the shallow-water microscopic conservation law
h_{n}
defined in (
4.5
) has rank
1+\frac{1}{2}n
.
Moreover, each monomial in
h_{n}
satisfies

(4.47)

0\leq\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\leq\min(\#\partial_{x},\#\delta),\quad\#v+\#\partial_{x}\leq n+1,\quad\text{and}\quad 0\leq\#\delta\leq n.

(ii)
Given any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the KdV microscopic conservation law
h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{n}
defined in (
4.20
) has rank
1+\frac{1}{2}n
with
\#\delta=\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}=0
.

(iii)
Given any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
the new shallow-water microscopic conservation law
\widetilde{h}_{n}

defined in (
4.36
) has rank
2+\frac{1}{2}n-\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{p}(n)
,
where
\mathfrak{p}(n)
is as in (
4.37
).
Moreover, each monomial in
\int_{\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{h}_{n}dx
satisfies

(4.48)

\begin{split}&0\leq\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\leq\min(\#\partial_{x},\#\delta+1-\mathfrak{p}(n)),\quad\#v+\#\partial_{x}\leq n+1,\quad\text{and}\\
\quad&0\leq\#\delta\leq n+\mathfrak{p}(n)-2.\end{split}

Proof.

(i)
From (
4.5
) and (
4.4
)
with (
4.46
), we see that
the first claim holds for
n=0,1
.
Fix an integer
n\geq 2
and assume that the first claim holds for
h_{k}
with
0\leq k\leq n-1
.
Then, we see that
the summand (with fixed
j=2,\dots,n
) of
the first sum (in
j
) on the right-hand side of (
4.5
) has rank
j+\frac{1}{2}(n_{1}+\cdots+n_{j})-\frac{1}{2}(j-2)=1+\frac{1}{2}n
, where we used
n_{1}+\cdots+n_{j}=n-j
.
Similarly, the summand (with fixed
j=2,\dots,n+1
) of
the second sum (in
j
) on the right-hand side of (
4.5
) has rank

j+\frac{1}{2}(n_{1}+\cdots+n_{j})-\frac{1}{2}(j-1)=1+\frac{1}{2}n
,
where we used

n_{1}+\cdots+n_{j}=n+1-j
.
In view of (
4.4
)
with the inductive hypothesis,
the last term on the right-hand side of (
4.5
) also has rank
1+\frac{1}{2}n
.
Therefore, by induction, we conclude that
h_{n}
has rank
1+\frac{1}{2}n
.

The first bound in (
4.47
) follows from the fact that

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
comes with
\delta
and
\partial_{x}
in (
4.5
) through
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
defined in (
4.4
).
From (
4.5
), we see that the second and third bounds in
(
4.47
) hold when
n=0,1
.
For
n\geq 2
, the claim follows easily
from induction, using (
4.5
) with (
4.4
).

(ii) While this result was shown in
[
68
]
,
we present a proof for readers’ convenience.
From (
4.20
),
we see that the claim holds for
n=0,1
.
Fix an integer
n\geq 2
and assume that the claim holds for
h_{k}^{\text{\rm KdV}}
with
0\leq k\leq n-1
.
Then,
by the inductive hypothesis, we see that
all monomials
h_{n_{1}}^{\text{\rm KdV}}h_{n_{2}}^{\text{\rm KdV}}
(with
n_{1}+n_{2}=n-2)
and
\partial_{x}h_{n-1}^{\text{\rm KdV}}

in (
4.20
)
have rank
1+\frac{1}{2}n
.
Therefore, the claim follows from induction.

(iii)
From from (
4.36
), (
4.46
), and Part (i),
we easily see that

\widetilde{h}_{n}
has rank
2+\frac{1}{2}n-\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{p}(n)
.
From Lemma
4.5
with (
4.4
), we have

(4.49)

\int_{\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{h}_{n}dx=-\frac{(-i\delta)^{\mathfrak{p}(n)}}{2i\delta^{3}}\sum_{j=2}^{n+1}\frac{(2i\delta)^{j}}{j!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\ldots,n_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
n_{1\cdots j}=n+1-j\end{subarray}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}h_{n_{1}}\ldots h_{n_{j}}dx.

The first two bounds in (
4.48
) follow
from (
4.49
) and (
4.47
).
The upper bound

\#\delta\leq n+\mathfrak{p}(n)-2

follows from (
4.36
)
and (
4.47
).

It remains to show
\#\delta\geq 0
.
When
n
is odd,

\#\delta\geq 0

follows
from (
4.49
) and (
4.47
).
When
n
is even,
the contribution from
j\geq 3
in (
4.49
) satisfies
\#\delta\geq 0
.
It remains to consider the contribution from
j=2
in (
4.49
).
In view of the third bound in (
4.47
),
it remains to consider

(4.50)

\delta^{-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
n_{12}=n-1\end{subarray}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}h_{n_{1},0}h_{n_{2},0}dx,

where
h_{n,0}=h_{n,0}^{\delta}

denotes the coefficient of
\delta^{0}
in
h_{n}
.
23
23

23

Note that
h_{n,0}
can still depend on
\delta
through
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
.
In fact, we show that (
4.50
) vanishes.
From (
4.5
) with (
4.4
), we obtain the following recurrence relation for
h_{n,0}
:

(4.51)

\displaystyle h_{0,0}

\displaystyle=-v,

\displaystyle h_{1,0}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{x}v,

\displaystyle h_{n,0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
n_{12}=n-2\end{subarray}}h_{n_{1},0}h_{n_{2},0}+\partial_{x}h_{n-1,0}.

By comparing (
4.51
) and (
4.20
), we see that

(4.52)

h_{n,0}=h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{n}

for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
In particular, from (
4.50
), (
4.51
), and (
4.52
), we have

\displaystyle\eqref{saux3}=\delta^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{n+1}dx

which vanishes in view of
Lemma
4.3
(i) since
n+1
is odd.
∎

4.3.
2-to-1 collapse of the shallow-water conservation laws

In this subsection,
we construct shallow-water conservation laws and prove their convergence to the corresponding KdV conservation laws,
thus establishing Theorem
1.4
.

As in Definition
3.7
,
we introduce the classes
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n}(v)
of (monic) monomials in
v
, adapted to the
current shallow-water setting.

Definition 4.9
.

Let
v\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
with
\int_{\mathbb{T}}vdx=0
. Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we defined the classes
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n}(v)
of monomials by setting

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(v)

\displaystyle=\Big\{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{\alpha}\partial_{x}^{\beta}v:\,\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\Big\},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(v)

\displaystyle=\Big\{\big[\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{x}^{\beta_{1}}v\big]\big[\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{\alpha_{2}}\partial_{x}^{\beta_{2}}v\big]:\,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\Big\},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n}(v)

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{\alpha_{\ell}}\partial_{x}^{\beta_{\ell}}p_{j_{\ell}}(v):\,k\in\{2,\ldots,n\},\ j_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N},\ j_{1\cdots k}=n,

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102ptp_{j_{\ell}}(v)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j_{\ell}}(v),\ \alpha_{\ell},\beta_{\ell}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\bigg\}.

Given a monomial
p(v)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n}(v)
, we define its fundamental form
\widetilde{p}(v)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n}(v)
which is obtained by dropping all the
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
operators appearing in
p(v)
.
Moreover, given a monomial
p(v)

with the fundamental form of the form:

\widetilde{p}(v)=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{x}^{\beta_{j}}v,

we let
|p(u)|
denote the maximum number of derivatives on each factor by setting

\displaystyle|p(v)|=\max_{j=1,\ldots,n}\beta_{j}.

Given a monomial
p(v)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{n}(v)
,
we also define its semi-fundamental form

\overline{p}(v)
,
obtained
by dropping all the
\delta\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
operators
appearing in
p(v)

(but keeping
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
unpaired with
\delta
).
We use semi-fundamental forms
only in Subsection
7.3
.

We are now ready to define the shallow-water conservation laws
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)
and
prove their shallow-water convergence.
Theorem
1.4
follows from Propositions
4.10
and
4.11
.
Recall that we are working under the mean-zero assumption on
v

throughout the paper.

Proposition 4.10
.

(i)
Let
0<\delta<\infty
.
Define
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, by setting

(4.53)

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{0}(v)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{h}_{1}^{\delta}dx,

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\kappa+1}\frac{3}{4\kappa}\Re\int_{\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{h}^{\delta}_{2\kappa}dx,

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\kappa+1}\frac{1}{2}\Re\int_{\mathbb{T}}\widetilde{h}^{\delta}_{2\kappa+1}dx

for
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\widetilde{h}_{n}^{\delta}
is the microscopic conservation law defined in (
4.36
).
Then,
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)
is conserved under the flow of the scaled ILW (
1.5
).
Moreover, for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)
satisfies
the following statements.

(i.a)
When
k=2\kappa-1
,
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
, is odd,

(i.a.1)

the odd-order shallow-water conservation law

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)
has rank
\kappa+2
in the sense of Definition
4.7
,
where each monomial in
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)
satisfies

(4.54)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&0\leq\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\leq\min(\#\partial_{x},\#\delta+1),\quad\#v+\#\partial_{x}\leq 2\kappa+1,\quad\text{and}\\
&0\leq\#\delta\leq 2\kappa-2,\end{split}

(i.a.2)

the quadratic
(
in
v
)
part of
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)
is given by

(4.55)

\frac{3}{4\kappa}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=1\\
\textup{odd}\end{subarray}}^{2\kappa-1}\binom{2\kappa}{\ell}\delta^{\ell-1}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{\frac{\ell}{2}}v\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}},

where we used the convention (
1.17
).

(i.b)
When
k=2\kappa
,
\kappa\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, is even,

(i.b.1)

the even-order shallow-water conservation law

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)
has rank
\kappa+2
in the sense of Definition
4.7
,
where each monomial in
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)
satisfies

(4.56)

\begin{split}&0\leq\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\leq\min(\#\partial_{x},\#\delta),\quad\#v+\#\partial_{x}\leq 2\kappa+2,\quad\text{and}\\
&0\leq\#\delta\leq 2\kappa,\end{split}

(i.b.2)

the quadratic
(
in
v
)
part of
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)
is given by

(4.57)

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\textup{even}\end{subarray}}^{2\kappa}\binom{2\kappa+1}{\ell}\delta^{\ell}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{\frac{\ell}{2}}v\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\kappa}}.

In particular, we have

(4.58)

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{0}(v)=\frac{1}{2}\|v\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

and, defining
\widetilde{a}_{k,\ell}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, by

(4.59)

\widetilde{a}_{2\kappa-1,\ell}=\frac{3}{2\kappa}\binom{2\kappa}{\ell}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widetilde{a}_{2\kappa,\ell}=\binom{2\kappa+1}{\ell},

the conservation law
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)
in (
4.53
) reduces to the form (
1.16
)
with (
1.18
).

(ii)
Given
\kappa\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we obtain the following conservation laws for KdV (
1.6
):

(4.60)

\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v)=(-1)^{\kappa}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{2\kappa+2}(v)dx,

where
h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{n}
is the microscopic conservation law for KdV defined in (
4.20
). Moreover,

(ii.a)

\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v)
has rank
\kappa+2
with
\#\delta=\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}=0
,

(ii.b)

\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v)
is of the form (
1.19
).

Proof.

The conservation of
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)

under the flow of the scaled ILW (
1.5
)
follows from (
4.36
) and (
4.18
).
Similarly,
the conservation of
\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v)

under the flow of KdV (
1.6
)
follows from (
4.21
).
The claims (i.a.1), (i.b.1), and (ii.a)
follow from
Lemma
4.8
.

The claim (
4.57
) for
\kappa=0
, namely (
4.58
),
follows from
(
4.39
) with (
4.4
).
More generally, the claims (i.a.2) and (i.b.2) follow
from Lemma
4.6
.
Lastly, the claim in (ii.b) follows from
Lemma
4.3
(ii).
∎

Proposition 4.11
.

Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
0<\delta<\infty
, we have

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)=\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil}(v)+\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v),

where

\lceil x\rceil
denotes
the smallest integer greater than or equal to
x
,
and

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)
and
\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil}(v)
are as in (
4.53
) and (
4.60
), respectively.
Here,
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)
consists of the terms in
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)

which depend on
\delta
in an explicit manner and those with
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
is replaced by
the perturbation operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\delta}=\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}+\frac{1}{3}\partial_{x}

defined in (
1.21
).
Moreover, for any
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
and
v\in H^{\kappa}(\mathbb{T})
,
we have

(4.61)

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)=0,

namely

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)=\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v).

In the deep-water case (Proposition
3.8
),
the deep-water convergence followed from an explicit power of
\delta^{-1}
;
see (
3.55
).
In the current shallow-water setting,
the situation is more subtle
due
to the slower convergence of the perturbation operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\delta}
in (
1.21
)
and also insufficient powers of
\delta
(tending to
0
),
and thus we need to proceed with more care.

Proof of Proposition
4.11
.

Let
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},0}(v)
be the
\delta
-free part of
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)
defined in (
4.53
), namely the collection of all terms that do not depend on
\delta
explicitly and
with
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
replaced by
-\frac{1}{3}\partial_{x}
;
24
24

24

This latter process of replacing
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}

by
-\frac{1}{3}\partial_{x}
is relevant only when
k
is odd.
When
k=2\kappa
is even, it follows from (
4.56
)
that

\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\leq\#\delta
holds for any monomial in
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)

and thus any monomial with
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\geq 1

(which implies
\#\delta\geq 1
) is removed
in constructing the
\delta
-free part.

see also Remark
2.5
.
Then, we define
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)
by

(4.62)

\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)=\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)-\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},0}(v),

where all the terms in
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)
involve at least one power of
\delta
or
the perturbation operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\delta}=\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}+\frac{1}{3}\partial_{x}
.

Part 1:

In this part, we prove (
4.61
).
In the following, we assume
0<\delta\leq 1
.

\bullet
Case A:

k=2\kappa-1
is odd.

From Proposition
4.10
(i.a), we see that
any monomial in
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)
satisfies

(4.63)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\#v+\frac{1}{2}(\#\partial_{x}+\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}-\#\delta)=\kappa+2,\quad\#v\geq 2,

\displaystyle 0\leq\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\leq\min(\#\partial_{x},\#\delta+1),\quad\#v+\#\partial_{x}\leq 2\kappa+1,\quad\text{and}\quad 0\leq\#\delta\leq 2\kappa-2,

where we ignore the extra
\partial_{x}
operator in
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\delta}=\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}+\frac{1}{3}\partial_{x}
in determining
\#\partial_{x}

but count occurrences of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\delta}
in
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
.
With our perturbative viewpoint (
1.21
), we set

\displaystyle\#\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\delta}

\displaystyle=\#\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\delta}(p(v))=\text{number of appearance of $\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\delta}$ in $p(v)$}.

Then,
from (
4.62
)
and the definition of the
\delta
-free part

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}(v)
,
we also have

(4.64)

\text{(a) }\#\delta\geq 1,\quad\text{or}\quad\text{(b) }\#\delta=0,\quad\#\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\delta}=1,

where the terms satisfying (b) arise from those in
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)
with no powers of
\delta

(which in particular implies
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\leq 1
in view of (
4.63
))
and one instance of
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
, which we rewrite as
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}=-\frac{1}{3}\partial_{x}+\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\delta}
with the first contribution going into
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}(v)

and the latter going into
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)
.
Thus, each monomial in
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)
is of the form
\delta^{m}\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(v)dx
for some

0\leq m\leq 2\kappa-2
and
p(v)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}(v)
with
j=\#v\in\{2,\ldots,2\kappa+1\}
.
Then, after integration by parts,
we have

(4.65)

\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)=\sum_{m=0}^{2\kappa-2}\sum_{j=2}^{\kappa+2+\frac{1}{2}m}\sum_{\ell=0}^{2\kappa+1-j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p(v)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}(v)\\
\text{\eqref{EEodd1}-\eqref{EEodd1a} hold}\\
\#\partial_{x}(p)=\ell\\
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(p)\leq\#\delta+1=m+1\\
|p(v)|\leq\kappa\end{subarray}}c(p)\delta^{m}\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(v)dx.

Fix
p(v)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}(v)
with
|p(v)|\leq\kappa
appearing on the right-hand side of (
4.65
). Let
\widetilde{p}(v)=\prod_{k=1}^{j}\partial_{x}^{\beta_{j}}v
denote the fundamental form of the monomial
p(v)
,
where
\beta_{1}+\cdots+\beta_{j}\leq 2\kappa+1-j
and
0\leq\beta_{k}\leq\kappa
.
We note from (
4.63
) with
\#v\geq 2
that there exists at most one
k
such that
\beta_{k}=\kappa
.

\bullet
Subcase A.1:

m=\#\delta\geq 1
and
\widetilde{m}=\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\leq\#\delta
.

From (
2.11
)
in Lemma
2.4
with
0<\delta\leq 1
, we have

(4.66)

|\delta^{m}\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{\widetilde{m}}}(n)|\lesssim\delta^{m}\min\Big(\frac{1}{\delta},|n|\Big)^{m}\leq\min(1,\delta|n|)\leq\delta|n|

for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.
Note from (
4.63
) that
\#\partial_{x}\leq 2\kappa-1
. Consequently, accounting for the loss of
one derivative
from estimating
\delta^{m}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{\widetilde{m}}
in (
4.66
),

p(v)
has at most
2\kappa
derivatives in total.
Hence, by integration by parts, we can reduce it to the form where there are at most two factors of
v
with
\kappa
derivatives, while the remaining factors have at most
\kappa-1<\kappa-\frac{1}{2}
derivatives.
Then,
by
viewing the product on the physical side as
an iterated convolution on the Fourier side
and applying
Young’s inequality
and
Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality,
we have

(4.67)

\begin{split}\bigg|\delta^{m}\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(v)dx\bigg|&\lesssim\delta\big(1+\|v\|_{H^{\kappa}}\big)^{2}\big(1+\|v\|_{\mathcal{F}L^{\kappa-1,1}}\big)^{2\kappa-1}\lesssim\delta\big(1+\|v\|_{H^{\kappa}}\big)^{2\kappa+1}\\
&\longrightarrow 0,\end{split}

as
\delta\to 0
,
where the
\mathcal{F}L^{s,r}
-norm is as in (
3.53
).

\bullet
Subcase A.2:

m=\#\delta\geq 1
,
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}=\#\delta+1
, and
j=\#v\geq 3
.

From (
2.11
)
in Lemma
2.4
with
0<\delta\leq 1
, we have

(4.68)

|\delta^{m}\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{m+1}}(n)|\leq\delta^{m}\min\Big(\frac{1}{\delta},|n|\Big)^{m+1}\leq\delta|n|^{2}

for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.
Note from (
4.63
) that
\#\partial_{x}\leq 2\kappa+1-j\leq 2\kappa-2
. Thus, accounting for the loss of 2 derivatives from (
4.68
), we have at most
2\kappa
derivatives in total.
Hence, by proceeding as in Subcase A.1,
we obtain (
4.67
).

\bullet
Subcase A.3:

m=\#\delta\geq 1
,
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}=\#\delta+1
, and
j=\#v=2
.

Since
\delta^{m}p(v)
is quadratic in
v
with
\#\delta\geq 1
,
it follows from
(
4.55
) in Proposition
4.10
that the collection of such
\delta^{m}p(v)
is given by

(4.69)

P(v)=\frac{3}{4\kappa}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=3\\
\text{odd}\end{subarray}}^{2\kappa-1}\binom{2\kappa}{\ell}\delta^{\ell-1}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{\frac{\ell}{2}}v\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}}.

From (
2.11
) in Lemma
2.4
, we have

(4.70)

|\delta^{\ell-1}\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{\ell}}(n)n^{2\kappa-1}|\lesssim\begin{cases}\eta^{\ell-1}|n|^{2\kappa},&\text{for }\delta|n|\leq\eta,\\
|n|^{2\kappa},&\text{for }\delta|n|>\eta\end{cases}

for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
and
\eta>0
.
Therefore, from (
4.69
) and (
4.70
)
and choosing
\eta=\sqrt{\delta}
,
it follows from the dominated convergence theorem that

(4.71)

\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{T}}P(v)dx\bigg|\lesssim\delta\|v\|^{2}_{H^{\kappa}}+\sum_{|n|>\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}}|n|^{2\kappa}|\widehat{v}(n)|^{2}\longrightarrow 0,

as
\delta\to 0
.

\bullet
Subcase A.4:

m=\#\delta=0
and
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\delta}=1
.

In this case, we can write
p(v)
as

p(v)=\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\delta}\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{j_{0}}\partial_{x}^{\beta_{k}}v\bigg)\cdot\prod_{k=j_{0}+1}^{j}\partial_{x}^{\beta_{k}}v

for some
2\leq j\leq\kappa+2
with
1\leq j_{0}\leq j
,
where
\beta_{1}+\cdots+\beta_{j}\leq 2\kappa+1-j

and
0\leq\beta_{k}\leq\kappa
for any
k=1,\dots,j

with
at most one
k
such that
\beta_{k}=\kappa
.
From Lemma
2.3
with (
1.21
), we have

|\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\delta}}(n)|=\frac{1}{3}|n||\mathfrak{h}(\delta,n)|
,
where
\mathfrak{h}
is as in (
2.8
).
Under
n_{1\dots j}=0
,
it follows from the triangle inequality that

\max_{k=1,\dots,j}|n_{k}|\sim n^{*}_{2}
,
where
n^{*}_{2}
denotes the second largest in
|n_{k}|
,
k=1,\dots,j
.
Then, by (
2.8
)
and
Young’s
and Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequalities,
we have

(4.72)

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(v)dx\bigg|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\ldots,n_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\\
n_{1\cdots j}=0\end{subarray}}\max_{k=1,\dots,j}|n_{k}|\cdot|\mathfrak{h}(\delta,n_{1\cdots j_{0}})|\prod_{k=1}^{j}|n_{k}|^{\beta_{k}}|\widehat{v}(n_{k})|

\displaystyle\sim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},\ldots,n_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\\
n_{1\cdots j}=0\end{subarray}}n^{*}_{2}\cdot|\mathfrak{h}(\delta,n_{1\cdots j_{0}})|\prod_{k=1}^{j}|n_{k}|^{\beta_{k}}|\widehat{v}(n_{k})|

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+\|v\|_{H^{\kappa}})^{\kappa+2},

uniformly in
0<\delta<\infty
,
where, in the last step,
we used the fact that there exists at most one
k
such that
\beta_{k}=\kappa
.
Moreover, from the pointwise (in
n
) bound
and convergence of
\mathfrak{h}
in (
2.9
) and the dominated convergence theorem,
we conclude that

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\eqref{EEodd5a}=0
.

Therefore, putting all the subcases together, we obtain

(4.73)

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)=0.

\bullet

Case B:
k=2\kappa
is even.

From Proposition
4.10
(i.b), we see that
any monomial in
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)
satisfies

(4.74)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\#v+\frac{1}{2}(\#\partial_{x}+\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}-\#\delta)=\kappa+2,\quad\#v\geq 2,

\displaystyle 0\leq\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\leq\min(\#\partial_{x},\#\delta),\quad\#v+\#\partial_{x}\leq 2\kappa+2,\quad\text{and}\quad 1\leq\#\delta\leq 2\kappa,

where we used the fact that the terms with
\#\delta=0

(which implies
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}=0
in view of (
4.56
))
are included in the
\delta
-free part
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa,0}(v)
.
Thus, each monomial in
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)
is of the form
\delta^{m}\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(v)dx
for some
1\leq m\leq 2\kappa
and
p(v)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}(v)
with
j=\#v\in\{2,\ldots,2\kappa+2\}
.
Then, after integration by parts,
we have

(4.75)

\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)=\sum_{m=1}^{2\kappa}\sum_{j=2}^{\kappa+2+\frac{1}{2}m}\sum_{\ell=0}^{2\kappa+2-j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p(v)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}(v)\\
\eqref{EEaux1}\text{ holds}\\
\#\partial_{x}(p)=\ell\\
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(p)\leq m\\
|p(v)|\leq\kappa\end{subarray}}c(p)\delta^{m}\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(v)dx.

Fix
p(v)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}(v)
with
|p(v)|\leq\kappa
appearing on the right-hand side of (
4.75
). Let
\widetilde{p}(v)=\prod_{k=1}^{j}\partial_{x}^{\beta_{j}}v
denote the fundamental form of the monomial
p(v)
, where
0\leq\beta_{k}\leq\kappa
and
\beta_{1}+\cdots+\beta_{j}\leq 2\kappa+2-j
.
Note that, unlike Case A,
there can be two
k
’s such that
\beta_{k}=\kappa
.

\bullet
Subcase B.1:

m=\#\delta\geq\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}+1
.

Proceeding as in (
4.67
)
with (
4.66
),
(but using the upper bound by
1
in (
4.66
)), we have

(4.76)

\bigg|\delta^{m}\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(v)dx\bigg|\lesssim\delta\big(1+\|v\|_{H^{\kappa}}\big)^{2\kappa+2}\longrightarrow 0,

as
\delta\to 0
.

\bullet
Subcase B.2:

m=\#\delta=\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\geq 1
and
j=\#v\geq 3
.

By noting that at most one factor of
v
in
p(v)
has
\kappa
derivatives,
we can proceed as in Subcase A.1
and obtain (
4.76
).

\bullet
Subcase B.3:

m=\#\delta=\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\geq 1
and
j=\#v=2
.

Since
\delta^{m}p(v)
is quadratic in
v
with
\#\delta\geq 1
,
it follows from
(
4.57
) in Proposition
4.10
that the collection of such
\delta^{m}p(v)
is given by

(4.77)

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m=2\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{2\kappa}\binom{2\kappa+1}{m}\delta^{m}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{\frac{m}{2}}v\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\kappa}}.

From (
2.11
) in Lemma
2.4
, we have

(4.78)

|\delta^{m}\widehat{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{m}}(n)n^{2\kappa}|\lesssim\begin{cases}\eta^{m}|n|^{2\kappa},&\text{for }\delta|n|\leq\eta,\\
|n|^{2\kappa},&\text{for }\delta|n|>\eta\end{cases}

for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
and
\eta>0
.
Therefore, from (
4.77
) and (
4.78
), and choosing
\eta=\sqrt{\delta}
,
it follows from the dominated convergence theorem that

\delta^{m}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{\frac{m}{2}}v\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\kappa}}\leq\delta\|v\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\kappa}}+\sum_{|n|>\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}}|n|^{2\kappa}|\widehat{v}(n)|^{2}\longrightarrow 0,

as
\delta\to 0
.

Therefore, putting all the subcases together, we obtain

(4.79)

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)=0.

From (
4.73
)
and (
4.79
), we obtain
(
4.61
).

Part 2:

It remains to show

(4.80)

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}(v)=\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa,0}(v)=\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v).

We first consider
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa,0}(v)
.
In this case, from
(
4.53
) and (
4.38
)
in Lemma
4.5
, we have

(4.81)

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa,0}(v)=(-1)^{\kappa}\frac{1}{2}\Re\sum_{n_{12}=2\kappa}\int_{\mathbb{T}}h_{n_{1},0}h_{n_{2},0}dx,

where

h_{n,0}
denotes the coefficient of
\delta^{0}
in
h_{n}
.
Then, from
(
4.81
), (
4.52
), (
4.20
), and (
4.60
), we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa,0}(v)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\kappa}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n_{12}=2\kappa}\int_{\mathbb{T}}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{n_{1}}h^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{n_{2}}dx=(-1)^{\kappa}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}h_{2\kappa+2}^{\text{\rm KdV}}dx

\displaystyle=\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v),

which establishes (
4.80
) for
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa,0}(v)
.

Next, we show (
4.80
) for
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}(v)
.
Since
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}(v)

is the
\delta
-free part of
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)
,
it follows from (
4.55
) that
the quadratic part in
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}(v)
is given by

\frac{3}{4\kappa}\binom{2\kappa}{1}\frac{1}{3}\|v\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\kappa}}=\frac{1}{2}\|v\|_{\dot{H}^{\kappa}}^{2},

which agrees with the quadratic part of
E^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v)
in (
1.19
).
Suppose for now that
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}(v)
is conserved for KdV (
1.6
).
Then, we can conclude
(
4.80
) for
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}(v)

from the fact that
any polynomial conservation law under KdV
with
\|v\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\kappa}}
as its leading order quadratic part can be written as a linear combination of the conservation laws
\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{k}(v)
for
k=0,\ldots,\kappa

defined in (
4.60
);
see, for example,
[
59
, Theorem 2.16]
.

In the following, we show that

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}(v)
is indeed conserved under KdV (
1.6
).
Let
v_{0}\in H^{\kappa}(\mathbb{T})
and
0<\delta\leq 1
. From Lemma
8.2
,
there exists
T>0
, independent of
0<\delta\leq 1
, and unique solutions
v^{\delta},v^{\text{\rm KdV}}\in C([-T,T];H^{\kappa}(\mathbb{T}))

to the scaled ILW and KdV, respectively, with
v^{\delta}|_{t=0}=v^{\text{\rm KdV}}|_{t=0}=v_{0}
such that

(4.82)

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\|v^{\delta}-v^{\text{\rm KdV}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\kappa}_{x}}=0

and

(4.83)

\displaystyle\|v^{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\kappa}_{x}}\leq\|v^{\text{\rm KdV}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\kappa}_{x}}+1

for any
0<\delta\leq 1
.

From (
4.62
) and the conservation of
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}
under the scaled ILW,
we can write

(4.84)

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}(v^{\text{\rm KdV}}(t))-\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}(v_{0})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}(v^{\text{\rm KdV}}(t))-\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v_{0})+\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v_{0})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}(v^{\text{\rm KdV}}(t))-\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v^{\delta}(t))+\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v_{0})

\displaystyle=\Big(\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}(v^{\text{\rm KdV}}(t))-\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}(v^{\delta}(t))\Big)-\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v^{\delta}(t))+\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v_{0}).

From Proposition
4.10
(i.a)
with Definitions
4.7
and
4.9
,
we see that any monomial in

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}(v)

is of the form
\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(v)dx
for some
p(v)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}(v)
for
j=2,\ldots,\kappa+2
,
satisfying
25
25

25

Here, before considering the
\delta
-free part,
it is possible to have
\#\delta(p)=0
but
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(p)=1
under (
4.54
).
Then, in constructing the
\delta
-free part, we replaced
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
by
-\frac{1}{3}\partial_{x}

which increased
\#\partial_{x}
by 1.
This explains the upper bound
2\kappa+2
in (
4.85
).

(4.85)

\#\delta(p)=\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(p)=0,\quad\#v+\#\partial_{x}\leq 2\kappa+2,\quad\text{and}\quad|p(v)|\leq\kappa,

where the last bound follows from the fact that

\#v\geq 2
(which implies
\#\partial_{x}\leq 2\kappa
)
and integration by parts.
Thus, there exist at most two factors of
v
with
\kappa
derivatives, while the remaining factors have at most
\kappa-1<\kappa-\frac{1}{2}
.
Moreover, by using the multilinearity of the polynomials
p(v)

and applying
Young’s inequality
and
Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality
on the Fourier side as in (
4.67
),
(
4.83
), and (
4.82
), we have

(4.86)

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}(v^{\text{\rm KdV}}(t))-\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}(v^{\delta}(t))|&\lesssim\big(1+\|v^{\text{\rm KdV}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\kappa}_{x}}\big)^{\kappa+1}\|v^{\delta}-v^{\text{\rm KdV}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\kappa}_{x}}\\
&\longrightarrow 0,\end{split}

as
\delta\to 0
.

From (
4.61
), we have

(4.87)

|\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v_{0})|\longrightarrow 0,

as
\delta\to 0
.
We point out that, by a slight modification of the argument in Part 1 leading to (
4.61
),
we also have

(4.88)

|\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v^{\delta}(t))|\longrightarrow 0,

as
\delta\to 0
.
Indeed, for the contributions from monomials,
satisfying the assumptions
of Subcase A.1 and A.2,
it follows
from
(
4.67
) (with
v
replaced by
v^{\delta}(t)
)
with
the uniform bound (
4.83
)
that

(4.89)

\displaystyle\bigg|\delta^{m}\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(v^{\delta}(t))dx\bigg|\longrightarrow 0,

as
\delta\to 0
.
In
Subcase A.3,
we used the dominated convergence theorem,
and thus more care is needed.
Let
P(v)
be as in Subcase A.3 above.
Then, by writing

(4.90)

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{T}}P(v^{\delta}(t))dx\bigg|\leq\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{T}}P(v^{\delta}(t))-P(v(t))dx\bigg|+\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{T}}P(v(t))dx\bigg|,

it follows from
(
4.71
)
that the second term on the right-hand side of (
4.90
) tends to
0

as
\delta\to 0
.
As for the first term,
it follows from (
4.82
) and (
4.83
) that

(4.91)

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{T}}P(v^{\delta}(t))-P(v(t))dx\bigg|\lesssim\big(\|v^{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\kappa}_{x}}+\|v\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\kappa}_{x}}\big)\|v^{\delta}-v\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{\kappa}_{x}}\longrightarrow 0,

as
\delta\to 0
.
For the remaining cases, namely
Subcase A.4,
the required modification is analogous to that for Subcase A.3 discussed above,
and thus we omit details.
Hence, (
4.88
) follows from (
4.89
),
(
4.90
), and (
4.91
).

Lastly,
recalling that, by definition,
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}
is independent of
\delta
,
it follows from (
4.84
), (
4.86
), (
4.87
), and (
4.88
) that

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}(v^{\text{\rm KdV}}(t))=\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}(v_{0})

for any
|t|\leq T
.
This shows that
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2},0}(v)
is conserved under KdV
and thus must be equal to
E^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v)

since they have the same quadratic part.
This concludes
the proof of Proposition
4.11
.
∎

4.4.
Structure of the shallow-water conservation laws

We conclude this section by stating the final structure of the shallow-water conservation laws
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)
defined in (
4.53
).

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Then, from
(the proofs of) Propositions
4.10

and
4.11
, we see that the shallow-water conservation law
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)
defined in (
4.53
) is indeed given by (
1.16
):

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{0}(v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|v\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=1\\
\text{odd}\end{subarray}}^{2\kappa-1}\widetilde{a}_{2\kappa-1,\ell}\,\delta^{\ell-1}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{\frac{\ell}{2}}v\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}}+\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v),

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{2\kappa}\widetilde{a}_{2\kappa,\ell}\,\delta^{\ell}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}^{\frac{\ell}{2}}v\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\kappa}}+\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v),

for
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
with
\widetilde{a}_{k,\ell}
as in (
4.59
).
Here, the interaction potentials

\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)
and
\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)
, satisfying
\#v\geq 3
, are
given by

(4.92)

\displaystyle\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{2\kappa-2}\sum_{j=3}^{\kappa+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p(v)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}(v)\\
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(p)=m+1\leq\#\partial_{x}(p)\\
\#\partial_{x}(p)=2\kappa+3-2j\\
|p(v)|\leq\kappa-1\end{subarray}}c(p)\delta^{m}\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(v)dx

\displaystyle+\sum_{m=0}^{2\kappa-2}\sum_{j=3}^{\kappa+2+\frac{1}{2}m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p(v)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}(v)\\
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(p)\leq\min(m,\#\partial_{x}(p))\\
\#\partial_{x}(p)\leq 2\kappa+1-j\\
|p(v)|\leq\kappa-1\end{subarray}}c(p)\delta^{m}\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(v)dx,

\displaystyle\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{2\kappa}\sum_{j=3}^{\kappa+2+\frac{1}{2}m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p(v)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}(v)\\
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(p)\leq\min(m,\#\partial_{x}(p))\\
\#\partial_{x}(p)\leq 2\kappa+2-j\\
|p(v)|\leq\kappa\end{subarray}}c(p)\delta^{m}\int_{\mathbb{T}}p(v)dx

with suitable constants
c(p)\in\mathbb{R}
, independent of
\delta
,
where the condition
\#\partial_{x}(p)=2\kappa+3-2j

in the first sum comes from
the fact that

\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\kappa-\frac{1}{2}}(v)
has rank
\kappa+2
with

\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}=\#\delta+1
.

5.
Deep-water generalized Gibbs measures

In this section, we
study the construction and convergence properties
of the generalized Gibbs measures in the deep-water regime,
claimed in
Theorem
1.6
.
As pointed out in
[
64
]
,
the main observation is the following:

(i)

In order to construct the limiting generalized Gibbs measure
\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

for each fixed
0<\delta\leq\infty

(Theorem
1.6
(i)),
it suffices to prove an

L^{p}
-integrability of
the truncated density
F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)
,
uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
but for each fixed
0<\delta\leq\infty
.

(ii)

In order to prove convergence of the generalized Gibbs measures
in the deep-water limit (Theorem
1.6
(ii)),
we need to establish
an

L^{p}
-integrability of
the truncated density
F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)
,
uniformly in

both

N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta\gg 1
.

Here, we need to study an
L^{p}
-integrability of
the truncated density
with respect to the Gaussian measure
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
in (
1.25
)
which is different for different values of
\delta
.
In order to establish a uniform (in
\delta
) bound,
it is therefore more convenient to work with the associated Gaussian process
X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

in (
1.26
)
and the underlying probability measure
\mathbb{P}
on
\Omega
.

The main tool for proving such a uniform (in
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta\gg 1
)

L^{p}
-integrability bound
is a variational approach; see Subsection
5.2
,
where the structural property
of the deep-water conservation laws
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

established in
Section
3
plays a crucial role.
Another key ingredient in establishing
convergence
of the deep-water generalized Gibbs measures
\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

is the corresponding convergence property of
the associated Gaussian measures
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
.
In Subsection
5.1
, we carry out detailed analysis
on
the Gaussian measures
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
; see Proposition
5.1
.

In Subsection
5.3
,
we present a proof of Theorem
1.6
(i)
on the construction of the deep-water generalized Gibbs measure for fixed
0<\delta\leq\infty
.
We then present
a proof of Theorem
1.6
(ii)
on deep-water convergence of the generalized Gibbs measure
in Subsection
5.4
.

In the following,
we
often suppress the dependence on
k
and
K

for various constants.
We apply this convention in the remaining part of this paper.
Recall also our convention for using

\varepsilon>0
to denote an arbitrarily small constant,
where we suppress
\varepsilon
-dependence of various constants.

5.1.
Equivalence of the base Gaussian measures

Given

0<\delta\leq\infty
and

k\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

be the Gaussian measure defined in (
1.25
) (and (
1.28
) when
\delta=\infty
).
Recall that

\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
is the induced probability measure
under the map
\omega\in\Omega\mapsto X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\omega)
,
where
X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
is as in (
1.26
).
From Lemma
2.2
with (
1.27
) and (
1.8
), we have

(5.1)

\displaystyle 0\leq T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)\sim_{\delta}|n|^{k}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{\delta\to\infty}T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)=T_{\infty,\frac{k}{2}}(n)=|n|^{k}

for any
0<\delta\leq\infty
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
,
where the implicit constant in the first bound is independent of
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
With (
5.1
), one can easily show that

X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
belongs to

W^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon,r}(\mathbb{T})\setminus W^{\frac{k-1}{2},r}(\mathbb{T})
for any
1\leq r\leq\infty
, almost surely.
The following proposition
establishes regularity, equivalence, and convergence properties
of the Gaussian measure
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

and the associated random variable

X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
,
which generalizes
Proposition 3.1 in
[
64
]

on the
k=1
case; see also
Proposition 3.4 in
[
64
]
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, the following statements hold.

(i)

Given any
0<\delta\leq\infty
,
finite
p\geq 1
, and
1\leq r\leq\infty
,
the sequence
\{\mathbf{P}_{N}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

converges
to
X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
in
L^{p}(\Omega;W^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon,r}(\mathbb{T}))

as
N\to\infty
.
Moreover, given any finite
p\geq 1

and
1\leq r\leq\infty
,
there exist
C_{\delta}=C_{\delta}(p,r)>0
and
\theta>0
such that

(5.2)

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\Big\|\|\mathbf{P}_{N}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}\|_{W^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon,r}_{x}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle<C_{\delta}<\infty,

(5.3)

\displaystyle\sup_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\Big\|\|\mathbf{P}_{M}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}-\mathbf{P}_{N}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}\|_{W^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon,r}_{x}}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\delta}}{N^{\theta}}

for any
M\geq N\geq 1
,
where the constant
C_{\delta}
is independent of
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
In particular, the rate of convergence is uniform in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.

(ii)

Given any finite
p\geq 1
and
1\leq r\leq\infty
,

X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
converges to

X^{\infty}_{\frac{k}{2}}
in
L^{p}(\Omega;W^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon,r}(\mathbb{T}))

as
\delta\to\infty
. In particular,
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
converges weakly to
\mu^{\infty}_{\frac{k}{2}}
as
\delta\to\infty
.

(iii)

For any
0<\delta<\infty
, the Gaussian measures
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
and
\mu^{\infty}_{\frac{k}{2}}
are equivalent.

(iv)

As
\delta\to\infty
, the measure
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
converges to
\mu^{\infty}_{\frac{k}{2}}
in the Kullback-Leibler divergence
defined in Definition
2.11
. In particular,
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
converges to
\mu^{\infty}_{\frac{k}{2}}
in total variation
as
\delta\to\infty
.

Remark 5.2
.

The regularity threshold
s=\frac{k-1}{2}
is sharp in the sense
that
X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
does not belong to
W^{\frac{k-1}{2},r}(\mathbb{T})

for any
r\geq 1
, almost surely.
The same comment applies to the shallow-water regime
(Proposition
6.1
).

Proposition
5.1

follows from a straightforward modification of the proof of Proposition 3.1 in
[
64
]

(and also of Proposition 3.4 in
[
64
]
with
k=1
),
but for readers’ convenience
and due to its importance, we present its proof.

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

(i) We only prove the difference estimate (
5.3
) since
(
5.2
) follows from a similar computation.
We first consider the case
r<\infty
.
When
p\geq r\geq 1
,
it follows from
Minkowski’s inequality, the Wiener chaos estimate (Lemma
2.8
),
(
1.26
),
and (
5.1
) that

(5.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|&\mathbf{P}_{M}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}-\mathbf{P}_{N}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}\|_{L^{p}_{\omega}W^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon,r}_{x}}\leq p\big\|\langle\partial_{x}\rangle^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbf{P}_{M}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}-\mathbf{P}_{N}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})\big\|_{L^{r}_{x}L^{2}_{\omega}}\\
&=p\bigg(\sum_{N<|n|\leq M}\frac{\langle n\rangle^{k-1-2\varepsilon}}{T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\sim_{\delta}p\bigg(\sum_{N<|n|\leq M}\frac{1}{|n|^{1+2\varepsilon}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\frac{p}{N^{\theta}}\end{split}

for some
\theta>0
.
Note that the implicit constant is independent of
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
When
1\leq p<r
, it follows from Hölder’s inequality (in
\omega
)
and (
5.4
) that

(5.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\mathbf{P}_{M}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}-\mathbf{P}_{N}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}\|_{L^{p}_{\omega}W^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon,r}_{x}}&\leq\|\mathbf{P}_{M}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}-\mathbf{P}_{N}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}\|_{L^{r}_{\omega}W^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon,r}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim_{\delta}\frac{r}{N^{\theta}},\end{split}

where
the implicit constant is independent of
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.

When
r=\infty
, from Sobolev’s inequality, we have

(5.6)

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{P}_{M}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}-\mathbf{P}_{N}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}\|_{L^{p}_{\omega}W^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon,\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\|\mathbf{P}_{M}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}-\mathbf{P}_{N}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}\|_{L^{p}_{\omega}W^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon,r_{0}}_{x}}

for any finite
r_{0}>\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\gg 1
.
Then, the bound (
5.3
) follows from
(
5.6
) with (
5.4
) and (
5.5
)
(with
r
and
\varepsilon
replaced by
r_{0}
and
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
, respectively).

(ii)
From (
1.27
) (with (
1.8
) and (
2.5
)) and
Lemma
2.2
, we have

\displaystyle 0\leq|n|^{k}-T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{k}a_{k,\ell}|n|^{\ell}\big[|n|^{k-\ell}-(\mathfrak{K}_{\delta}(n))^{k-\ell}\big]

\displaystyle<\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{k}a_{k,\ell}|n|^{\ell}\big[|n|^{k-\ell}-(|n|-\delta^{-1})^{k-\ell}\big]

\displaystyle=\delta^{-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{k-1}a_{k,\ell}\sum_{j=0}^{k-\ell-1}|n|^{k-1-j}(|n|-\delta^{-1})^{j},

from which we obtain

(5.7)

\displaystyle 0\leq|n|^{k}-T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)\lesssim\begin{cases}\delta^{-1}|n|^{k-1},&\text{for }|n|>\delta^{-1},\\
\delta^{-k},&\text{for }|n|\leq\delta^{-1}\end{cases}

for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.
Let
2\leq\delta<\infty
.
Then,
proceeding as in (
5.4
) with (
5.1
) and (
5.7
), we have

(5.8)

\displaystyle\|X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}-X^{\infty}_{\frac{k}{2}}\|_{L^{p}_{\omega}W^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon,r}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq p\bigg(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\langle n\rangle^{k-1-2\varepsilon}\bigg[\frac{1}{(T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n))^{\frac{1}{2}}}-\frac{1}{|n|^{\frac{k}{2}}}\bigg]^{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim p\bigg(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{1}{\langle n\rangle^{1+2\varepsilon}}\frac{|n|^{k}-T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)}{T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{p}{\delta^{\frac{1}{2}}}\bigg(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{1}{\langle n\rangle^{2+2\varepsilon}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\frac{p}{\delta^{\frac{1}{2}}}\longrightarrow 0,

as
\delta\to\infty
,
where, in the second inequality, we used
the fact that
\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{b}\leq\sqrt{a-b}
for
a\geq b\geq 0
.
Recalling that
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}=\Law(X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})

for
0<\delta\leq\infty
,
we then conclude weak convergence of
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
to
\mu^{\infty}_{\frac{k}{2}}
.

(iii) Let
0<\delta\leq\infty
. Recalling
g_{-n}=\overline{g_{n}}
, we have

\displaystyle X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(x;\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{2}{(T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n))^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Big(\Re g_{n}\cos(nx)-\Im g_{n}\sin(nx)\Big).

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, set

\displaystyle A_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Re g_{n}}{(T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n))^{\frac{1}{2}}},\quad A_{-n}=-\frac{\Im g_{n}}{(T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n))^{\frac{1}{2}}},\quad B_{n}=\frac{\Re g_{n}}{|n|^{\frac{k}{2}}},\quad B_{-n}=-\frac{\Im g_{n}}{|n|^{\frac{k}{2}}}

with
a_{\pm n}=\mathbb{E}[A^{2}_{\pm n}]=(T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n))^{-1}
and
b_{\pm n}=\mathbb{E}[B^{2}_{\pm n}]=|n|^{-k}
. Then, using (
5.1
) and (
5.7
), we have

(5.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\bigg(\frac{a_{n}}{b_{n}}-1\bigg)^{2}&=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\bigg(\frac{|n|^{k}}{T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)}-1\bigg)^{2}\\
&\lesssim\frac{1}{\delta^{2k}}\sum_{0<|n|\leq\frac{1}{\delta}}\frac{1}{|n|^{2k}}+\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\sum_{|n|>\frac{1}{\delta}}\frac{1}{|n|^{2}}<\infty.\end{split}

Hence, from Kakutani’s theorem (Lemma
2.9
), we conclude
equivalence of
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
and
\mu^{\infty}_{\frac{k}{2}}
.

(iv) In the following, we focus on proving
convergence in the Kullback-Leibler divergence.
Once we prove convergence in the Kullback-Leibler divergence,
convergence in total variation follows from
Pinsker’s inequality (Lemma
2.12
).

Given
0<\delta\leq\infty
, we first write
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
as the product
of Gaussian measures on
\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\bigotimes_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}(n)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)(\Re\widehat{u}(n))^{2}}d\Re\widehat{u}(n)\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg(\bigotimes_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}(n)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)(\Im\widehat{u}(n))^{2}}d\Im\widehat{u}(n)\bigg).

With
x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we then have

(5.10)

\displaystyle d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

\displaystyle=\bigotimes_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)}{2\pi}e^{-\frac{1}{2}T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)|x|^{2}}dx=:\bigotimes_{n\in\mathbb{N}}d\mu^{\delta,(n)}_{\frac{k}{2}}

The Radon-Nikodym derivative
d\mu^{\delta,(n)}_{\frac{k}{2}}/d\mu^{\infty,(n)}_{\frac{k}{2}}

is given by

(5.11)

\displaystyle\frac{d\mu^{\delta,(n)}_{\frac{k}{2}}}{d\mu^{\infty,(n)}_{\frac{k}{2}}}=\frac{T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)}{n^{k}}e^{\frac{1}{2}(n^{k}-T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n))|x|^{2}}.

Then, arguing as in
[
64
, (3.9)]
with
Part (iii), (
2.27
), (
2.26
),
(
5.10
), and (
5.11
), we have

(5.12)

\displaystyle d_{\rm KL}\big(\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}},\mu^{\infty}_{\frac{k}{2}}\big)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}d_{\rm KL}(\mu^{\delta,(n)}_{\frac{k}{2}},\mu^{\infty,(n)}_{\frac{k}{2}})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\phi\bigg(\frac{n^{k}}{T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)}\bigg),

where
\phi(t)=t-1-\log t
.
Note that
\phi(1)=0
and
\phi^{\prime}(t)>0
for
t>1
.
Recalling from Lemma
2.2
with (
1.27
)
that
\frac{n^{k}}{T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)}
decreases to 1 as
\delta\to\infty
,
we see that the summand on the right-hand side of (
5.12
)
decreases to
\phi(1)=0

as
\delta\to\infty
.
Moreover,
by noting
\phi(t)\leq(t-1)^{2}
for
t\geq 1
, it follows from (
5.9
) that

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\phi\bigg(\frac{n^{k}}{T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)}\bigg)\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{(n^{k}-T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n))^{2}}{T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}^{2}(n)}<\infty

for any
\delta>0
.
Hence, by
the dominated convergence theorem, we
conclude that

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to\infty}d_{\rm KL}\big(\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}},\mu^{\infty}_{\frac{k}{2}}\big)=\lim_{\delta\to\infty}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\phi\bigg(\frac{n^{k}}{T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)}\bigg)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\lim_{\delta\to\infty}\phi\bigg(\frac{n^{k}}{T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)}\bigg)=0.

This concludes the proof
of Proposition
5.1
.
∎

5.2.
Uniform integrability of the deep-water densities

Our main goal in this subsection is to
establish the following uniform (in
N
and also in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
)

L^{p}
-integrability of the truncated density
F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)
defined in (
1.30
), where
F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
is given by

(5.13)

\displaystyle\begin{split}F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)=\eta_{K}\big(\|u\|_{L^{2}}\big)\exp\Big(-R^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)\Big)\end{split}

for a fixed constant
K>0
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer and
K>0
.
Then, given any
0<\delta\leq\infty
and finite
p\geq 1
,
there exists
C_{\delta,p}>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\|F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\|F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)\|_{L^{p}(d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})}\leq C_{\delta,p}<\infty,

where the constant
C_{\delta,p}
is independent of

2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
Here,

X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
and

\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
are as in (
1.26
) and (
1.25
), respectively.

We prove Proposition
5.3

via a variational approach, popularized by Barashkov and Gubinelli
[
2
]
.
In particular,
we follow the approach
developed in
[
83
,
89
,
84
,
107
]

to study
focusing Gibbs measures.
Recall that

\displaystyle\eta_{K}(x)\leq\mathbf{1}_{\{|x|\leq 2K\}}\leq\exp\big(-A|x|^{\alpha}\big)\exp\big(A(2K)^{\alpha}\big)

for any
K,A,\alpha>0
.
Then,
by setting

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)=\exp\Big(-R^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)-A\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{\alpha}\Big)

for some
A,\alpha>0
(to be chosen later),
we have

\displaystyle F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)\leq C_{A,K}\cdot\mathcal{F}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u).

Hence,
Proposition
5.3
follows once we prove the following
uniform bound on
\mathcal{F}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)
.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer.
Then, given any
0<\delta\leq\infty
and finite
p\geq 1
,
there exist
A=A(p)>0
,
\alpha>0
, and
C_{\delta,p}>0
such that

(5.14)

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\|\mathcal{F}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}=\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\|\mathcal{F}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)\|_{L^{p}(d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})}\leq C_{\delta,p}<\infty,

where the constant
C_{\delta,p}
is independent of

2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
Here,
A=A(p)
is independent of
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
, while

\alpha
is independent of
p
and
0<\delta\leq\infty
.

Let us introduce some notations before stating the variational formula.
Let
W(t)
be a cylindrical Brownian motion in

\displaystyle L^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{T})=\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}L^{2}(\mathbb{T})

of mean-zero functions on
\mathbb{T}
.
Namely, we have

(5.15)

\displaystyle W(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}B_{n}(t)e_{n},

where

\{B_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}
is a sequence of mutually independent complex-valued
26
26

26

By convention, we normalize
B_{n}
such that
\text{Var}(B_{n}(t))=2t
.

Brownian motions such that

\overline{B_{n}}=B_{-n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.
Let
\mathcal{T}_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}

be the
Fourier multiplier operator
with multiplier
T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)
defined in (
1.27
).
Then,
we define a centered Gaussian process
\mathcal{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}

by setting

(5.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(t)=\mathcal{T}_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}W(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\frac{B_{n}(t)}{\big(T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}}e_{n}.

By comparing (
5.16
) with (
1.26
),
we have

(5.17)

\displaystyle\Law\big(\mathcal{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(1)\big)=\Law\big(X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}\big).

We use
\mathbb{H}_{a}
to denote the collection of drifts,
belonging to

L^{2}([0,1];L^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{T}))
,
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely,
which are progressively measurable
with respect to the filtration
induced by

W
;
see

[
117
, p. 477]
for a discussion of filtration
in an abstract Wiener space.

We now state the Boué-Dupuis variational formula
[
10
,
116
]
.
See also
[
117
, Theorem 3.2]

and
[
107
, Appendix A]
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer,
0<\delta\leq\infty
,
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Suppose that
G:C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\to\mathbb{R}

is measurable such that
\mathbb{E}\big[|G(\mathbf{P}_{N}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})|^{p}\big]<\infty

and
\mathbb{E}\big[\big|\exp\big(G(\mathbf{P}_{N}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})\big)\big|^{q}\big]<\infty

for some
1<p,q<\infty
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
.
Then, we have

(5.18)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\log\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\big(G(\mathbf{P}_{N}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})\big)\Big]\\
&\quad=\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{H}_{a}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[G\big(\mathbf{P}_{N}\mathcal{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(1)+\mathbf{P}_{N}I_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(\theta)(1)\big)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\|\theta(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}dt\bigg],\end{split}

where

I_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(\theta)
is defined by

\displaystyle I_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(\theta)(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{T}_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\theta(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}

and the expectation
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}

is taken with respect to the underlying probability measure
\mathbb{P}
.

In view of (
5.17
),
with a slight abuse of notation,
we use

X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
to denote

\mathcal{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(1)

in applying the variational formula (
5.18
) in the remaining part
of this subsection.

Given finite
p\geq 1
, we prove Proposition
5.4

by applying the variational formula (Lemma
5.5
)
for

\displaystyle G(u)=-p\big(R^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)+A\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{\alpha}\big).

Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
it is easy to see that
G(u)
satisfies the assumption of Lemma
5.5

in view of
the bound
R^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)\leq C_{N}<\infty
,
uniformly in
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
(see, for example, the proof of Lemma
5.15
),
and
Proposition
5.1
.
Proceeding as in the proof of
[
42
, Lemma 4.7]

with (
1.27
),
we have

(5.19)

\displaystyle\|I_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(\theta)(1)\|_{H^{\frac{k}{2}}_{x}}^{2}\leq a_{\delta}\int_{0}^{t}\|\theta(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{x}}dt

for any
\theta\in\mathbb{H}_{a}
,
where the constant
a_{\delta}>0
is independent of
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
Then, from Lemma
5.5
and (
5.19
),
we see that
Proposition
5.4
follows once
we establish an
upper bound on

(5.20)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{\delta,\frac{k}{2},p,N}(\Theta)&=\mathbb{E}\bigg[-pR^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}+\mathbf{P}_{N}\Theta)\\
&\hphantom{XXX}-pA\|\mathbf{P}_{N}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}+\mathbf{P}_{N}\Theta\|_{L^{2}}^{\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}a_{\delta}^{-1}\|\Theta\|^{2}_{H^{\frac{k}{2}}}\bigg]\end{split}

for each
0<\delta\leq\infty
,
uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\Theta\in H^{\frac{k}{2}}_{0}(\mathbb{T})=\mathbf{P}_{\neq 0}H^{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbb{T})

and also
uniformly in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.

For simplicity of notation,
we set

(5.21)

\displaystyle X^{\delta}_{N}=\mathbf{P}_{N}X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Theta_{N}=\mathbf{P}_{N}\Theta,

where both
X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
and
\Theta
have mean zero on
\mathbb{T}
.
Then, from (
3.62
), we have

(5.22)

\displaystyle R^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(X^{\delta}_{N}+\Theta_{N})

\displaystyle=A^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{k}{2}}(X^{\delta}_{N}+\Theta_{N})+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{\delta^{k-\ell}}A^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{\ell}{2}}(X^{\delta}_{N}+\Theta_{N}),

where

A^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{\ell}{2}}(u)
is as in
(
3.63
), (
3.64
), and (
3.65
).
In the following,
we separately estimate
the contribution
from the lower order terms (
1\leq\ell\leq k-1
) in (
5.22
),
which is handled deterministically
(Lemma
5.6
),
and from the leading order term (
\ell=k
) in (
5.22
),
for which we need to use probabilistic estimates; see Lemma
5.7
.

The first lemma controls the lower order terms
in (
5.22
).

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer.
Given
0<\delta\leq\infty
and small
\varepsilon_{0}>0
,
there exist
\alpha\geq 1
, independent of
\delta
and
\varepsilon_{0}
, and

C_{\delta,\varepsilon_{0}}>0
such that

(5.23)

\displaystyle|A^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{\ell}{2}}(u_{1}+u_{2})|\leq C_{\delta,\varepsilon_{0}}\Big(1+\|u_{1}\|^{\alpha}_{W^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon,\infty}}+\|u_{2}\|_{L^{2}}^{\alpha}\Big)+\varepsilon_{0}\|u_{2}\|_{H^{\frac{k}{2}}}^{2}

for any
1\leq\ell\leq k-1
,
where the constant
C_{\delta,\varepsilon_{0}}>0
is independent of
2\leq\delta\leq\infty

and
\alpha
is independent of
0<\delta\leq\infty
.

The next lemma controls the leading contribution in (
5.22
).

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer.
Given
0<\delta\leq\infty
and small
\varepsilon_{0}>0
,
there exist
\alpha\geq 1
, independent of
\delta
and
\varepsilon_{0}
, and

C_{\delta,\varepsilon_{0}}>0
such that

(5.24)

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{E}\big[A^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{k}{2}}(X^{\delta}_{N}+\Theta_{N})\big]\big|\leq C_{\delta,\varepsilon_{0}}+\mathbb{E}\Big[C_{\delta,\varepsilon_{0}}\|\Theta_{N}\|_{L^{2}}^{\alpha}+\varepsilon_{0}\|\Theta_{N}\|_{H^{\frac{k}{2}}}^{2}\Big],

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
where the constant
C_{\delta,\varepsilon_{0}}>0
is independent of
2\leq\delta\leq\infty

and
\alpha
is independent of
0<\delta\leq\infty
.

Remark 5.8
.

From (
3.63
) and
(
3.64
), we see that the leading order term
A^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{k}{2}}(u)

involves two factors with
\partial_{x}^{\frac{k-1}{2}}u
.
27
27

27

When
k
is even, it is
|\partial_{x}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{x}^{\frac{k-2}{2}}u
to be precise.

Since
X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
does not belong to
W^{\frac{k-1}{2},r}(\mathbb{T})

for any
1\leq r\leq\infty
, almost surely,
we need to rely on a probabilistic argument in handling
the leading order term

A^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{k}{2}}(X^{\delta}_{N}+\Theta_{N})

in Lemma
5.7
.

We now present a proof of
Proposition
5.4

(and hence of Proposition
5.3
)
by assuming Lemmas
5.6
and
5.7
.

Proof of Proposition
5.4
.

It follows from (
5.20
), Lemmas
5.6
and
5.7
,
and Proposition
5.1
(i) that given any small
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, we have

(5.25)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{\delta,\frac{k}{2},p,N}(\Theta)&\leq pC_{\delta,\varepsilon_{0}}+\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(Cp\varepsilon_{0}-\tfrac{1}{2}a_{\delta}^{-1}\big)\|\Theta_{N}\|_{H^{\frac{k}{2}}}^{2}\\
&\quad+pC_{\delta,\varepsilon_{0}}\|\Theta_{N}\|_{L^{2}}^{\alpha}-pA\|X^{\delta}_{N}+\Theta_{N}\|_{L^{2}}^{\alpha}\Big],\end{split}

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}

and
\Theta\in H^{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbb{T})
.
Moreover, the constant

C_{\delta,\varepsilon_{0}}
is independent of

2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
From
[
83
, (5.35)]
, given
\alpha>0
,
there exists
C_{\alpha}>0
such that

(5.26)

\displaystyle|a+b|^{\alpha}\geq\frac{1}{2}|a|^{\alpha}-C_{\alpha}|b|^{\alpha}

for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
.
Thus, we have

(5.27)

\displaystyle-\|X^{\delta}_{N}+\Theta_{N}\|_{L^{2}}^{\alpha}\leq-\frac{1}{2}\|\Theta_{N}\|_{L^{2}}^{\alpha}+C_{\alpha}\|X^{\delta}_{N}\|_{L^{2}}^{\alpha}

for some
C_{\alpha}>0
.
Hence, by choosing
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(p,a_{\delta})=\varepsilon_{0}(p,\delta)>0
sufficiently small
and then
A=A(p,\varepsilon_{0})=A(p,\delta)\gg 1

sufficiently large,
it follows from (
5.25
) and (
5.27
) with
Proposition
5.1
(i)
that

(5.28)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{\delta,\frac{k}{2},p,N}(\Theta)&\lesssim_{\delta,p}1,\end{split}

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}

and
\Theta\in H^{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbb{T})
,
where the implicit constant is independent of

2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
Since
a_{\delta}
is independent of
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
,
the constants
\varepsilon_{0}
and
A

are also independent of
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
Therefore,
(
5.14
) with uniformity in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
follows from
(
5.28
) and Lemma
5.5
.
This proves
Proposition
5.4

(and hence Proposition
5.3
).
∎

The remaining part of this subsection is devoted to proofs of
Lemmas
5.6
and
5.7
.
We first state a preliminary lemma.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer.
Let
0<\delta\leq\infty

and
