\widetilde{p}(v)=v\partial_{x}^{\kappa-1}v\partial_{x}^{\kappa}v
.

Since
k=2\kappa
, we have

\widetilde{p}(v)=v\partial_{x}^{\frac{k}{2}-1}v\partial_{x}^{\frac{k}{2}}v
.
Thus, we can proceed as in
in Case 1 of the proof of Lemma
5.7
,
where we treated

p(u)=u\partial_{x}^{m-1}u\partial_{x}^{m}u
with
m=\frac{k}{2}

(see (
5.40
)-(
5.44
)),
and
we obtain (
6.26
).

\bullet

Subcase 2.b:

\widetilde{p}(v)=\prod_{i=1}^{j}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{i}}v
as in (b) of (
6.36
),
satisfying
(
6.37
).

In this case,
we have
(i)

\alpha_{i}\leq\kappa-1=\frac{k-2}{2}
for any
i=1,\dots,j

such that (
5.35
) holds,
and
(ii) under the condition

\alpha_{1\cdots j}\leq 2\kappa+2-j
with
j\geq 3
,
(
5.36
) with
2m=k

holds.
Hence, by proceeding as in Subcase 1.b, we obtain
(
6.26
).

This concludes the proof of Lemma
6.7
.
∎

6.3.
Construction of the shallow-water generalized Gibbs measures

Arguing as in Subsection
5.3
,
Theorem
1.7
(i)
on the construction of the shallow-water generalized Gibbs measures
follows once we prove
the following lemma
on
uniform (in
N
and
\delta
)
convergence of the truncated interaction potential

\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\widetilde{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})
,
where
\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)

and
\widetilde{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
are as in
(
4.92
)
and (
1.33
), respectively.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer.
Given any
0<\delta<\infty

and finite
p\geq 1
,
the sequence
\{\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\widetilde{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}

converges
to
\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\widetilde{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})

in
L^{p}(\Omega)
as
N\to\infty
.
Moreover, given any finite
p\geq 1
,
there exist
C_{\delta}=C_{\delta}(p)>0
and
\theta>0
such that

(6.38)

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{0<\delta\leq 1}\|\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\widetilde{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\delta}<\infty,

(6.39)

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{M}\widetilde{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})-\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\widetilde{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\delta}}{N^{\theta}}

for any
M\geq N\geq 1
,
where the constant
C_{\delta}
is independent of
0<\delta\leq 1
.
In particular, the rate of convergence is uniform in
0<\delta\leq 1
.
When
k
is even, (
6.38
) and (
6.39
) also hold
for
\delta=0
.

In the previous subsection,
together with the
L^{r}
-boundedness of
\delta\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}

and the boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
from

W^{1,r}(\mathbb{T})
from
L^{r}(\mathbb{T})

for
1<r<\infty

(see (
2.14
) and (
2.15
) in Lemma
2.4
),
we saw how Lemma
6.7

followed from a slight modification
of the proofs of Lemmas
5.6
and
5.7
.
Lemma
6.9
also follows
from a straightforward modification
of the proof of the corresponding lemma in the deep-water case
(Lemma
5.11
).
We omit details.

Remark 6.10
.

Let
k\geq 2
be an even integer.
Recall from (
4.80
) that

\widetilde{E}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v)=\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa,0}(v)
,
where

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa,0}(v)

is the
\delta
-free part of
the shallow-water conservation law

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)
in (
4.53
),
defined as the collection of all terms that do not depend on
\delta
explicitly;
see Footnote
24
.
Then,
from (
1.19
), we see that

\widetilde{R}^{\text{\rm KdV}}_{\kappa}(v)=\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\kappa,0}(v)
,
where

\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\kappa,0}(v)

denotes the
\delta
-free part of

\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)
in (
4.92
).
Namely, all the monomials in

\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\kappa,0}(v)

appear in

\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\kappa}(v)
.
Hence,
all the results established in Subsections
6.2

and
6.3
for even-order quantities extend to
the
\delta=0
case.

6.4.
Shallow-water convergence of the generalized Gibbs measures

As in the deep-water case,
we have the following lemma on
the
L^{p}(\Omega)
-convergence properties of the truncated density

\widetilde{F}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}v)
defined in (
6.20
).

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer and
K>0
.
Then, given any finite
p\geq 1
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\|\widetilde{F}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\widetilde{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})-\widetilde{F}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\|\widetilde{F}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\frac{k}{2}})-\widetilde{F}^{0}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\frac{k}{2}})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=0.

Arguing as in the proof of
Lemma
5.15
,
we have
the following
deep-water analogue of Lemma
5.15

on an

N
-dependent
(but uniform in
0<\delta\leq 1
)
deterministic bound:

(6.40)

\displaystyle\sup_{0<\delta\leq 1}\big|\widetilde{F}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}v)\big|\leq C_{k,K,N}<\infty

for any
v\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
,
which
holds including
\delta=0
, when
k
is even.
Then,
Lemma
6.11

follows from a straightforward modification
of the proof of
Lemma
5.14

with Lemma
6.2
and
(
6.40
).
We omit details.

We conclude this section by presenting a proof of Theorem
1.7
(ii) and (iii).

Proof of Theorem
1.7
(ii) and (iii).

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer.
The claim on singularity / equivalence
follows from the corresponding statement
for the base Gaussian measures (Proposition
6.1
(iii) and (iv))
and the equivalence of the shallow-water generalized Gibbs measures
and the base Gaussian measures with an
L^{2}
-cutoff
(Theorem
1.7
(i)).

Next, we show that
the measure
\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
converges weakly to
\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil}

as
\delta\to 0
.
While we follow closely the argument in Subsection 4.3 of
[
64
]
,
we present details for readers’ convenience.
Fix
a Borel subset
A
of
H^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})

with

\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil}(\partial A)=0
, where
\partial A
denotes the boundary of the set
A
.
By the portmanteau lemma, weak convergence of

\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
to

\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil}

follows once we show that

(6.41)

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(A)=\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil}(A)

for every such
A
.
By the triangle inequality, we have

(6.42)

\displaystyle\big|\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(A)-\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil}(A)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{0<\delta\leq 1}\big|\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(A)-\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}(A)\big|

\displaystyle+\big|\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}(A)-\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil,N}(A)\big|+\big|\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil,N}(A)-\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil}(A)\big|.

In view of Theorem
1.7
(i),
the first and third terms
on the right-hand side of (
6.42
) vanish
as
N\to\infty
.
Hence, once we show that

(6.43)

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\widetilde{\rho}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}(A)=\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil,N}(A)

for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
then
(
6.41
) follows from first taking a limit as
\delta\to 0

and then a limit as
N\to\infty
in (
6.42
),

It remains to show (
6.43
).
As in
[
64
, (4.18)]
, it suffices to prove

(6.44)

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathbb{E}\Big[\widetilde{F}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\widetilde{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\widetilde{F}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil})\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil})\Big].

By the triangle inequality and Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality, we have

(6.45)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bigg|\mathbb{E}&\Big[\widetilde{F}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\widetilde{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})\Big]-\mathbb{E}\Big[\widetilde{F}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil})\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil})\Big]\bigg|\\
&\leq\big\|\widetilde{F}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\widetilde{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})-\widetilde{F}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil})\big\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\\
&\quad+\|\widetilde{F}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})-\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil})\big|\mathbf{1}_{B}(\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil})\Big],\end{split}

where

\displaystyle B=\big\{v\in H^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}):\eta_{K}\big(\|v\|_{L^{2}}\big)>0\big\}.

Note that by the continuity of
\eta_{K}
,
we see that
B
is open in
H^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})
.
Since

\widetilde{\rho}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil}(\partial A)=0
, we have

\widetilde{\mu}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil}(\partial A\cap B)=0
.
Then, we have

(6.46)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}&\Big[\big|\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})-\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil})\big|\mathbf{1}_{B}(\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil})\Big]\\
&=\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbf{1}_{(\textup{int}A)\cap B}(\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil})\big|\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})-\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil})\big|\Big]\\
&\quad+\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbf{1}_{(\textup{int}A^{c})\cap B}(\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil})\big|\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})-\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil})\big|\Big],\end{split}

where
\text{int}A
denotes the interior of
A

given by
\text{int}A=A\setminus\partial A
.
From
Proposition
6.1
(ii)
and the openness of
(\text{int}A)\cap B
and
(\text{int}A^{c})\cap B
,
we see that the integrands of the terms on the right-hand side
of (
6.46
) tend to
0
as
\delta\to 0
.
Hence, by the bounded convergence theorem,
we obtain

(6.47)

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})-\mathbf{1}_{A}(\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil})\big|\mathbf{1}_{B}(\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil})\Big]=0.

Finally, the desired identity (
6.44
)
follows from (
6.45
),
Lemma
6.11
,
Proposition
6.4
,
and (
6.47
).
This concludes
the proof of Theorem

1.7
(ii) and (iii).
∎

Remark 6.12
.

If we consider the generalized Gibbs measure
with
a sharp
L^{2}
-cutoff
\mathbf{1}_{\{\|v\|_{L^{2}}\leq K\}}

in place of the smooth
L^{2}
-cutoff

\eta_{K}\big(\|v\|_{L^{2}}\big)
,
we can not exploit the continuity
of
\eta_{K}
as above.
Instead, we simply can use

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\|\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil}\|_{L^{2}}=K\Big)=0.

See (
5.50
).
Then, we can exploit the openness
of the sets

\big\{v\in H^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T}):\|\widetilde{X}^{0}_{\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil}\|_{L^{2}}<K\big\}
.

7.
Probabilistic asymptotic conservation

In this section,
we establish the probabilistic asymptotic
conservation of the form (
1.44
)
for both the deep-water and shallow-water regimes
(Propositions
7.1
and
7.3
),
which is a key ingredient for proving invariance
of the generalized Gibbs measures.
In the next section,
we prove invariance for each
fixed

\delta
.
For this reason, there is no need to establish
probabilistic asymptotic conservation
which is uniform in
\delta\gg 1
(or
\delta\ll 1
).

7.1.
Main statements

We first consider the deep-water regime.
Fix
0<\delta\leq\infty
.
Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
consider the truncated ILW (
1.43
).
It is easy to check that the
L^{2}
-norm
is conserved for the truncated dynamics (
1.43
)
and thus (
1.43
) is globally well-posed in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
.
Moreover, (
1.43
) is a Hamiltonian PDE
with the Hamiltonian:

\displaystyle E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{\frac{1}{2}}u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}}+R^{\delta}_{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)=\frac{1}{2}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{\frac{1}{2}}u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\frac{1}{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)^{3}dx,

which is also conserved under the truncated ILW (
1.43
).
As observed in
[
113
,
114
,
115
]
,
the main source of difficulty in proving invariance
of the generalized Gibbs measures
\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
in (
1.29
) under ILW (
1.1
)
comes
from the non-conservation of
the truncated
k
th energy:

(7.1)

\displaystyle E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{k}a_{k,\ell}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{\frac{k-\ell}{2}}u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{k}{2}}}+R^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)

for
k\geq 2
,
which causes non-invariance of the truncated generalized Gibbs measures

\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}
in (
1.30
)
under the truncated ILW dynamics (
1.43
).

Let us take a closer look.
By writing
u=\mathbf{P}_{N}u+\mathbf{P}_{>N}u
, where

\mathbf{P}_{>N}=\textup{Id}-\mathbf{P}_{N}
,
we can decouple a solution
u
to (
1.43
)
into
the high frequency part
\mathbf{P}_{>N}u
, satisfying the linear ILW equation:

(7.2)

\partial_{t}\mathbf{P}_{>N}u-\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}\mathbf{P}_{>N}u=0,

and
the low frequency part
\mathbf{P}_{N}u
, satisfying

(7.3)

\partial_{t}\mathbf{P}_{N}u-\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}\mathbf{P}_{N}u=\mathbf{P}_{N}\partial_{x}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)^{2},

which is a finite-dimensional system of ODEs when viewed on the Fourier side,
preserving the Hamiltonian

E^{\delta}_{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)
.
On the one hand, the linear equation (
7.2
)
preserves

(7.4)

\displaystyle E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},>N}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=0\\
\text{even}\end{subarray}}^{k}a_{k,\ell}\|\mathcal{G}_{\delta}^{\frac{k-\ell}{2}}\mathbf{P}_{>N}u\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{k}{2}}}

for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
On the other hand,
as pointed out in the introduction,

E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)

is not conserved under (
7.3
) for any
k\geq 2
,
which is the source of the non-conservation of the truncated
k
th energy

E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}(u)
in (
7.1
)
under the truncated ILW (
1.43
).

As proposed in
[
114
,
115
]
,
we aim to prove almost invariance of
the truncated generalized Gibbs measures

\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}

(Proposition
8.4
)
which, in the limit as
N\to\infty
,
yields invariance of the limiting generalized Gibbs measure
\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
.
A key ingredient is
the following probabilistic asymptotic conservation
of the truncated energy

E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)

under (
7.3
).
Before stating a result, let us introduce a notation;
given
0<\delta\leq\infty
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
let

\Phi_{N}(t)=\Phi_{N}(t;\delta)
be the solution map for the truncated ILW dynamics (
1.43
),
sending initial data
u(0)
at time
0
to a solution
u(t)=\Phi_{N}(t)(u(0))
at time
t
.

Proposition 7.1

(deep-water regime)
.

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer and
0<\delta\leq\infty
.
Then, given any finite
p\geq 1
,
we have

(7.5)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\bigg\|\frac{d}{dt}E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\Phi_{N}(t)(u))\Big|_{t=0}\bigg\|_{L^{p}(d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})}=0.

We present a proof of
Proposition
7.1
in Subsection
7.2
.
As we explain below, thanks to (
5.1
) and (
2.10
),
most of the terms can be treated
by a slight modification
of the argument in
[
113
,
114
,
115
]
.
Thus, we keep our presentation brief.

Remark 7.2
.

As it is clear from the proof,
the rate of convergence in (
7.5
) is uniform in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
On the other hand,
our argument for
(
7.8
)
in the shallow-water regime
does not
provide a uniform rate of convergence
for
0<\delta\leq 1

and in fact it diverges as
\delta\to 0
.
We provide a separate argument for
\delta=0
.

Next, we consider the shallow-water regime.
Given
0\leq\delta<\infty
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
we consider the following truncated scaled ILW:

(7.6)

\partial_{t}v-\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}v=\mathbf{P}_{N}\partial_{x}(\mathbf{P}_{N}v)^{2},

where a solution
v
decouples into
the high frequency part
\mathbf{P}_{>N}v
, satisfying the linear scaled ILW equation:

\partial_{t}\mathbf{P}_{>N}v-\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}\mathbf{P}_{>N}v=0,

and
the low frequency part
\mathbf{P}_{N}v
, satisfying

(7.7)

\partial_{t}\mathbf{P}_{N}v-\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}\mathbf{P}_{N}v=\mathbf{P}_{N}\partial_{x}(\mathbf{P}_{N}v)^{2}.

Let

\widetilde{\Phi}_{N}(t)=\widetilde{\Phi}_{N}(t;\delta)
be the solution map for the truncated scaled ILW (
7.6
),
sending initial data
v(0)
at time
0
to a solution
v(t)=\widetilde{\Phi}_{N}(t)(v(0))
at time
t
.
Then, our main goal is
to establish
the following probabilistic asymptotic conservation
of the truncated energy

\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}v)

under (
7.7
).

Proposition 7.3

(shallow-water regime)
.

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer and
0<\delta<\infty
.
Then, given any finite
p\geq 1
,
we have

(7.8)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\bigg\|\frac{d}{dt}\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\widetilde{\Phi}_{N}(t)(v))\Big|_{t=0}\bigg\|_{L^{p}(d\widetilde{\mu}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})}=0.

When
k
is even, (
7.8
) also holds for
\delta=0
.

Proposition
7.3
follows
from a slight modification of the proof of Proposition
7.1

in the deep-water regime.
Due to the non-uniformity in
0<\delta\leq 1

of the rate of convergence, we need to provide a separate
argument for
\delta=0
.
See
Subsection
7.3

for a further discussion.

Notations.

Before proceeding further,
let us introduce some notations,
following Section 8 in
[
113
]
.
Let
p(u)
be a monomial in
\mathcal{P}_{j}(u)

for some
j\in\mathbb{N}

with the fundamental form

\displaystyle\widetilde{p}(u)=\prod_{i=1}^{j}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{i}}u

for some
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
;
see Definition
3.7
.
Then, given
N\in\mathbb{N}
, by viewing

p(u)=p(\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}u,\dots,\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{j}}u)

as a
j
-linear function,
we define
a new monomial
p_{i,N}^{*}(u)
,

i=1,\dots,j
,
by setting

\displaystyle p_{i,N}^{*}(u)=p(u)\big|_{\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{i}}u=-2\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{i}}(\mathbf{P}_{>N}(u\partial_{x}u))},

namely,

p_{i,N}^{*}(u)

is the monomial obtained from
p(u)
by
replacing
\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{i}}u
in the
i
th factor by
-2\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{i}}(\mathbf{P}_{>N}(u\partial_{x}u))
.
Furthermore, we define
p_{N}^{*}(u)
as

(7.9)

\displaystyle p^{*}_{N}(u)=\sum_{i=1}^{j}p_{i,N}^{*}(u).

Note that

\displaystyle\#u(p^{*}_{N})=\#u(p)+1\qquad\text{and}\qquad\#\partial_{x}(p^{*}_{N})=\#\partial_{x}(p)+1

in the sense of Definition
3.3
.
The notation
p^{*}_{N}(u)
in (
7.9
)
will become useful in expressing
\partial_{t}E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\Phi_{N}(t)(u))

in a concise manner.
We extend these definitions
to monomials in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{j}(v)
defined in Definition
4.9
.

We use
u^{+}
(and
u^{-}
, respectively)
to denote the projection of
u

onto positive frequencies (and negative frequencies, respectively).
Then, we have

(7.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}u^{+}=-iu^{+}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{H}u^{-}=iu^{-}

and

(7.11)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{>N}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u_{1}\mathbf{P}_{N}u_{2})=\mathbf{P}_{>N}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u_{1}^{+}\mathbf{P}_{N}u_{2}^{+})+\mathbf{P}_{>N}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u_{1}^{-}\mathbf{P}_{N}u_{2}^{-}).

In particular, from (
7.10
) and (
7.11
), we have

(7.12)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{>N}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u_{1}\mathcal{H}\mathbf{P}_{N}u_{2})=\mathcal{H}\mathbf{P}_{>N}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u_{1}\mathbf{P}_{N}u_{2}).

Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we use the following short-hand notation:

(7.13)

\displaystyle u_{N}=\mathbf{P}_{N}u.

7.2.
Deep-water regime

Fix an integer
k\geq 2
,
0<\delta\leq\infty
, and
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
u
be a solution to the truncated ILW (
1.43
).
Then, the low-frequency part
\mathbf{P}_{N}u
satisfies (
7.3
),
which can be rewritten as

\partial_{t}\mathbf{P}_{N}u-\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}\mathbf{P}_{N}u=2(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)\partial_{x}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)-2\mathbf{P}_{>N}\big((\mathbf{P}_{N}u)\partial_{x}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)\big).

Then, from the conservation of
E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

under the (untruncated) ILW (
1.1
)
(Theorem
1.3
),
(
3.61
), and (
3.62
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\frac{1}{\delta^{k-\ell}}A_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{\ell}{2},N}^{*}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u),

where
A_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{\ell}{2},N}^{*}(u)
is
defined by replacing each monomial
p(u)
in

A^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{\ell}{2}}(u)
, appearing in
(
3.63
), (
3.64
),
and (
3.65
),
by
p_{N}^{*}(u)
defined in (
7.9
).
Then, Proposition
7.1
follows once we prove the following proposition
on

A^{*}_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{\ell}{2},N}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)
.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer and
0<\delta\leq\infty
.
Then, given any finite
p\geq 1
,
we have

(7.14)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\Big\|A^{*}_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{\ell}{2},N}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)\Big\|_{L^{p}(d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})}=0

for
\ell=1,\dots,k
.

We first prove (
7.14
) when
\ell=1
.

Proof of Proposition
7.4
when
\ell=1
.

Let
u=X^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
.
Then,
from (
3.65
),
(
7.9
), (
7.13
),
and (
1.26
) we have

(7.15)

\displaystyle\begin{split}A^{*}_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{1}{2},N}(u_{N})&=-6c_{0}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{N}^{2}\mathbf{P}_{>N}(u_{N}\partial_{x}u_{N})dx\\
&=-\frac{6c_{0}}{(2\pi)^{\frac{3}{2}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1\cdots 4}=0\\
0<|n_{j}|\leq N\\
|n_{34}|>N\end{subarray}}\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{4}g_{n_{j}}(in_{4})}{\prod_{j=1}^{4}(T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n_{j}))^{\frac{1}{2}}}\\
&=:\hskip 1.42262pt\text{I}\hskip 1.42262pt+\text{I \hskip-7.96677pt I},\end{split}

where
I

denotes the contribution from frequencies with no pair
and
I I

denotes the contribution from frequencies with two pairs
in the sense of Definition
5.13
.
Then, by
the Wiener chaos estimate (Lemma
2.8
),
Jensen’s inequality as in (
5.52
)-(
5.53
), (
5.1
),
and Lemma
2.7
(recall that
k\geq 2
),
we have

(7.16)

\displaystyle\|\hskip 1.42262pt\text{I}\hskip 1.42262pt\|_{L^{p}(d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})}^{2}\lesssim_{p}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1\cdots 4}=0\\
0<|n_{j}|\leq N\\
|n_{34}|>N\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|n_{1}|^{k}|n_{2}|^{k}|n_{3}|^{k}|n_{4}|^{k-2}}\lesssim N^{-1+\varepsilon}\longrightarrow 0,

as
N\to\infty
, where we used the fact that
|n_{12}|=|n_{34}|>N
.

Next, we consider the case when
there are two pairs in (
7.15
)
(note that
n_{12}=-n_{34}\neq 0
due to
\mathbf{P}_{>N}
).
Without loss of generality, we suppose that
n_{13}=n_{24}=0
.
We note from (
7.11
) that
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
have the same sign in (
7.15
).
Then, by splitting the sum into two sums,
where
n_{1},n_{2}>0
and
n_{1},n_{2}<0
, respectively,
and relabeling (with
|g_{-n}|=|g_{n}|
and
T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(-n)=T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n)
),
we have

(7.17)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{I \hskip-7.96677pt I}&=-\frac{6c_{0}}{(2\pi)^{\frac{3}{2}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<|n_{1}|,|n_{2}|\leq N\\
|n_{12}|>N\end{subarray}}\frac{|g_{n_{1}}|^{2}|g_{n_{2}}|^{2}(-in_{2})}{T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n_{1})T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n_{2})}\\
&=-\frac{6c_{0}}{(2\pi)^{\frac{3}{2}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<n_{1},n_{2}\leq N\\
n_{12}>N\end{subarray}}\frac{|g_{n_{1}}|^{2}|g_{n_{2}}|^{2}(-in_{2}+in_{2})}{T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n_{1})T_{\delta,\frac{k}{2}}(n_{2})}=0.\end{split}

Hence, (
7.14
) follows from
(
7.15
), (
7.16
), and (
7.17
)
∎

In the remaining part of this subsection,
we discuss a proof of (
7.14
)
for
\ell=2,\dots,k
.
In view of the structure of
A_{\frac{k}{2},\frac{\ell}{2}}(u)

with (
1.26
) and (
5.1
),
it suffices to prove (
7.14
) for
\ell=k
;
see
[
115
, Remark 8.3]
.
Once we prove Lemmas
7.5

and
7.6
below,
Proposition
7.4

for
\ell=k\geq 2
follows.

Let us first consider the leading order contributions.
It follows from Lemma
3.10
that
the leading order cubic terms are given by

(7.18)

\displaystyle p_{0}(u)=u(\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}^{m-1}u)(\partial_{x}^{m}u)

when
k=2m
is even,
and

(7.19)

p_{\vec{\alpha}}(u)=(\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{1}}u)(\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{2}}\partial_{x}^{m}u)(\mathcal{H}^{\alpha_{3}}\partial_{x}^{m}u)

for
\vec{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})\in\mathcal{A}=\big\{(0,0,0),(0,0,1),(0,1,1)\big\}

when
k=2m+1
is odd.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer and
0<\delta\leq\infty
.

(i)

Let
k=2m
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then, given any finite
p\geq 1
,
we have

(7.20)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\bigg\|\int_{\mathbb{T}}p_{0,N}^{*}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)dx\bigg\|_{L^{p}(d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})}=0,

where
p_{0}(u)
is as in (
7.18
)
and
p_{0,N}^{*}(u)
is as in (
7.9
).

(ii)

Let
k=2m+1
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
and
\mathcal{A}=\big\{(0,0,0),(0,0,1),(0,1,1)\big\}

be as in (
3.60
).
Then, given any finite
p\geq 1
,
we have

(7.21)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\bigg\|\int_{\mathbb{T}}p_{\vec{\alpha},N}^{*}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)dx\bigg\|_{L^{p}(d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})}=0

for
any
\vec{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})\in\mathcal{A}
,
where
p_{\vec{\alpha}}(u)
is as in (
7.19
)
and
p_{\vec{\alpha},N}^{*}(u)
is as in (
7.9
).

Proof.

When
\delta=\infty
,
Tzvetkov and Visciglia proved
(
7.20
)
in

[
113
, Lemma 11.1]

(see also

[
114
, Proposition 3.1, in particular (3.10)]
)
for even
k\geq 6
,

[
115
, Subsection 6.1]
for
k=4
,
and

[
115
, Proposition 4.1]
for
k=2
,
and also proved
(
7.21
)
in
[
115
, Lemma 7.2]

for any odd
k\geq 3
.
In view of (
1.26
), (
5.1
), and
\#\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}=0
,
the bounds in
[
113
,
114
,
115
]

in proving (
7.20
) and (
7.21
) when
\delta=\infty

also hold for
0<\delta<\infty

with uniform constants for
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
,
thus yielding (
7.20
) and (
7.21
)
with a rate of convergence uniform in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.
∎

The next lemma treats the remaining terms.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
k\geq 2
be an integer and
0<\delta\leq\infty
.
Then, given any finite
p\geq 1
,
we have

(7.22)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\bigg\|\int_{\mathbb{T}}p_{N}^{*}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)dx\bigg\|_{L^{p}(d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})}=0

for any monomial
p(u)
appearing in

•

the second term on the right-hand side of (
3.63
)
when
k=2m
is even,

•

the second, third, and fourth terms on the right-hand side of (
3.64
)
when
k=2m+1
is odd.

Proof.

When
\delta=\infty
,
Lemma
7.6
was established
in

[
113
, Lemmas 11.2, 11.3, and 11.4]

(see also

[
114
, Proposition 3.1, in particular (3.11)]
)
for even
k\geq 6
,

[
115
, Subsection 6.1, in particular Second case and Third case]
for
k=4
,

[
115
, Proposition 4.1]
for
k=2
,
and

[
115
, Lemmas 7.3, 7.4, and 7.5]

for odd
k\geq 3
.
We point out that, when
k\geq 3
,
(
7.22
) for
0<\delta<\infty
follows from
the bounds in
[
113
,
114
,
115
]
for the
\delta=\infty
case
with (
1.26
) with (
5.1
) and
(
2.10
), giving a rate of convergence uniform in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.

It remains to consider the case
k=2
.
In this case, we have
p(u)=u^{4}
or
p(u)=u^{2}\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}u
(see Lemma
3.9
).
When
p(u)=u^{4}
, (
7.22
)
follows from

[
115
, Proposition 4.1]

with (
5.1
),
giving a rate of convergence uniform in
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.

We consider the remaining case:

p(u)=u^{2}\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}u
.
Let
u=X^{\delta}_{1}
.
Then, with (
7.13
),
we have

(7.23)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{T}}p^{*}_{N}(u_{N})dx&=-2\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{N}^{2}\mathbf{P}_{>N}\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}(u_{N}\partial_{x}u_{N})dx\\
&\quad-4\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{N}\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}u_{N}\mathbf{P}_{>N}(u_{N}\partial_{x}u_{N})dx\\
&=-\frac{2}{(2\pi)^{\frac{3}{2}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1\cdots 4}=0\\
0<|n_{j}|\leq N\\
|n_{34}|>N\end{subarray}}\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{4}g_{n_{j}}(\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{\delta}(n_{34})+2\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{\delta}(n_{2}))(in_{4})}{\prod_{j=1}^{4}(T_{\delta,1}(n_{j}))^{\frac{1}{2}}}\\
&=:\hskip 1.42262pt\text{I}\hskip 1.42262pt+\text{I \hskip-7.96677pt I},\end{split}

where
I

denotes the contribution from frequencies with no pair
and
I I

denotes the contribution from frequencies with two pairs
in the sense of Definition
5.13
,
Then, by
the Wiener chaos estimate (Lemma
2.8
),
Jensen’s inequality as in (
5.52
)-(
5.53
), (
5.1
), (
2.10
),
and Lemma
2.7
(recall that
k=2
),
we have

(7.24)

\displaystyle\|\hskip 1.42262pt\text{I}\hskip 1.42262pt\|_{L^{p}(d\mu^{\delta}_{1})}^{2}\lesssim_{\delta,p}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1\cdots 4}=0\\
0<|n_{j}|\leq N\\
|n_{12}|>N\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|n_{1}|^{2}|n_{2}|^{2}|n_{3}|^{2}}\lesssim N^{-1}\longrightarrow 0,

as
N\to\infty
,
where the implicit constant is independent of
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
.

Next, we consider the case when
there are two pairs in (
7.23
)
(note that
n_{12}\neq 0
due to
\mathbf{P}_{>N}
).
Without loss of generality, we suppose that
n_{13}=n_{24}=0
.
Then, by applying Minkowski’s inequality with (
5.1
) and (
2.10
),
splitting the sum into two sums,
where
|n_{1}|\gtrsim N
and
|n_{2}|\gtrsim N
, respectively,
and using
N-|n_{1}|\leq|n_{2}|\leq N
in the second sum, we have

(7.25)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\text{I \hskip-7.96677pt I}\|_{L^{p}(d\mu^{\delta}_{1})}&\lesssim_{\delta,p}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<|n_{1}|,|n_{2}|\leq N\\
|n_{12}|>N\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|n_{1}|^{2}|n_{2}|}\\
&\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<|n_{1}|,|n_{2}|\leq N\\
|n_{1}|\gtrsim N\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|n_{1}|^{2}|n_{2}|}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<|n_{1}|\leq N\\
N-|n_{1}|\leq|n_{2}|\leq N\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|n_{1}|^{2}N}\\
&\lesssim\frac{\log N}{N}\longrightarrow 0,\end{split}

as
N\to\infty
,
where the implicit constant is independent of
2\leq\delta\leq\infty
;
see also
[
113
, Lemma 10.1]
.

Hence, (
7.22
) in this case follows from (
7.23
),
(
7.24
), and (
7.25
).
∎

Remark 7.7
.

In
[
113
,
114
,
115
]
,
Tzvetkov and Visciglia proved
Lemma
7.5
for
\delta=\infty
,
from which the general case
0<\delta<\infty
follows as we explained.
We point out that in treating these
leading order contributions,
it is crucial to exploit certain cancellations, using the identity (
7.12
)
and the anti self-adjointness of
\mathcal{H}
.
In the following, we provide an example.
See
[
113
,
114
,
115
]
for further details.

Let
k=2m
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
and consider
p_{0}(v)
in (
7.18
).
We first consider the case
m=1
.
Namely,
p_{0}(u)=u\mathcal{H}u\partial_{x}u
.
Then, from (
7.9
) with (
7.13
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}p_{0,N}^{*}(u_{N})dx

\displaystyle=-2\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathbf{P}_{>N}(u_{N}\partial_{x}u_{N})(\mathcal{H}u_{N})(\partial_{x}u_{N})dx

\displaystyle\quad-2\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{N}\big(\mathcal{H}\mathbf{P}_{>N}(u_{N}\partial_{x}u_{N})\big)(\partial_{x}u_{N})dx

\displaystyle\quad-2\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{N}(\mathcal{H}u_{N})\partial_{x}\mathbf{P}_{>N}(u_{N}\partial_{x}u_{N})dx.

There are terms with two factors having
m=1
derivatives
(even after integration by parts), which
can not be handled directly, even with a probabilistic argument.
In this case, by integration by parts (for the third term), (
7.12
), and the anti self-adjointness of
\mathcal{H}
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}p_{0,N}^{*}(u_{N})dx=0.

Next, we consider the case
m\geq 2
.
From (
7.18
) and (
7.9
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}p_{0,N}^{*}(u)dx

\displaystyle=-2\int_{\mathbb{T}}\big(\mathbf{P}_{>N}(u\partial_{x}u)\big)(\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}^{m-1}u)(\partial_{x}^{m}u)dx

\displaystyle\quad-2\int_{\mathbb{T}}u\big(\mathbf{P}_{>N}\partial_{x}^{m-1}\mathcal{H}(u\partial_{x}u)\big)(\partial_{x}^{m}u)dx

\displaystyle\quad-2\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}^{m-1}u)\mathbf{P}_{>N}\partial_{x}^{m}(u\partial_{x}u)dx

\displaystyle=:\text{I}_{N}(u)+\text{I \hskip-7.96677pt I}_{N}(u)+\text{I \hskip-7.96677pt I \hskip-7.96677pt I}_{N}(u).

The first term
\text{I}_{N}(u)

can be handled as in (
7.24
) and (
7.25
).
On the other hand,
as for
\text{I \hskip-7.96677pt I}_{N}(u)
and
\text{I \hskip-7.96677pt I \hskip-7.96677pt I}_{N}(u)
,
there are terms with two factors having
m
derivatives
(even after integration by parts), which
can cause a potential issue.
By the Leibniz rule and the anti self-adjointness of
\mathcal{H}
,
we have

(7.26)

\displaystyle\text{I \hskip-7.96677pt I}_{N}(u)=\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}c_{m,j}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u\partial_{x}^{m}u\mathbf{P}_{>N}\mathcal{H}(\partial_{x}^{j}u\partial_{x}^{m-j}u)dx.

Namely, the contribution from the worst term
u\partial_{x}^{m}u\mathbf{P}_{>N}\mathcal{H}(u\partial_{x}^{m}u)
vanishes.
Similarly,
by integration by parts, the Leibniz rule, (
7.12
), and the anti self-adjointness of
\mathcal{H}
,
we have

(7.27)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{I \hskip-7.96677pt I \hskip-7.96677pt I}_{N}(u_{N})&=2\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{x}u_{N}\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}^{m-1}u_{N}\mathbf{P}_{>N}\partial_{x}^{m-1}(u_{N}\partial_{x}u_{N})dx\\
&\quad+2\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{N}\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}^{m}u_{N}\mathbf{P}_{>N}\partial_{x}^{m-1}(u_{N}\partial_{x}u_{N})dx\\
&=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}c_{m,j}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{x}u_{N}\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}^{m-1}u_{N}\mathbf{P}_{>N}(\partial_{x}^{j}u_{N}\partial_{x}^{m-j}u_{N})dx\\
&\quad+\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}c_{m,j}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{N}\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}^{m}u_{N}\mathbf{P}_{>N}(\partial_{x}^{j}u_{N}\partial_{x}^{m-j}u_{N})dx.\end{split}

Namely, the worst contribution from
j=0
in the second sum vanishes.
Once
we reduce

\text{I \hskip-7.96677pt I}_{N}(u)
and
\text{I \hskip-7.96677pt I \hskip-7.96677pt I}_{N}(u)
to the forms
on the right-hand sides of (
7.26
) and (
7.27
),
we can proceed as in
as in (
7.24
) and (
7.25
).
We omit details.

7.3.
Shallow-water regime

Proposition
7.3
follows
from a slight modification of the proof of
Proposition
7.1
.
We sketch the argument in the following.

Let
v
be a solution to the truncated scaled ILW (
7.6
).
Then, the low-frequency part
\mathbf{P}_{N}v
satisfies (
7.7
),
which can be rewritten as

\partial_{t}\mathbf{P}_{N}v-\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{2}\mathbf{P}_{N}v=2(\mathbf{P}_{N}v)\partial_{x}(\mathbf{P}_{N}v)-2\mathbf{P}_{>N}\big((\mathbf{P}_{N}v)\partial_{x}(\mathbf{P}_{N}v)\big).

Then, from the conservation of
\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(v)

under the (untruncated) scaled ILW (
1.5
)
(Theorem
1.4
),
we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\widetilde{E}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}v)=\widetilde{R}^{\delta,*}_{\frac{k}{2},N}(\mathbf{P}_{N}v),

where
\widetilde{R}^{\delta,*}_{\frac{k}{2},N}(v)
is
defined by replacing each monomial
p(v)
in

\widetilde{R}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
, appearing in (
4.92
),
by
p_{N}^{*}(v)
defined in (
7.9
).
Therefore, Proposition
7.3

follows once we prove

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\Big\|\widetilde{R}^{\delta,*}_{\frac{k}{2},N}(\mathbf{P}_{N}v)\Big\|_{L^{p}(d\widetilde{\mu}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})}=0.

Let us first consider the contribution from
the lower order terms.
Here, the lower order terms are given as follows:

(i)

when
k=2\kappa-1
is odd,
a monomial
p(v)
whose fundamental form
\widetilde{p}(v)
is of the form
(b) in (
6.32
), satisfying (
6.33
), and

(ii)

when
k=2\kappa
is even,
a monomial
p(v)
whose fundamental form
\widetilde{p}(v)
is of the form
(b) in (
6.36
), satisfying (
6.37
).

In this case, there is no need to exploit
cancellation.
In particular,
as mentioned in
the proof of Lemma
6.7
,
we drop any occurrence of
\delta\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}

thanks to
the
L^{r}
-boundedness of
\delta\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
for
1<r<\infty

(see (
2.14
) in Lemma
2.4
);
see also the discussion after Lemma
6.9
.
More concretely,
by noting that
the condition (
6.33
)
with
m=\kappa-1
when
k=2\kappa-1
is odd,
and
the condition (
6.37
)
with
m=\kappa

when
k=2\kappa
is even
both satisfy
the hypothesis of Lemma
7.6
,
we see that
a slight modification of the proof of Lemma
7.6

with Lemma
2.4

(to bound
\delta\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
)
yields

(7.28)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\bigg\|\int_{\mathbb{T}}p_{N}^{*}(\mathbf{P}_{N}v)dx\bigg\|_{L^{p}(d\widetilde{\mu}^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})}=0

in this case,
where

p_{N}^{*}(v)
is as in (
7.9
).
This part of analysis extends to
the
\delta=0
case
in view of (
4.80
) and Remark
6.10
.

Hence, it remains to consider the leading order terms:

(iii)

when
k=2\kappa-1
is odd,
a monomial
p(v)
whose fundamental form
\widetilde{p}(v)
is of the form
(a)
\widetilde{p}(v)=v\partial_{x}^{\kappa-1}v\partial_{x}^{\kappa-1}v
in (
6.32
)
with the understanding that, when

\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(p)=\#\delta(p)+1

(namely, there is a factor of
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
unpaired with
\delta
),
one of the
\partial_{x}
-factors is replaced by
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
.
See the proof of Lemma
6.7

for a further discussion.

(iv)

when
k=2\kappa
is even,
a monomial
p(v)
whose fundamental form
\widetilde{p}(v)
is of the form
(a)
\widetilde{p}(v)=v\partial_{x}^{\kappa-1}v\partial_{x}^{\kappa}v
in (
6.36
).

For these leading order terms, we need to exploit cancellations
as explained in Remark
7.7
.
However, the identity (
7.12
)
does not hold for
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
and
\delta\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
and thus we need to proceed with care.

\bullet

Case 1:

\delta>0
.

Fix a monomial
p(v)
as in (iii) or (iv) above.
Suppose that
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(p)\leq\#\delta(p)
.
In this case, we can pair
every
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
in
p(v)
with
\delta
.
Recall from
(
4.5
) with (
4.4
)
that (before integration by parts
to reduce the monomial to the form
appearing in (
4.92
))
every
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
must come with
\partial_{x}
.
Given
p(v)
in (
4.92
),
let
p_{0}(v)
be its original form before integration by parts.
By substituting

(7.29)

\displaystyle\delta\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\partial_{x}=\mathcal{G}_{\delta}\partial_{x}=\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}+\mathcal{Q}_{\delta},

which follows from (
1.5
) and (
1.14
),
into
every occurrence
\delta\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\partial_{x}
in
p_{0}(v)

and expanding via the multilinearity,
we end up with new monomials,
involving
\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}
and

\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
.
Except for one monomial (call it
p_{1}(v)
),
all the monomials involve
at least one factor of

\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}
.
In view of the
L^{r}
-boundedness of
\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}

(Lemma
2.4
), in particular, without any derivative loss,
these monomials are now of lower order, belonging
to the classes (i) or (ii) described above,
and thus can be handled as in
(the proof of) Lemma
7.6

(but with constants diverging as
\delta\to 0
).
On the other hand,

p_{1}(v)
involves only
\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}

and thus must be of the form described in Lemma
3.10
,
which then can be treated by Lemma
7.5
.
As a conclusion, we obtain (
7.28
).

Next, we consider the case

\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}(p)=\#\delta(p)+1
which happens only in (iii).
In this case, the semi-fundamental form
\overline{p}(v)
of
p(v)

(see Definition
4.9
)
is of the form:

\overline{p}(v)=v(\partial_{x}^{\kappa-1}v)(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{\kappa-2}v)
.
Then, by artificially inserting
\delta^{-1}\cdot\delta
and using (
7.29
),
we have

(7.30)

\displaystyle p(v)=\delta^{-1}p_{2}(v)+\delta^{-1}p_{3}(v)

such that the semi-fundamental forms of
p_{2}(v)
and
p_{3}(v)

are given by
we have

(7.31)

\displaystyle\overline{p}_{2}(v)=v\partial_{x}^{\kappa-1}v\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}^{\kappa-1}v\qquad\text{and}\qquad\overline{p}_{3}(v)=v\partial_{x}^{\kappa-1}v\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{\kappa-2}v

We see that
p_{3}(v)
is a monomial
of lower order
belonging to the class (i) above,
which can be treated as in
(the proof of) Lemma
7.6
.
As for
p_{2}(v)
,
we substitute (
7.29
)
in every occurrence of
\delta\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}
(if any)
and treat those monomials involving
\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}

as in
(the proof of) Lemma
7.6
, since they are of lower order.
Lastly,
by arguing as in the proofs of Lemmas
3.9
and
3.10
,
the remaining monomial, only involving
\mathcal{H}
, must be of the form

p_{\vec{\alpha}}(v)
in (
7.19
) (with
m=\kappa-1
)
which is already treated in Lemma
7.5
.

This concludes the proof of Proposition
7.3

for each
fixed

0<\delta<\infty
.
In view of the use of Lemma
2.4
on
\mathcal{Q}_{\delta}

and (
7.30
) which artificially introduced a factor of
\delta^{-1}
,
the rate of convergence in (
7.8
)
diverges as
\delta\to 0
, and thus the argument presented above
does
not
extend to the
\delta=0
case.

\bullet

Case 2:

\delta=0
and
k
is even.

We only need to consider monomials in the class (iv).
In this case, we have
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\delta}=\#\delta=0
.
Thus, we have

\widetilde{p}(v)=v\partial_{x}^{\kappa-1}v\partial_{x}^{\kappa}v
,
which is treated in Lemma
7.5
,
yielding (
7.31
).
This proves Proposition
7.3

when
\delta=0
.

8.
Dynamical problem

In this section,
by studying the associated dynamical problem,
we prove invariance of the generalized Gibbs measures
and deep-water (and shallow-water) convergence of the invariant dynamics
(Theorems
1.9
and
1.10
).

We first recall the following well-posedness
and convergence results for the (scaled) ILW (
1.1
) and (
1.5
);
see
[
74
, Theorem 1.9 and Corollary 1.10]

and
[
63
, Theorems 1.3 and 1.4 and Corollary 1.5]
.
See also
[
11
,
69
,
70
,
71
,
19
]
.
While the well-posedness result in
[
74
]

is stated for the full dynamics,
it also applies
to the truncated (scaled) ILW equations (
1.43
) and (
7.6
)
in view of the uniform (in
N
) boundedness of
the frequency projector
\mathbf{P}_{N}
.

Lemma 8.1

(deep-water regime)
.

(i)

Let
0<\delta\leq\infty
.
Then, the ILW equation (
1.1
)

(
and the BO equation (
1.3
) when
\delta=\infty
)
is globally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for
{s\geq\frac{1}{2}}
.
In particular,
given
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
there exists
T=T\big(\delta,\|u_{0}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\big)>0

such that there exists a unique solution
u(t)=\Phi(t)(u_{0})=\Phi(t;\delta)(u_{0})
to (
1.1
)
with
u|_{t=0}=u_{0}

in the class
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
, satisfying the bound
:

\|\Phi(t)(u_{0})\|_{L^{\infty}([-T,T];H^{s})},\,\|\Phi_{N}(t)(u_{0})\|_{L^{\infty}([-T,T];H^{s})}\lesssim_{\delta}\|u_{0}\|_{H^{s}},

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
where

\Phi_{N}(t)=\Phi_{N}(t;\delta)
denotes the solution map for the truncated ILW (
1.43
).

(ii)

Let
s>\frac{1}{2}
.
Suppose that

u_{0}^{\delta}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
2\leq\delta<\infty
,
converges to
some
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
as
\delta\to\infty
.
Then, as
\delta\to\infty
,
the corresponding global-in-time solution
u^{\delta}
to ILW (
1.1
)

(
with the depth parameter
\delta
)

with
u^{\delta}|_{t=0}=u^{\delta}_{0}

converges
to the solution
u
to BO (
1.3
) with
u|_{t=0}=u_{0}

in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
,
endowed with the compact-open topology in time.

Lemma 8.2

(shallow-water regime)
.

(i)

Let
0\leq\delta\leq 1
.
Then, the scaled ILW equation (
1.5
)

(
and the KdV equation (
1.6
) when
\delta=0
)
is globally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for
{s\geq\frac{1}{2}}
.
In particular,
given
v_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
there exists
T=T\big(\delta,\|v_{0}\|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}}\big)>0

such that there exists a unique solution
v(t)=\widetilde{\Phi}(t)(v_{0})=\widetilde{\Phi}(t;\delta)(v_{0})
to (
1.5
)
with
v|_{t=0}=v_{0}

in the class
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
, satisfying the bound
:

\|\widetilde{\Phi}(t)(v_{0})\|_{L^{\infty}([-T,T];H^{s})},\,\|\widetilde{\Phi}_{N}(t)(v_{0})\|_{L^{\infty}([-T,T];H^{s})}\lesssim_{\delta}\|v_{0}\|_{H^{s}},

uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
where

\widetilde{\Phi}_{N}(t)=\widetilde{\Phi}_{N}(t;\delta)
denotes the solution map for
the truncated scaled ILW (
7.6
)

(ii)

Let
s>\frac{1}{2}
.
Suppose that

v_{0}^{\delta}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
,
0<\delta\leq 1
,
converges to
some
v_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
as
\delta\to 0
.
Then, as
\delta\to 0
,
the corresponding global-in-time solution
v^{\delta}
to
the scaled ILW (
1.5
)
(
with the depth parameter
\delta
)

with
v^{\delta}|_{t=0}=v^{\delta}_{0}

converges
to the solution
v
to KdV (
1.6
) with
v|_{t=0}=v_{0}

in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
,
endowed with the compact-open topology in time.

Remark 8.3
.

(i)
Lemmas
8.1
and
8.2

are based on an energy method without using a gauge transform.
In
[
19
]
, we extended Lemma
8.1

to the range
s\geq 0
. However, the argument in
[
19
]

uses the gauge transform for BO (
1.3
)
and thus is not suitable for our purpose.
We point out that the solutions in Lemmas
8.1
and
8.2

are unconditionally unique
in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
.

(ii) In view of the
H^{\frac{1}{2}}
-well-posedness
stated in Lemmas
8.1
(i) and
8.2
(i),
it is likely that the convergence claims in
Lemmas
8.1
(ii) and
8.2
(ii)
extend to the endpoint
s=\frac{1}{2}
.
We, however, do not pursue this issue,
and simply use the convergence results from
[
63
]
.

In the remaining part of this paper, we restrict our attention to the deep-water regime,
since the same argument applies to the shallow-water regime with a straightforward modification.

8.1.
Invariance of the generalized Gibbs measures

In this subsection,
we prove invariance of the deep-water generalized Gibbs measures (Theorem
1.9
(i)).
With the tools developed in the previous sections,
Theorem
1.9
(i)
follows from the lines in

[
114
,
115
]
,
and thus
we will be very brief here.
Let us fix notations.

•

Fix an integer
k\geq 3
,

0<\delta\leq\infty
, and
K>0
in the following.
Set
\frac{1}{2}<s<\sigma<\frac{k-1}{2}
.

•

F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)
denotes the density of the generalized Gibbs measure
\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
as in (
1.29
).
Recall that they depend on an
L^{2}
-cutoff size
K>0
but we suppress the
K
-dependence
from our notation.

•

Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

\Phi_{N}(t)=\Phi_{N}(t;\delta)
denotes the solution map for the truncated ILW (
1.43
).
We use
\Phi(t)
to denote
the solution map for ILW (
1.1
).

•

Given
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
R>0
,
we use
B^{s}(R)
to denote the ball in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
of radius
R>0

centered at the origin.

As in
[
114
,
115
]
,
the main goal is to prove the following
almost invariance of the
generalized Gibbs measure, which can be regarded as a probabilistic counterpart
of the method of almost conservation laws
[
23
]
.

Proposition 8.4
.

Given any
T>0
, we have

(8.1)

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in[0,T]\\
A\in{\mathcal{B}}(H^{\sigma})\end{subarray}}\bigg|\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Phi_{N}(t)(A)}F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)\bigg|=0,

where

{\mathcal{B}}(H^{\sigma})
denotes the Borel sets in
H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T})
.

See

[
114
, Proposition 5.4]
,
where such a result was first introduced.
Once we have Proposition
8.4
,
the rest of the argument follows the lines in
[
114
,
115
]

by using Lemma
8.5
below
(see
[
114
, pp. 4710-4713]
),
yielding invariance of the generalized Gibbs measure
\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
.
(Theorem
1.9
(i)). We omit details.

As the end of this subsection,
we present a proof of Proposition
8.4
,
following the strategy developed in
[
114
,
115
]
,
where we show how our simplified construction of the generalized Gibbs measures
simplifies the proof of Proposition
8.4
;
see
Remark
8.7
.

We state several preliminary lemmas.
The first lemma is on the approximation
property of the truncated ILW dynamics (
1.43
);
see
[
114
, Proposition 4.1]
.
This lemma is not needed for proving Proposition
8.4
,
but is needed for proving an analogue of
[
114
, Lemma 5.6]
.

Lemma 8.5
.

Given
R>0
, there exists

T=T(R)>0
such that
for every
\varepsilon_{0}>0
there exists
N_{0}=N_{0}(\varepsilon_{0})\in\mathbb{N}

such that

\displaystyle\Phi_{N}(t)(A)

\displaystyle\subset\Phi(t)(A)+B^{s}(\varepsilon_{0})

for any
N\geq N_{0}
,
t\in[-T,T]
,
and
A\in B^{\sigma}(R)
.

Lemma
8.5

follows from a slight modification of the proof of

[
74
, Proposition 3.5]

on the difference estimate.
The only additional term
is
\mathbf{P}_{>N}\partial_{x}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)^{2}

which is in fact
O(N^{s-\sigma})

for

\sigma>s>\frac{1}{2}

thanks to the
projection
\mathbf{P}_{>N}
onto high frequencies
and the uniform (in
N
)
bound on the (higher)
H^{\sigma}
-norm of
a solution to the truncated ILW (
1.43
),
coming from Lemma
8.1
(i).

Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
we write
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}=\mu^{\delta,\leq N}_{\frac{k}{2}}\otimes\mu^{\delta,>N}_{\frac{k}{2}}
,
where

\mu^{\delta,\leq N}_{\frac{k}{2}}

and

\mu^{\delta,>N}_{\frac{k}{2}}

are defined as the pushforward measures
of
\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
under the maps

\mathbf{P}_{N}
and
\mathbf{P}_{<N}
, respectively.
In particular, we have

d\mu^{\delta,>N}_{\frac{k}{2}}=Z^{-1}_{\delta,\frac{k}{2},>N}\exp\big(-E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},>N}(u)\big)d\mathbf{P}_{>N}u
,
where

E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},>N}(u)
is as in
(
7.4
).
Let

dL_{N}=\prod_{0<n\leq N}d\widehat{u}(n)

denotes the Lebesgue measure on
E_{N}=\text{span}\big\{\cos(nx),\sin(nx)\big\}_{0<n\leq N}\cong\mathbb{C}^{N}
.
Then, we can write the truncated generalized Gibbs measure
\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}
in (
1.30
)
as

(8.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2},N}(du)&=Z^{-1}_{\delta,\frac{k}{2},N}\,F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)\\
&=\widehat{Z}_{\delta,\frac{k}{2},N}^{-1}\eta_{K}\big(\|\mathbf{P}_{N}u\|_{L^{2}}\big)\exp\Big(-E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)\Big)dL_{N}\otimes d\mu^{\delta,>N}_{\frac{k}{2}}.\end{split}

Next, we state a
a change-of-variables formula;

[
114
, Proposition 5.1]
.
In recent years, such a change-of-variables formula
has also played an important role
in the study of
quasi-invariant Gaussian measures for Hamiltonian PDEs;
see, for example,
[
111
,
92
,
93
,
42
]
.

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\Phi_{N}(t)(A))

\displaystyle=Z_{\delta,\frac{k}{2},N}^{-1}\int_{\Phi_{N}(t)(A)}F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)

\displaystyle=\widehat{Z}_{\delta,\frac{k}{2},N}^{-1}\int_{A}\eta_{K}\big(\|\mathbf{P}_{N}\Phi_{N}(t)(u)\|_{L^{2}}\big)

\displaystyle\hphantom{XXXXL}\times\exp\Big(-E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}(\Phi_{N}(t)(u))\Big)dL_{N}\otimes d\mu^{\delta,>N}_{\frac{k}{2}}

for any
A\in\mathcal{B}(H^{\sigma})
,
where

Z_{\delta,\frac{k}{2},N}
and
\widehat{Z}_{\delta,\frac{k}{2},N}

are as in (
8.2
).

We conclude this subsection
by presenting a proof of
Proposition
8.4
.

Proof of Proposition
8.4
.

We proceed as in the proof of
[
114
, Proposition 5.4]
.
We first show that

(8.3)

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{A\in{\mathcal{B}}(H^{\sigma})}\bigg|\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Phi_{N}(t)(A)}F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)\Big|_{t=0}\bigg|=0.

From the change-of-variable formula
(Lemma
8.6
) and the
L^{2}
-conservation under the truncated dynamics (
7.3
),
we have

(8.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Phi_{N}(t)(A)}F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)\Big|_{t=0}\\
&\quad=-\int_{A}\frac{d}{dt}E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\Phi_{N}(t)(u))\Big|_{t=0}F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u).\end{split}

Then, by Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality
and Proposition
5.3
, we have

(8.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sup_{A\in{\mathcal{B}}(H^{\sigma})}\bigg|\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Phi_{N}(t)(A)}F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)\Big|_{t=0}\bigg|\\
&\quad\leq\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\|F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)\|_{L^{2}(d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})}\cdot\bigg\|\frac{d}{dt}E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\Phi_{N}(t)(u))\Big|_{t=0}\bigg\|_{L^{2}(d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})}\\
&\quad\lesssim\bigg\|\frac{d}{dt}E^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}\Phi_{N}(t)(u))\Big|_{t=0}\bigg\|_{L^{2}(d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}})}.\end{split}

Then, (
8.3
) follows from
Proposition
7.1
.

Let
0<\tau\leq T
.
Since we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Phi_{N}(t)(A)}F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)\Big|_{t=\tau}=\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Phi_{N}(t)(\Phi_{N}(\tau)(A))}F^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)d\mu^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}(u)\Big|_{t=0},

we can repeat the computation in (
8.4
) and (
8.5
),
where we replace
A
by
\Phi_{N}(\tau)(A)
.
In particular,
(
8.5
) holds uniformly in
0\leq\tau\leq T
,
allowing us to insert a supremum over
0\leq\tau\leq T

in (
8.5
).
This proves (
8.1
).
∎

Remark 8.7
.

Thanks to our simplified construction of the generalized Gibbs measures
only with an
L^{2}
-cutoff,
the step (
8.4
) is simpler, since it does not involve
the time derivatives
of lower order conservations laws.
Compare (
8.4
) with
the bottom math display on
[
114
, p. 4709]
.

8.2.
Dynamical convergence

We conclude this paper by presenting a proof of Theorem
1.9
(ii)
on the deep-water convergence of the invariant ILW dynamics
constructed in the previous subsection.
Theorem
1.10
(iii) and (iv)
on shallow-water convergence follow from a similar discussion
which we omit.

Fix
k\geq 3
.
From Theorem
1.6
(ii),
we know that, as
\delta\to\infty
,
\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
converges to

\rho^{\infty}_{\frac{k}{2}}
in total variation and hence weakly.
Then,
by the
Skorokhod representation theorem
along a continuous parameter (Lemma
2.10
),
there exist random functions

\{u^{\delta}_{0}\}_{2\leq\delta\leq\infty}\subset H^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})

such that
\Law(u^{\delta}_{0})=\rho^{\delta}_{\frac{k}{2}}
for each
2\leq\delta\leq\infty

and
u^{\delta}_{0}
converges almost surely to
u^{\infty}_{0}
in
H^{\frac{k-1}{2}-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{T})

as
\delta\to\infty
.

Let
u^{\delta}
be the unique global-in-time solution to ILW (
1.1
)
(with the depth parameter
\delta
; BO (
1.3
) when
\delta=\infty
) with
u^{\delta}|_{t=0}=u^{\delta}_{0}
.
Then, it follows from
Lemma
8.1
(ii)
that
u^{\delta}
converges almost surely to
u^{\infty}

in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))
,
endowed with the compact-open topology in time.
This proves Theorem
1.9
(ii).
